「万古磐石」（Rock of Ages）是一家低级酒馆门楣上的字号，它堵住了另一条巷子的入口——出了市议员的院子便见到——那条巷子若说同别条有什么不同，便是更加荒凉、更加凄惨。这名字听着像一句嘲讽，出自那幸好已经过去的年代：那时「纽约最邪恶的人」就住在拐角过去不远的地方，还为自己这头衔扬扬自得。在樱桃街上走不了几步，就会撞见某一桩罪行业绩——或属往昔、或属当今——的遗存；而且难得有一处不能翻出未来窃贼的标本砖来。樱桃街的恶棍无孔不入。去问问警察总监默里（Superintendent Murray）吧：他做橡树街警队队长时，七个月里办成的盗窃、抢劫和杀人案的定罪，累计不下五百三十年苦役。他会告诉你他的看法：哪怕只算最近这二十年，第四选区造就的罪犯，也比全城其余地方加在一起还多。
===Original===
But though the “Swamp Angels” have gone to their reward, their successors carry on business at the old stand as successfully, if not as boldly. There goes one who was once a shining light in thiefdom. He has reformed since, they say. The policeman on the corner, who is addicted to a professional unbelief in reform of any kind, will tell you that while on the Island once he sailed away on a shutter, paddling along until he was picked up in Hell Gate by a schooner’s crew, whom he persuaded that he was a fanatic performing some sort of religious penance by his singular expedition. Over yonder, Tweed, the arch-thief, worked in a brush-shop and earned an honest living before he took to politics. As we stroll from one narrow street to another the odd contrast between the low, old-looking houses in front and the towering tenements in the back yards grows even more striking, perhaps because we expect and are looking for it. Nobody who was not would suspect the presence of the rear houses, though they have been there long enough. Here is one seven stories high behind one with only three floors. Take a look into this Roosevelt Street alley; just about one step wide, with a five-story house on one side that gets its light and air﻿—God help us for pitiful mockery!﻿—from this slit between brick walls. There are no windows in the wall on the other side; it is perfectly blank. The fire-escapes of the long tenement fairly touch it; but the rays of the sun, rising, setting, or at high noon, never do. It never shone into the alley from the day the devil planned and man built it. There was once an English doctor who experimented with the sunlight in the soldiers’ barracks, and found that on the side that was shut off altogether from the sun the mortality was one hundred percent greater than on the light side, where its rays had free access. But then soldiers are of some account, have a fixed value, if not a very high one. The people who live here have not. The horse that pulls the dirt-cart one of these laborers loads and unloads is of ever so much more account to the employer of his labor than he and all that belongs to him. Ask the owner; he will not attempt to deny it, if the horse is worth anything. The man too knows it. It is the one thought that occasionally troubles the owner of the horse in the enjoyment of his prosperity, built of and upon the successful assertion of the truth that all men are created equal.

===Chinese===
不过，「沼泽天使」们虽然已经各得报应，他们的后继者却照旧在老铺面上把生意做得红红火火，只是不那么明目张胆罢了。那边走过去的一个，当年曾是贼道上一颗耀眼的明星。据说后来他改邪归正了。街角的警察——他对任何形式的悔改都抱有一种职业性的不信——会告诉你：此人有一回蹲在岛上（指布莱克韦尔岛上的监狱）时，靠一副窗板扬帆出逃，一路划水漂到地狱门（Hell Gate），被一艘帆船的船员搭救上来，他愣是让那些人相信，他是个借这场奇特的远航修行赎罪的教门狂徒。再那边，巨盗窃贼特威德（Tweed）在投身政治之前，也在刷子工场里做工，挣过一份清白的饭吃。我们从一条窄街逛到另一条窄街，前面低矮的老式房子与后院里高高耸起的廉租公寓之间那种古怪的对照，愈看愈触目——也许因为我们正期待着、寻访着它。若不是有心，谁也想不到这些后楼的存在，尽管它们立在那里已经年深日久。这里便有一栋七层的高楼，躲在一栋只有三层的小楼背后。看看这条罗斯福街（Roosevelt Street）的巷子吧：约莫一步宽，一侧一栋五层的房子，它的光和空气——上帝原谅这可怜的嘲弄吧！——就来自这道砖墙间的缝隙。另一侧的墙上没有一扇窗；整面墙光秃秃的。长长一栋廉租公寓的太平梯（fire-escape）几乎要贴上它；可太阳的光线，无论初升、西沉，还是正午高照，从来碰不上它。打从魔鬼设计、人类建造它的那一天起，太阳就从来没有照进过这条巷子。从前有位英国医生在士兵营房里做过阳光实验，发现完全晒不着太阳的那一侧，死亡率比阳光可以自由照进的向阳一侧高出百分之一百。不过，士兵们到底是有些分量的，有一个固定的价值——纵然不算太高。住在这里的人却没有。这些苦力装车卸车的那辆运灰车，拉车的马在雇主眼里的分量，远过于他本人和他的一切。不信去问马主人；只要那马还值几个钱，他是不会试图否认的。那人也自己知道。马的主人安享着他的富足——那富足建立在对「人人生而平等」这一真理的成功宣示之上、又是靠它营建起来的——偶尔叫他心绪不宁的，正是这一件事。

===Original===
With what a shock did the story of yonder Madison Street alley come home to New Yorkers one morning, eight or ten years ago, when a fire that broke out after the men had gone to their work swept up those narrow stairs and burned up women and children to the number of a full half score. There were fire-escapes, yes! but so placed that they could not be reached. The firemen had to look twice before they could find the opening that passes for a thoroughfare; a stout man would never venture in. Some wonderfully heroic rescues were made at that fire by people living in the adjoining tenements. Danger and trouble﻿—of the imminent kind, not the everyday sort that excites neither interest nor commiseration﻿—run even this common clay into heroic moulds on occasion; occasions that help us to remember that the gap that separates the man with the patched coat from his wealthy neighbor is, after all, perhaps but a tenement. Yet, what a gap! and of whose making? Here, as we stroll along Madison Street, workmen are busy putting the finishing touches to the brownstone front of a tall new tenement. This one will probably be called an apartment house. They are carving satyrs’ heads in the stone, with a crowd of gaping youngsters looking on in admiring wonder. Next door are two other tenements, likewise with brownstone fronts, fair to look at. The youngest of the children in the group is not too young to remember how their army of tenants was turned out by the health officers because the houses had been condemned as unfit for human beings to live in. The owner was a wealthy builder who “stood high in the community.” Is it only in our fancy that the sardonic leer on the stone faces seems to list that way? Or is it an introspective grin? We will not ask if the new house belongs to the same builder. He too may have reformed.

===Chinese===
八九年前的一个早晨，那边麦迪逊街（Madison Street）一条巷子的惨闻，给了纽约人怎样的一记重击：男人们上工之后，一场火灾骤起，顺着那些狭窄的楼梯扫上来，烧死了足足十来个妇女和孩子。太平梯是有的，没错！可装的位置根本够不着。消防队员得看上两眼，才找得到那个权充通道的口子；胖一点的人是绝不敢进去的。火灾中，邻楼的住户们做出了几桩惊人英勇的救援。危险与患难——那种迫在眉睫的，而不是那种既引不起关切也引不起怜悯的日常琐细——有时能把这样寻常的泥土也灌进英雄的模子；这样的时刻提醒我们：隔开穿补丁外衣的人和他阔邻人的那道鸿沟，说到底，也许不过是一座廉租公寓。然而，这是怎样的一道鸿沟！又是谁造出来的？此时我们沿着麦迪逊街走，工匠们正忙着给一座新高廉租公寓的褐砂石门面做收尾。这一座多半会被称作公寓楼。他们正在石头上雕刻萨堤尔（satyr，希腊神话中的羊人）的头像，一群张着嘴的小孩子满怀赞叹地围观。隔壁还有两栋廉租公寓，同样装着褐砂石门面，看上去相当体面。围观的孩子里最小的那个，也还没小到记不起来：那两栋楼里的房客大军是怎么被卫生官员们轰出去的——因为房子被判定为不适宜人类居住。房主是一位富有的营造商，「在社会上颇有地位」。石像脸上那副讥诮的鬼脸，似乎正朝那一边斜眼睨着——难道只是我们的错觉？抑或那是一抹自嘲的冷笑？新楼是否也出自同一位营造商之手，我们就不问了。他说不定也改邪归正了呢。
===Original===
We have crossed the boundary of the Seventh Ward. Penitentiary Row, suggestive name for a block of Cherry Street tenements, is behind us. Within recent days it has become peopled wholly with Hebrews, the overflow from Jewtown adjoining, pedlars and tailors, all of them. It is odd to read this legend from other days over the door: “No pedlars allowed in this house.” These thrifty people are not only crowding into the tenements of this once exclusive district﻿—they are buying them. The Jew runs to real estate as soon as he can save up enough for a deposit to clinch the bargain. As fast as the old houses are torn down, towering structures go up in their place, and Hebrews are found to be the builders. Here is a whole alley nicknamed after the intruder, Jews’ Alley. But abuse and ridicule are not weapons to fight the Israelite with. He pockets them quietly with the rent and bides his time. He knows from experience, both sweet and bitter, that all things come to those who wait, including the houses and lands of their persecutors.

===Chinese===
我们已经跨过了第七选区（Seventh Ward）的界线。「监狱排」（Penitentiary Row）——给樱桃街一排廉租公寓起这个名字，颇堪玩味——已被抛在身后。近来这一排已住得清一色全是希伯来人（Hebrew），全是从隔壁「犹太镇」（Jewtown）溢出来的人口，个个是货郎和裁缝。在门上读到往昔年月留下的这条告示，未免古怪：「本楼谢绝货郎入内。」这些勤俭的人们不但正涌进这个从前排外的街区的廉租公寓——他们在把它们买下来。犹太人一攒够一笔能敲定买卖的定金，就一头扎进房地产。老房子拆掉一栋，原地便竖起一座高楼，而人们发现，营造商正是希伯来人。这里就有一整条巷子，因这位闯入者而得了绰号：犹太巷（Jews' Alley）。可是，辱骂和讥笑都不是对付以色列人（Israelite）的武器。他不动声色地把它们连同租金一并收进腰包，静候自己的时机。他从经验——甜的和苦的都有一份——里深知：凡事终归会落到肯等待的人头上，连迫害他们的人的房屋地产也不例外。

===Original===
Here comes a pleasure party, as gay as any on the avenue, though the carryall is an ash-cart. The father is the driver and he has taken his brown-legged boy for a ride. How proud and happy they both look up there on their perch! The queer old building they have halted in front of is “The Ship,” famous for fifty years as a ramshackle tenement filled with the oddest crowd. No one knows why it is called “The Ship,” though there is a tradition that once the river came clear up here to Hamilton Street, and boats were moored alongside it. More likely it is because it is as bewildering inside as a crazy old ship, with its ups and downs of ladders parading as stairs, and its unexpected pitfalls. But Hamilton Street, like Water Street, is not what it was. The missions drove from the latter the worst of its dives. A sailors’ mission has lately made its appearance in Hamilton Street, but there are no dives there, nothing worse than the ubiquitous saloon and tough tenements.

===Chinese===
这边来了一支出游的队伍，欢快得同大道上的任何一乘游乐马车不相上下，虽说这辆「游览马车」其实是辆运灰车。父亲执缰驾车，带上了他那个晒得腿杆黄褐的儿子兜风。爷儿俩坐在高处，瞧着多神气、多快活！他们在那栋古怪老楼前停了下来——这栋楼便是「船屋」（The Ship），五十年来以一座摇摇欲坠的廉租公寓闻名，里面塞满了顶顶古怪的各色人等。没人知道它为什么叫「船屋」，虽说有个传说：从前河水一直漫到这里的汉密尔顿街（Hamilton Street），小船就拴在楼边。更可能是因为它里面同一条疯癫的老船一样叫人晕头转向：忽上忽下的梯子冒充楼梯，冷不防便是一个陷坑。不过汉密尔顿街同水街（Water Street）一样，已不复当年了。布道会把水街上最坏的几家下等酒馆赶走了。汉密尔顿街近来也出现了一家水手布道会，不过那里本没有下等酒馆，最糟的不过是那无处不在的酒馆和藏污纳垢的廉租公寓。

===Original===
Enough of them everywhere. Suppose we look into one? No. —﻿—﻿— Cherry Street. Be a little careful, please! The hall is dark and you might stumble over the children pitching pennies back there. Not that it would hurt them; kicks and cuffs are their daily diet. They have little else. Here where the hall turns and dives into utter darkness is a step, and another, another. A flight of stairs. You can feel your way, if you cannot see it. Close? Yes! What would you have? All the fresh air that ever enters these stairs comes from the hall-door that is forever slamming, and from the windows of dark bedrooms that in turn receive from the stairs their sole supply of the elements God meant to be free, but man deals out with such niggardly hand. That was a woman filling her pail by the hydrant you just bumped against. The sinks are in the hallway, that all the tenants may have access﻿—and all be poisoned alike by their summer stenches. Hear the pump squeak! It is the lullaby of tenement-house babes. In summer, when a thousand thirsty throats pant for a cooling drink in this block, it is worked in vain. But the saloon, whose open door you passed in the hall, is always there. The smell of it has followed you up. Here is a door. Listen! That short hacking cough, that tiny, helpless wail﻿—what do they mean? They mean that the soiled bow of white you saw on the door downstairs will have another story to tell﻿—Oh! a sadly familiar story﻿—before the day is at an end. The child is dying with measles. With half a chance it might have lived; but it had none. That dark bedroom killed it.

===Chinese===
这样的楼到处都够多的了。不妨进一栋看看？樱桃街 ——— 号。请当心一点！门厅里黑，你会被那几个在里头掷铜板的孩子绊倒的。绊倒了也伤不着他们；拳脚本来就是他们每天的口粮，他们别的也没有什么。这边，门厅一拐弯、扎进一团漆黑的地方，有一级台阶，又一级，又一级。一段楼梯。看不见，你总还摸得着。憋闷？可不！不然还能怎样？进得了这段楼梯的新鲜空气，全来自那扇摔个不停的大门，和几间黑卧室的窗户——而那些卧室能分到的空气，又全从楼梯上来：上帝本意白白赐予的元素，人却撒着手这样克扣着发放。你刚撞上的，是一位在水栓旁灌水的妇人。污水槽就设在门厅里，好让人人房客都够得着——也好让人人一律被它们夏天的恶臭毒一遍。听那水泵吱吱呀呀！那是廉租公寓婴孩的摇篮曲。夏天，这一整个街区一千条干渴的喉咙都喘着气想讨一口清凉的水时，这泵是怎么摇也摇不出水来的。可酒馆总在那里——你方才在门厅里经过了它敞开的门。它的气味一路跟着你上来了。这里有一扇门。听！那短促的干咳，那一声细弱无援的啼哭——是什么意思？它们的意思是：你在楼下门上看见的那个污渍斑斑的白蝴蝶结，今天不到天黑就会另有一段故事可讲——唉！一段悲惨得叫人熟悉的故事。孩子正死于麻疹。但凡有半分机会，他本可以活下来；可他一分也没有。那间黑卧室杀死了他。
===Original===
“It was took all of a suddint,” says the mother, smoothing the throbbing little body with trembling hands. There is no unkindness in the rough voice of the man in the jumper, who sits by the window grimly smoking a clay pipe, with the little life ebbing out in his sight, bitter as his words sound: “Hush, Mary! If we cannot keep the baby, need we complain﻿—such as we?”

===Chinese===
「他是一下子猛地病倒的。」母亲说，用颤抖的手抚着那微微悸动的小身子。窗边坐着个穿粗布工装的男人，闷头抽着陶土烟斗，眼睁睁看着那条小性命在自己眼前一点点耗尽；他的话虽听着辛涩，粗嘎的嗓音里并无狠戾：「别哭了，玛丽！咱们要是留不住这孩子，咱们这样的人——还有什么可抱怨的呢？」

===Original===
Such as we! What if the words ring in your ears as we grope our way up the stairs and down from floor to floor, listening to the sounds behind the closed doors﻿—some of quarrelling, some of coarse songs, more of profanity. They are true. When the summer heats come with their suffering they have meaning more terrible than words can tell. Come over here. Step carefully over this baby﻿—it is a baby, spite of its rags and dirt﻿—under these iron bridges called fire-escapes, but loaded down, despite the incessant watchfulness of the firemen, with broken household goods, with washtubs and barrels, over which no man could climb from a fire. This gap between dingy brick-walls is the yard. That strip of smoke-colored sky up there is the heaven of these people. Do you wonder the name does not attract them to the churches? That baby’s parents live in the rear tenement here. She is at least as clean as the steps we are now climbing. There are plenty of houses with half a hundred such in. The tenement is much like the one in front we just left, only fouler, closer, darker﻿—we will not say more cheerless. The word is a mockery. A hundred thousand people lived in rear tenements in New York last year. Here is a room neater than the rest. The woman, a stout matron with hard lines of care in her face, is at the washtub. “I try to keep the childer clean,” she says, apologetically, but with a hopeless glance around. The spice of hot soapsuds is added to the air already tainted with the smell of boiling cabbage, of rags and uncleanliness all about. It makes an overpowering compound. It is Thursday, but patched linen is hung upon the pulley-line from the window. There is no Monday cleaning in the tenements. It is washday all the week round, for a change of clothing is scarce among the poor. They are poverty’s honest badge, these perennial lines of rags hung out to dry, those that are not the washerwoman’s professional shingle. The true line to be drawn between pauperism and honest poverty is the clothesline. With it begins the effort to be clean that is the first and the best evidence of a desire to be honest.

===Chinese===
咱们这样的人！这话若还在你耳边回响，就请随我们摸索着上楼，再一层一层走下来，听听那些关着的门背后的声响吧——有的是吵骂，有的是粗鄙的小调，更多的是亵渎的咒骂。他们说的是实话。等到夏日酷暑挟着它的苦难一同到来，这些话便有了言语无从诉说的更可怕的含义。到这边来。小心跨过这个婴儿——尽管一身破烂、满身泥垢，他到底是个婴儿——从这些号称太平梯的铁桥底下走过；可是尽管消防队员盯得再紧，铁桥上还是堆满了破烂家什、洗衣盆和木桶，真着了火，谁也别想翻越它们逃生。这污砖墙之间的一道豁口就是院子。头顶那一条烟色的天，就是这些人的天堂。你还奇怪那名字为什么吸引不了他们进教堂吗？那孩子的父母就住在这里的后院廉租公寓（rear tenement）里。她至少同我们正在攀登的台阶一样干净。住着足足五十个这样孩子的房子，有的是。这座后楼同我们刚离开的前楼大同小异，只是更脏、更挤、更黑——更凄凉倒谈不上。这个词成了笑柄。去年，纽约有十万人住在后院廉租公寓里。这里有一间屋子比别处齐整些。妇人是个结实的当家主妇，脸上刻着操劳的硬纹，正在洗衣盆边劳作。「我总想法子让这些娃儿干干净净的。」她说，带着歉意，环顾四周的眼神却毫无指望。滚烫皂水的气味，又添进本已被煮洋白菜味、烂布条味和四处不洁的气味浸透的空气里，合成一股压倒一切的混合物。今天是星期四，窗前滑轮晾衣绳上却挂满了打补丁的旧衣。廉租公寓里没有星期一大扫除这一说。天天都是洗衣日，因为穷人家难得有一件换洗衣裳。这一条条常年悬挂晾晒的破衣，是贫穷的诚实徽记——凡不是洗衣妇职业招牌的那些，皆是如此。要区分贫民化（pauperism）与清白的贫穷，真正的界线是那根晾衣绳。从它开始，才有力求洁净的努力——那是想做个诚实的人的第一份、也是最好的一份凭证。

===Original===
What sort of an answer, think you, would come from these tenements to the question “Is life worth living?” were they heard at all in the discussion? It may be that this, cut from the last report but one of the Association for the Improvement of the Condition of the Poor, a long name for a weary task, has a suggestion of it: “In the depth of winter the attention of the Association was called to a Protestant family living in a garret in a miserable tenement in Cherry Street. The family’s condition was most deplorable. The man, his wife, and three small children shivering in one room through the roof of which the pitiless winds of winter whistled. The room was almost barren of furniture; the parents slept on the floor, the elder children in boxes, and the baby was swung in an old shawl attached to the rafters by cords by way of a hammock. The father, a seaman, had been obliged to give up that calling because he was in consumption, and was unable to provide either bread or fire for his little ones.”

===Chinese===
试想，倘若这些廉租公寓真能在讨论中发出声音，问它们一句「人生值得活吗」，会得到怎样的回答？下面这段文字剪自贫民状况改善协会（Association for the Improvement of the Condition of the Poor）倒数第二份年报——好长的名字，配一桩累人的差事——也许透出一点答案：「隆冬时节，本协会注意到樱桃街一所悲惨廉租公寓的阁楼里，住着一个新教徒家庭。这家人的境况极其凄惨。丈夫、妻子和三个幼小的孩子挤在一间屋子里发抖，无情的朔风就从屋顶呼啸而过。屋里几乎没有什么家具；父母睡在地板上，大些的孩子睡在木箱里，婴儿则吊在一条旧披巾里，披巾用绳子拴在椽木上，权当吊床。父亲是个水手，因染肺痨不得不放弃那个营生，连面包和炉火都无力供给他的孩子们。」

===Original===
Perhaps this may be put down as an exceptional case, but one that came to my notice some months ago in a Seventh Ward tenement was typical enough to escape that reproach. There were nine in the family: husband, wife, an aged grandmother, and six children; honest, hardworking Germans, scrupulously neat, but poor. All nine lived in two rooms, one about ten feet square that served as parlor, bedroom, and eating-room, the other a small hall-room made into a kitchen. The rent was seven dollars and a half a month, more than a week’s wages for the husband and father, who was the only breadwinner in the family. That day the mother had thrown herself out of the window, and was carried up from the street dead. She was “discouraged,” said some of the other women from the tenement, who had come in to look after the children while a messenger carried the news to the father at the shop. They went stolidly about their task, although they were evidently not without feeling for the dead woman. No doubt she was wrong in not taking life philosophically, as did the four families a city missionary found housekeeping in the four corners of one room. They got along well enough together until one of the families took a boarder and made trouble. Philosophy, according to my optimistic friend, naturally inhabits the tenements. The people who live there come to look upon death in a different way from the rest of us﻿—do not take it as hard. He has never found time to explain how the fact fits into his general theory that life is not unbearable in the tenements. Unhappily for the philosophy of the slums, it is too apt to be of the kind that readily recognizes the saloon, always handy, as the refuge from every trouble, and shapes its practice according to the discovery.

===Chinese===
这一桩或许可以记作特例；但几个月前进入我视线的第七选区一户廉租公寓人家，就典型得足以免受这种指摘了。这家九口人：丈夫、妻子、一位年老的外祖母和六个孩子；是诚实、勤劳的德国人，极其讲究整洁，却穷。九口人住两间屋：一间约十英尺见方，兼作客厅、卧室和饭厅；另一间是小过道间，改作了厨房。房租每月七块半美元，比那位丈夫兼父亲一个星期的工钱还多，而他是一家里唯一挣面包的人。就在那一天，那位母亲从窗口跳了下去，被人从街上抬起来时已经死了。她是「心灰了」，楼里另外几个女人这样说；她们进来照看孩子，一边打发人去工场把消息带给孩子的父亲。她们木然地做着自己的事，虽然对死者显然并非全无感情。她错就错在没能像那四户人家那样达观地对待生活——一位城市传教士曾发现那四户合住在一间屋子的四个角落里各自当家过日子。他们原本相安无事，直到其中一户收了个搭铺客，惹出了乱子。照我那位乐观的朋友说来，达观哲学天然就栖息在廉租公寓里。住在那里的人看待死亡同我们其余人不一样——不往心里去。他一直腾不出工夫解释：这一事实如何塞得进他的那个总理论——廉租公寓里的生活并非不堪忍受。不幸的是，贫民窟的这套哲学，十有八九是下面这一种：它轻而易举地认出近在咫尺的酒馆，把它当作一切烦恼的避难所，并依照这一发现来安排自己的实践。

## V The Italian in New York / 第五章 纽约的意大利人

===Original===
Certainly a picturesque, if not very tidy, element has been added to the population in the “assisted” Italian immigrant who claims so large a share of public attention, partly because he keeps coming at such a tremendous rate, but chiefly because he elects to stay in New York, or near enough for it to serve as his base of operations, and here promptly reproduces conditions of destitution and disorder which, set in the framework of Mediterranean exuberance, are the delight of the artist, but in a matter-of-fact American community become its danger and reproach. The reproduction is made easier in New York because he finds the material ready to hand in the worst of the slum tenements; but even where it is not he soon reduces what he does find to his own level, if allowed to follow his natural bent.7 The Italian comes in at the bottom, and in the generation that came over the sea he stays there. In the slums he is welcomed as a tenant who “makes less trouble” than the contentious Irishman or the order-loving German, that is to say: is content to live in a pigsty and submits to robbery at the hands of the rent-collector without murmur. Yet this very tractability makes of him in good hands, when firmly and intelligently managed, a really desirable tenant. But it is not his good fortune often to fall in with other hospitality upon his coming than that which brought him here for its own profit, and has no idea of letting go its grip upon him as long as there is a cent to be made out of him.

===Chinese===
诚然，「受资助」（“assisted”）的意大利移民给这座城市的人口添上了一种颇具画意的成分——虽说远算不上整洁。这一群人之所以占据公众如此多的注意，一部分是因为他们涌来的势头实在惊人，但更主要的是因为他们选定留在纽约，或者住在离城足够近的地方，好把纽约当作自己营生的根据地，而且一到这里，便立刻再造出赤贫与混乱的生活境况。这种境况若镶在地中海式浓烈风情的框子里，正是画家的赏心乐事；可在讲求实际的美国社会里，它就成了危险与耻辱。在纽约，这种再造格外容易，因为最糟糕的贫民窟廉租公寓里现成摆着底子；不过即便没有现成的，只要听任他顺着本性发展，他很快也能把眼前的环境拉低到自己的水准。7 意大利人一来就落到了最底层，而且在漂洋过海而来的这一代人里，他就一直待在那里。在贫民窟里，他作为房客倒是受欢迎的，因为他比好争执的爱尔兰人或者热爱秩序的德国人「惹的麻烦少」——这就是说：他住得跟猪圈一样也心满意足，收租人怎么敲诈他都毫无怨言。然而恰恰是这种顺从，使他在得宜的管理之下——只要管得坚决而有头脑——成为一个真正合意的房客。可惜他初来乍到时，难得碰上别的什么善遇：把他弄到这里来的那股势力原是图着自身的利益，只要还能从他身上榨出一个铜板，就绝没有松手的道理。

===Original===
Recent Congressional inquiries have shown the nature of the “assistance” he receives from greedy steamship agents and “bankers,” who persuade him by false promises to mortgage his home, his few belongings, and his wages for months to come for a ticket to the land where plenty of work is to be had at princely wages. The padrone﻿—the “banker,” is nothing else﻿—having made his ten percent out of him en route, receives him at the landing and turns him to double account as a wage-earner and a rent-payer. In each of these roles he is made to yield a profit to his unscrupulous countryman, whom he trusts implicitly with the instinct of utter helplessness. The man is so ignorant that, as one of the sharpers who prey upon him put it once, it “would be downright sinful not to take him in.” His ignorance and unconquerable suspicion of strangers dig the pit into which he falls. He not only knows no word of English, but he does not know enough to learn. Rarely only can he write his own language. Unlike the German, who begins learning English the day he lands as a matter of duty, or the Polish Jew, who takes it up as soon as he is able as an investment, the Italian learns slowly, if at all. Even his boy, born here, often speaks his native tongue indifferently. He is forced, therefore, to have constant recourse to the middleman, who makes him pay handsomely at every turn. He hires him out to the railroad contractor, receiving a commission from the employer as well as from the laborer, and repeats the performance monthly, or as often as he can have him dismissed. In the city he contracts for his lodging, subletting to him space in the vilest tenements at extortionate rents, and sets an example that does not lack imitators. The “princely wages” have vanished with his coming, and in their place hardships and a dollar a day, beheft with the padrone’s merciless mortgage, confront him. Bred to even worse fare, he takes both as a matter of course, and, applying the maxim that it is not what one makes but what he saves that makes him rich, manages to turn the very dirt of the streets into a hoard of gold, with which he either returns to his Southern home, or brings over his family to join in his work and in his fortunes the next season.

===Chinese===
近来的国会调查已经揭露了这种「资助」的性质：贪婪的轮船公司代理人和「银行家」（“bankers”，代客汇兑管钱的意侨「银行家」）用虚假的许诺劝他把老家的房子、寥寥几件家当，连同往后数月的工钱统统抵押掉，换一张船票，去往那个活计多得做不完、工钱优厚赛王侯的国度。帕德罗内（padrone，意大利语「老板」，包揽同乡劳工雇佣与盘剥的中间人）——所谓「银行家」，无非就是他——一路上先从他身上赚足了百分之十的好处，等他一登岸便把他接住，让他一身派上两个用场：既当挣工钱的劳力，又当交房租的房客。在这两种身份里，他都得向那个无所顾忌的同胞交出一份利润，而他对这同胞毫无保留的信赖，纯粹是走投无路者的本能。这人无知到什么地步？正如一个专靠盘剥他为生的骗子有一次所说，不宰他「简直是罪过」。他的无知，加上他对生人那种改不掉的猜疑，亲手掘下了他随后跌进去的坑。他不但一个英文字都不识，甚至连该怎么学都不懂。能写本国文字的都难得找出几个。德国人把登岸当天就开始学英文当作本分，波兰犹太人一有能力便把学英文当作投资抓起来；意大利人却学得极慢，甚至根本不学。连他生在这里的儿子，说起祖国的语言来也常常含含糊糊。于是他不得不事事仰仗中间人，而中间人每经一手都要他付出高昂的代价。中间人把他雇给铁路承包商，从雇主和工人两头抽佣金，而且每月如法炮制一回——只要能设法让他被辞掉，就再演一遍。在城里，中间人替他包下住处，把最龌龊的廉租公寓里的床位以敲竹杠的租金转租给他，还树起了一个不乏仿效者的榜样。所谓「王侯般的工钱」随着他的到来烟消云散，取而代之的是重重苦楚和一天一美元，外加帕德罗内那笔无情抵押债压在头上。他从小过的日子比这还苦，便把这两样都视作理所当然；又本着「使人富的不是挣下多少，而是省下多少」的信条，硬是把街上的尘土脏物攒成了一囤金子——揣着这笔积蓄，他要么回意大利南部的老家去，要么第二年把家眷接过来，同他一起做工，也共享他的这份运气。

===Original===
The discovery was made by earlier explorers that there is money in New York’s ash-barrel, but it was left to the genius of the padrone to develop the full resources of the mine that has become the exclusive preserve of the Italian immigrant. Only a few years ago, when rag-picking was carried on in a desultory and irresponsible sort of way, the city hired gangs of men to trim the ash-scows before they were sent out to sea. The trimming consisted in levelling out the dirt as it was dumped from the carts, so that the scow might be evenly loaded. The men were paid a dollar and a half a day, kept what they found that was worth having, and allowed the swarms of Italians who hung about the dumps to do the heavy work for them, letting them have their pick of the loads for their trouble. Today Italians contract for the work, paying large sums to be permitted to do it. The city received not less than $80,000 last year for the sale of this privilege to the contractors, who in addition have to pay gangs of their countrymen for sorting out the bones, rags, tin cans and other waste that are found in the ashes and form the staples of their trade and their sources of revenue. The effect has been vastly to increase the power of the padrone, or his ally, the contractor, by giving him exclusive control of the one industry in which the Italian was formerly an independent “dealer,” and reducing him literally to the plane of the dump. Whenever the back of the sanitary police is turned, he will make his home in the filthy burrows where he works by day, sleeping and eating his meals under the dump, on the edge of slimy depths and amid surroundings full of unutterable horror. The city did not bargain to house, though it is content to board, him so long as he can make the ash-barrels yield the food to keep him alive, and a vigorous campaign is carried on at intervals against these unlicensed dump settlements; but the temptation of having to pay no rent is too strong, and they are driven from one dump only to find lodgement under another a few blocks farther up or down the river. The fiercest warfare is waged over the patronage of the dumps by rival factions represented by opposing contractors, and it has happened that the defeated party has endeavored to capture by strategy what he failed to carry by assault. It augurs unsuspected adaptability in the Italian to our system of self-government that these rivalries have more than once been suspected of being behind the sharpening of city ordinances, that were apparently made in good faith to prevent meddling with the refuse in the ash-barrels or in transit.

===Chinese===
更早的探索者已经发现，纽约的炉灰桶里有油水；可要把这座矿藏的全部资源开发出来，还得靠帕德罗内的天才——如今这座矿已成了意大利移民独占的禁地。就在几年前，拾荒还是有一搭没一搭、没人负责的营生，市里雇了一伙伙的人，在灰土驳船出海之前把它们平整好。所谓平整，就是把一车车倒下来的灰土摊平，好让驳船装得均匀。干这行的人日薪一美元半，捡到值钱的东西归自己所有，还听任成群在垃圾场周围打转的意大利人替他们干重活，让这些人出力换得先挑一车的权利。如今这活计轮到意大利人承包了，还得交纳一大笔钱才被允许去干。去年，市府把这项特权卖给承包人，收入不下 $80,000；承包人此外还得雇用一帮帮本国同胞，从炉灰里分拣出骨头、破布、洋铁罐和其他废品——这些正是这一行的基本货色，也是他们的收入来源。其结果是帕德罗内或者他的盟友承包人的权势大增：意大利人从前在这一行里还算个独立的「经销商」，如今被置于他独家把持之下，实实在在沦落到与垃圾场齐平的地步。只要卫生警察一转过背去，他就在白天干活的那些污秽洞穴里安下家来，睡在垃圾场底下，吃在垃圾场底下，紧挨着黏滑的深渊边缘，四周尽是无法言说的可怖景象。市府从来没有答应给他安排住处——虽说只要他能从炉灰桶里刨出维持活命的吃食，市府倒乐得管他的饭——于是隔一阵子就对这批无照的垃圾场聚落发动一场雷厉风行的清剿。可是「一个子儿房租都不用付」的诱惑太强了，他们被从一处垃圾场赶走，只会在河的上游或下游几个街区外的另一处垃圾场底下再找到栖身之所。围绕垃圾场的包揽权，由对立承包人挂帅的敌对派系之间进行着最凶狠的争斗，败阵的一方甚至曾企图用计谋夺取强攻不下的东西。这些派系争斗不止一次被疑为是市政府收紧条例的幕后推手——那些条例表面上倒是诚意十足，为的是禁止人们翻动炉灰桶里的或转运途中的垃圾。这一事实倒预示着，意大利人对我们这套自治体制有着令人料想不到的适应力。

===Original===
Did the Italian always adapt himself as readily to the operation of the civil law as to the manipulation of political “pull” on occasion, he would save himself a good deal of unnecessary trouble. Ordinarily he is easily enough governed by authority﻿—always excepting Sunday, when he settles down to a game of cards and lets loose all his bad passions. Like the Chinese, the Italian is a born gambler. His soul is in the game from the moment the cards are on the table, and very frequently his knife is in it too before the game is ended. No Sunday has passed in New York since “the Bend” became a suburb of Naples without one or more of these murderous affrays coming to the notice of the police. As a rule that happens only when the man the game went against is either dead or so badly wounded as to require instant surgical help. As to the other, unless he be caught red-handed, the chances that the police will ever get him are slim indeed. The wounded man can seldom be persuaded to betray him. He wards off all inquiries with a wicked “I fix him myself,” and there the matter rests until he either dies or recovers. If the latter, the community hears after a while of another Italian affray, a man stabbed in a quarrel, dead or dying, and the police know that “he” has been fixed, and the account squared.

===Chinese===
倘若意大利人对民法的运转总能像偶尔对政治「门路」（“pull”）的操弄那样应付自如，他本可以替自己省去许多不必要的麻烦。平日里，用权威管住他并不难——唯独星期天除外：一到星期天，他便安下心来打牌，把满腔的恶劣情绪一齐放出来。和中国人一样，意大利人是天生的赌徒。牌一上桌，他的魂就进了牌局；而且常常牌局还没收场，他的刀子也进了局。自从「弯道区」（the Bend）变成那不勒斯的郊区以来，纽约没有一个星期天不发生一起或几起这类凶残的斗殴，闹到警察那里。而照例，只有当输牌的一方已经死了、或者伤得非立刻施救不可的时候，事情才会传到警察耳中。至于另一方，除非被当场人赃并获，警察抓到他的机会实在渺茫。受伤的人很少能被劝得供出他来。他一句恶狠狠的「我自己收拾他」，把一切盘问都挡了回去，事情便就此搁下，直到他要么死了，要么好了。若是后者，过一阵子社会上便会听说又起了一场意大利人的斗殴：有人吵架挨了刀，死了或是垂死，警察心里明白：「他」已经被收拾掉了，这笔账也两清了。

===Original===
With all his conspicuous faults, the swarthy Italian immigrant has his redeeming traits. He is as honest as he is hotheaded. There are no Italian burglars in the Rogues’ Gallery; the ex-brigand toils peacefully with pickaxe and shovel on American ground. His boy occasionally shows, as a pickpocket, the results of his training with the toughs of the Sixth Ward slums. The only criminal business to which the father occasionally lends his hand, outside of murder, is a bunco game, of which his confiding countrymen, returning with their hoard to their native land, are the victims. The women are faithful wives and devoted mothers. Their vivid and picturesque costumes lend a tinge of color to the otherwise dull monotony of the slums they inhabit. The Italian is gay, lighthearted and, if his fur is not stroked the wrong way, inoffensive as a child. His worst offence is that he keeps the stale-beer dives. Where his headquarters is, in the Mulberry Street Bend, these vile dens flourish and gather about them all the wrecks, the utterly wretched, the hopelessly lost, on the lowest slope of depraved humanity. And out of their misery he makes a profit.

===Chinese===
肤色黝黑的意大利移民尽管毛病显眼，却也自有其可取之处。他有多火爆，就有多诚实。案犯照片陈列室（Rogues’ Gallery）里找不到意大利夜盗；昔日啸聚山林的强盗，如今抡着镐头铁锹，在美国的土地上安分做工。倒是他的孩子偶尔会以扒手的身份露一手，亮出在第六选区贫民窟的恶棍堆里受训的成果。做父亲的除了杀人之外偶尔插上一手的唯一一桩犯罪的买卖，是「邦科」骗局（bunco game）——吃亏的正是他那轻信人的同胞：这些揣着积蓄返回故土的人，成了骗局里的冤大头。意大利女人是忠实的妻子、尽心竭力的母亲。她们那鲜亮悦目的衣装，给自己栖身的贫民窟平添了一抹色彩，冲淡了那里死沉沉的单调。意大利人生性快活、无忧无虑，只要别逆着毛捋他，就跟孩子一样不伤人。他最重的罪过，在于开坏啤酒窟（stale-beer dive）。在他的大本营桑树街弯道，这些下流的窟窿生意兴隆，把人性堕落最底坡上的全部残骸——彻底凄惨的、无可救药的人们——统统聚拢到自己身边。而他从他们的苦难中捞取利润。

## VI The Bend / VI 弯道区
===Original===
Where Mulberry Street crooks like an elbow within hail of the old depravity of the Five Points, is “the Bend,” foul core of New York’s slums. Long years ago the cows coming home from the pasture trod a path over this hill. Echoes of tinkling bells linger there still, but they do not call up memories of green meadows and summer fields; they proclaim the homecoming of the ragpicker’s cart. In the memory of man the old cow-path has never been other than a vast human pigsty. There is but one “Bend” in the world, and it is enough. The city authorities, moved by the angry protests of ten years of sanitary reform effort, have decided that it is too much and must come down. Another Paradise Park will take its place and let in sunlight and air to work such transformation as at the Five Points, around the corner of the next block. Never was change more urgently needed. Around “the Bend” cluster the bulk of the tenements that are stamped as altogether bad, even by the optimists of the Health Department. Incessant raids cannot keep down the crowds that make them their home. In the scores of back alleys, of stable lanes and hidden byways, of which the rent collector alone can keep track, they share such shelter as the ramshackle structures afford with every kind of abomination rifled from the dumps and ash barrels of the city. Here, too, shunning the light, skulks the unclean beast of dishonest idleness. “The Bend” is the home of the tramp as well as the ragpicker.

===Chinese===
桑树街（Mulberry Street）在这里拐出一个肘弯，与五点区（Five Points）那老牌的藏污纳垢之地只隔着一声吆喝的距离——这里便是「弯道区」（the Bend），纽约贫民窟污秽的核心。多年以前，放牧归来的牛群在山丘上踏出了一条小径。叮咚的铃铛声至今还在那里萦回，只是它唤不起绿草如茵的牧场和夏日田野的记忆；它宣告的是拾荒车归家。在世人的记忆里，这条老牛径从来就不是别的什么，而是一座庞大的人养猪圈。天底下只有一个「弯道区」，一个也就够了。市政当局被十年卫生改革努力的愤怒抗议所推动，终于断定它委实太过分，非拆不可。另一座乐园公园（Paradise Park）将取而代之，让阳光和空气进来，施展出如同在五点区——就在下一个街区拐角处——已然完成的那样的改造。从来没有哪一项变革比这更加刻不容缓。簇拥在「弯道区」四周的，大都是被盖上了“彻头彻尾糟糕”之印的廉租公寓（tenement）——连卫生局（Health Department）里的乐天派也不例外。无休无止的突击搜查，也压不住把这里安作家园的那一群群人。在几十条后巷、马厩弄和隐蔽的小道里——只有收租人一人摸得清它们的底细——他们同从城市垃圾场和炉灰桶里扒来的形形色色龌龊货色挤在一处，分用那些摇摇欲坠的破屋所能提供的栖身之地。在这里，避着光、贼头贼脑出没的，还有好逸恶劳这头不洁的畜生。「弯道区」既是拾荒者的家，也是流浪汉的家。

===Original===
It is not much more than twenty years since a census of “the Bend” district returned only twenty-four of the six hundred and nine tenements as in decent condition. Three-fourths of the population of the “Bloody Sixth” Ward were then Irish. The army of tramps that grew up after the disbandment of the armies in the field, and has kept up its muster roll, together with the inrush of the Italian tide, have ever since opposed a stubborn barrier to all efforts at permanent improvement. The more that has been done, the less it has seemed to accomplish in the way of real relief, until it has at last become clear that nothing short of entire demolition will ever prove of radical benefit. Corruption could not have chosen ground for its stand with better promise of success. The whole district is a maze of narrow, often unsuspected passageways﻿—necessarily, for there is scarce a lot that has not two, three, or four tenements upon it, swarming with unwholesome crowds. What a birds-eye view of “the Bend” would be like is a matter of bewildering conjecture. Its everyday appearance, as seen from the corner of Bayard Street on a sunny day, is one of the sights of New York.

===Chinese===
也不过就是二十来年前，对「弯道区」地带的一次清查还只把六百零九栋廉租公寓中的二十四栋报为状况像样。当时「流血的第六选区」（the “Bloody Sixth” Ward）的人口，四分之三是爱尔兰人。战场上的大军遣散之后拉起来、且花名册一直不曾缩短的流浪汉大军，再加上意大利移民潮的涌入，从此给一切谋求永久改善的努力横起了一道顽固的屏障。做的事情越多，在真正解救上收的效却似乎越少；直到最后终于看明白：不整片拆光，便休想有什么根治之效。腐败若要择地扎营顽抗，再也挑不出一处比这更有胜算的地盘了。整个地区是一座窄道织成的迷宫，其中许多条外人根本想不到——这也在所难免，因为几乎每一块宅地上都立着两座、三座、四座廉租公寓，里面挤满于健康有碍的人群。「弯道区」的鸟瞰图会是一副什么光景，是一个令人茫然无从揣想的问题。晴天从贝亚德街（Bayard Street）街角望去，它平日的景象，算得上纽约的一景。

===Original===
Bayard Street is the high road to Jewtown across the Bowery, picketed from end to end with the outposts of Israel. Hebrew faces, Hebrew signs, and incessant chatter in the queer lingo that passes for Hebrew on the East Side attend the curious wanderer to the very corner of Mulberry Street. But the moment he turns the corner the scene changes abruptly. Before him lies spread out what might better be the marketplace in some town in Southern Italy than a street in New York﻿—all but the houses; they are still the same old tenements of the unromantic type. But for once they do not make the foreground in a slum picture from the American metropolis. The interest centres not in them, but in the crowd they shelter only when the street is not preferable, and that with the Italian is only when it rains or he is sick. When the sun shines the entire population seeks the street, carrying on its household work, its bargaining, its lovemaking on street or sidewalk, or idling there when it has nothing better to do, with the reverse of the impulse that makes the Polish Jew coop himself up in his den with the thermometer at stewing heat. Along the curb women sit in rows, young and old alike with the odd head-covering, pad or turban, that is their badge of servitude﻿—hers to bear the burden as long as she lives﻿—haggling over baskets of frowsy weeds, some sort of salad probably, stale tomatoes, and oranges not above suspicion. Ash-barrels serve them as counters, and not infrequently does the arrival of the official cart en route for the dump cause a temporary suspension of trade until the barrels have been emptied and restored. Hucksters and pedlars’ carts make two rows of booths in the street itself, and along the houses is still another﻿—a perpetual market doing a very lively trade in its own queer staples, found nowhere on American ground save in “the Bend.” Two old hags, camping on the pavement, are dispensing stale bread, baked not in loaves, but in the shape of big wreaths like exaggerated crullers, out of bags of dirty bed-tick. There is no use disguising the fact: they look like and they probably are old mattresses mustered into service under the pressure of a rush of trade. Stale bread was the one article the health officers, after a raid on the market, once reported as “not unwholesome.” It was only disgusting. Here is a brawny butcher, sleeves rolled up above the elbows and clay pipe in mouth, skinning a kid that hangs from his hook. They will tell you with a laugh at the Elizabeth Street police station that only a few days ago when a dead goat had been reported lying in Pell Street it was mysteriously missing by the time the offal-cart came to take it away. It turned out that an Italian had carried it off in his sack to a wake or feast of some sort in one of the back alleys.

===Chinese===
贝亚德街是横过鲍厄里（the Bowery）通往「犹太镇」（Jewtown）的大道，从头到尾布满了以色列的前哨。希伯来人的面孔、希伯来文的招牌，还有下东区（East Side）上冒充希伯来语的那种古怪行话中喋喋不休的喧哗，一路殷勤陪伴着好奇的漫游者，直送到桑树街街角。可是他一拐过街角，景象便陡然一变。铺展在他面前的，与其说是纽约的一条街，不如说更像是意大利南部某座小镇的集市——只有房子除外；那些依旧是不带一点浪漫色彩的老式廉租公寓。只不过这一回，在这幅来自美国大都会的贫民窟图景里，它们没有占据前景。引人入胜的中心不是房子，而是它们收容的那群人——这些人只在街道不是更可取时才进去躲一躲，而对意大利人来说，那只在下雨或生病的时候。太阳一出，全体居民便涌上街头，把家务、讨价还价、谈情说爱统统搬到街上或人行道上去办，没事可干的时候就在那里闲待着——这股劲头，同把波兰犹太人关在闷罐似的小屋里、任温度计爬到炖煮一般高温的那股冲动恰好相反。沿着路边侧石，女人们一排排坐着，老少一个样，都戴着那种古怪的头衣——头垫或头巾，那是她们受苦役身份的标记——这副担子她要一直挑到闭眼那天——她们为一篮篮蔫头耷脑的菜草（多半是某种做沙拉的青菜）、不新鲜的番茄和来路说不清楚的橙子讨价还价。炉灰桶给她们当柜台，而开往垃圾场的公务垃圾车一到，常常造成买卖的暂时歇业，直到桶被倒空、物归原位。小贩和货郎车在街面上排出两行摊子，沿房屋又是一行——一个终年不散的市场，用它自己那套古怪货色做着十分红火的买卖，这等货色，在美国地面上除了「弯道区」别处一概寻不见。两个老太婆在人行道上扎营，从一条条肮脏的床袋布里往外售卖陈面包——那面包不烤成一个个的条，而烤成大花环的样子，活像放大了的油煎麻花圈。用不着掩饰这个事实：它们看上去就像、多半也确实是几张老床垫，在买卖一时应接不暇的压力下被征调来充数的。陈面包是卫生官们突查这个市场之后曾报为“并非有碍健康”的唯一货品。它只是令人作呕罢了。这边是一位膀大腰圆的屠夫，袖子挽到臂肘以上，嘴里叼着陶土烟斗，正给挂在钩子上的一只小山羊剥皮。在伊丽莎白街（Elizabeth Street）警察分局，人们会笑着告诉你：就在几天前，有人报告派尔街（Pell Street）上躺着一头死山羊，等收运下水的车前来拉走时，它却神秘地不见了。后来查明，是一个意大利人把它塞进麻袋背走了，弄到某条后巷里的一场守灵宴或什么席面上去了。

===Original===
On either side of the narrow entrance to Bandits’ Roost, one of the most notorious of these, is a shop that is a fair sample of the sort of invention necessity is the mother of in “the Bend.” It is not enough that trucks and ash-barrels have provided four distinct lines of shops that are not down on the insurance maps, to accommodate the crowds. Here have the very hallways been made into shops. Three feet wide by four deep, they have just room for one, the shopkeeper, who, himself within, does his business outside, his wares displayed on a board hung across what was once the hall door. Back of the rear wall of this unique shop a hole has been punched from the hall into the alley and the tenants go that way. One of the shops is a “tobacco bureau,” presided over by an unknown saint, done in yellow and red﻿—there is not a shop, a stand, or an ash-barrel doing duty for a counter, that has not its patron saint﻿—the other is a fish-stand full of slimy, odd-looking creatures, fish that never swam in American waters, or if they did, were never seen on an American fish-stand, and snails. Big, awkward sausages, anything but appetizing, hang in the grocer’s doorway, knocking against the customer’s head as if to remind him that they are there waiting to be bought. What they are I never had the courage to ask. Down the street comes a file of women carrying enormous bundles of firewood on their heads, loads of decaying vegetables from the market wagons in their aprons, and each a baby at the breast supported by a sort of sling that prevents it from tumbling down. The women do all the carrying, all the work one sees going on in “the Bend.” The men sit or stand in the streets, on trucks, or in the open doors of the saloons smoking black clay pipes, talking and gesticulating as if forever on the point of coming to blows. Near a particularly boisterous group, a really pretty girl with a string of amber beads twisted artlessly in the knot of her raven hair has been bargaining long and earnestly with an old granny, who presides over a wheelbarrow load of secondhand stockings and faded cotton yarn, industriously darning the biggest holes while she extols the virtues of her stock. One of the rude swains, with patched overalls tucked into his boots, to whom the girl’s eyes have strayed more than once, steps up and gallantly offers to pick her out the handsomest pair, whereat she laughs and pushes him away with a gesture which he interprets as an invitation to stay; and he does, evidently to the satisfaction of the beldame, who forthwith raises her prices fifty percent without being detected by the girl.

===Chinese===
「强盗窝」（Bandits’ Roost）是这类后巷里最臭名昭著者之一；它那狭窄入口的两侧各有一间铺子，堪为“需要乃发明之母”在「弯道区」里孵出的那一类发明的好样本。大卡车和炉灰桶已经提供了四条保险地图上根本不载的铺子线来接待人群，这还不够。连过道本身都给改成了铺子。宽三英尺、进深四英尺，刚刚容得下一个人——店主本人；他人待在铺子里，生意却在外头做，货色摆在横跨原先过道门挂起的一块木板上。在这间独一无二的铺子的后墙背后，有人从过道往小巷里凿开了一个洞，房客们便打那里出入。其中一间铺子是「烟草局」，由一位无名圣徒坐镇，涂着黄红两色——「弯道区」里没有一间铺子、一个摊子、一只充任柜台的炉灰桶是没有自己的保护圣徒的——另一间是鱼摊，摆满黏糊糊、生相古怪的生物：从来没在美国水域里游过的鱼——就算游过，也从没在美国鱼摊上露过面——还有蜗牛。粗大笨拙的香肠——说什么也引不起食欲——挂在杂货铺门口，磕碰着顾客的脑袋，仿佛提醒他：它们正在那儿等着被买走。它们究竟是什么做的，我始终没敢问。街上过来一列妇女，头上顶着一大捆一大捆的柴火，围裙里兜着从集市货车上扒下来的一堆堆腐烂菜蔬，胸前各自兜着一个吃奶的婴儿，用一种吊带兜住，免得跌落下来。妇女们包下了全部的搬运，包下了人们在「弯道区」见得到的全部活计。男人们呢，或坐或站在街上、卡车上、酒馆敞开的门口，抽着黑色陶土烟斗，一边说话一边打手势，仿佛永远处在眼看要动手斗殴的边缘。在一群闹得格外响的人旁边，一个当真标致的姑娘——乌黑的发髻里随随便便绞着一串琥珀珠链——正跟一位老奶奶一本正经地讨价还价，缠了许久；老奶奶看管着一独轮车的二手长袜和褪了色的棉线，一边起劲地夸赞自家货色的种种好处，一边勤快地织补最大的破洞。一个粗鲁的献殷勤汉子——补丁摞补丁的工装裤脚掖进靴筒里——姑娘的眼睛已经不止一次溜向他——走上前来，殷勤地提出要帮她挑一双最好看的长袜；姑娘听了哈哈一笑，把他推开，可那手势在他看来分明是请他留下来的邀请；他也就留下了，而且显然让那位老太婆称心满意——她当即把价钱抬高了百分之五十，还没有被姑娘察觉。

===Original===
Red bandanas and yellow kerchiefs are everywhere; so is the Italian tongue, infinitely sweeter than the harsh gutturals of the Russian Jew around the corner. So are the *ristorantes* of innumerable Pasquales; half of the people in “the Bend” are christened Pasquale, or get the name in some other way. When the police do not know the name of an escaped murderer, they guess at Pasquale and send the name out on alarm; in nine cases out of ten it fits. So are the “banks” that hang out their shingle as tempting bait on every hand. There are half a dozen in the single block, steamship agencies, employment offices, and savings-banks, all in one. So are the toddling youngsters, bowlegged half of them, and so are no end of mothers, present and prospective, some of them scarce yet in their teens. Those who are not in the street are hanging halfway out of the windows, shouting at someone below. All “the Bend” must be, if not altogether, at least half out of doors when the sun shines.

===Chinese===
红色大头巾和黄色帕子四处皆是；意大利话也四处皆是——比起街角那边俄国犹太人粗厉的喉音来，它要悦耳动听得多。还有无数个帕斯夸莱（Pasquale）开的 *ristorantes*（意大利餐馆）也四处皆是；「弯道区」里一半的人受洗得名帕斯夸莱，或是以别的路子得了这个名字。警察不知道在逃杀人凶手的姓名时，就猜他是帕斯夸莱，把这名字随警报发出去；十有八九不会猜错。还有那些「银行」也四处皆是——四面八方挂出招牌，当作诱人的香饵。光这一个街区里就有半打，轮船公司代理、职业介绍所兼储蓄银行，三位一体。蹒跚学步的小娃娃们也是如此——一半是罗圈腿；数不清的母亲们，现役的带候选的，也是如此——有的甚至还几乎没跨进十几岁的门槛。不在街上的那些，正半个身子探出窗外，朝底下什么人喊话。太阳一出，整个「弯道区」就算不是全体、至少也得有一半待在露天里。

===Original===
In the street, where the city wields the broom, there is at least an effort at cleaning up. There has to be, or it would be swamped in filth overrunning from the courts and alleys where the ragpickers live. It requires more than ordinary courage to explore these on a hot day. The undertaker has to do it then, the police always. Right here, in this tenement on the east side of the street, they found little Antonia Candia, victim of fiendish cruelty, “covered,” says the account found in the records of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, “with sores, and her hair matted with dried blood.” Abuse is the normal condition of “the Bend,” murder its everyday crop, with the tenants not always the criminals. In this block between Bayard, Park, Mulberry, and Baxter Streets, “the Bend” proper, the late Tenement House Commission counted 155 deaths of children8 in a specimen year (1882). Their percentage of the total mortality in the block was 68.28, while for the whole city the proportion was only 46.20. The infant mortality in any city or place as compared with the whole number of deaths is justly considered a good barometer of its general sanitary condition. Here, in this tenement, No. 59½, next to Bandits’ Roost, fourteen persons died that year, and eleven of them were children; in No. 61 eleven, and eight of them not yet five years old. According to the records in the Bureau of Vital Statistics only thirty-nine people lived in No. 59½ in the year 1888, nine of them little children. There were five baby funerals in that house the same year. Out of the alley itself, No. 59, nine dead were carried in 1888, five in baby coffins. Here is the record of the year for the whole block, as furnished by the Registrar of Vital Statistics, Dr. Roger S. Tracy:

===Chinese===
在街道上——城市在这里挥动扫帚——至少还作一番清扫的努力。也非这样不可，否则它早就被拾荒者聚居的院落和小巷里漫溢出来的污秽淹没了。大热天到这些地方去探一趟，需要的可不只是一般的胆量。那种时候干这差事的是殡葬承办人；而警察则从来免不了。恰恰就在这里，在街东面的这座廉租公寓里，人们发现了小安东尼娅·坎迪亚（Antonia Candia）——恶魔般残虐的受害者；防止虐待儿童协会（Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children）档案中的记载说，她“遍体溃疡，头发被干涸的血黏结成块”。虐待是「弯道区」的常态，凶杀是它每天的收成，而作恶的未必总是房客。在贝亚德街、帕克街（Park Street）、桑树街与巴克斯特街（Baxter Street）之间的这个街区——「弯道区」的本体——上届廉租公寓调查委员会（Tenement House Commission）在一个取样年份（1882 年）里数出 155 起儿童死亡8。儿童占该街区死亡总数的比例达百分之68.28，而全城的比例只有百分之46.20。任何城市或地方的婴儿死亡数同死亡总数相比的比值，向来被公正地视为其总体卫生状况的一支好晴雨表。就在这座紧挨着「强盗窝」的廉租公寓——59½ 号——里，那一年死了十四个人，其中十一个是儿童；61 号死了十一个，其中八个还不满五岁。据生命统计局（Bureau of Vital Statistics）的档案，1888 年住在 59½ 号的只有三十九口人，九个是小孩子。同一年，那座房子里办了五场婴儿的葬礼。单从那条小巷本身——59 号——1888 年抬出了九名死者，五名装在婴儿棺材里。这里是生命统计官（Registrar of Vital Statistics）罗杰·特雷西博士（Dr. Roger S. Tracy）提供的该街区全年记录：

===Original===
The general death-rate for the whole city that year was 26.27.

===Chinese===
那一年全城的总死亡率是 26.27。

===Original===
These figures speak for themselves, when it is shown that in the model tenement across the way at Nos. 48 and 50, where the same class of people live in greater swarms (161, according to the record), but under good management, and in decent quarters, the hearse called that year only twice, once for a baby. The agent of the Christian people who built that tenement will tell you that Italians are good tenants, while the owner of the alley will oppose every order to put his property in repair with the claim that they are the worst of a bad lot. Both are right, from their different standpoints. It is the standpoint that makes the difference﻿—and the tenant.

===Chinese===
这些数字本身就能说明问题：街对面 48 号和 50 号的模范廉租公寓（model tenement）里，住的是同一类人，而且住得更稠密（据记载为 161 人），但因为管理得当、居所像样，那一年灵车统共只来过两次，其中一次还是来接一个婴儿。兴建那座廉租公寓的基督徒们所派的经租人会告诉你，意大利人是好房客；而那条小巷的业主，却会拿“他们是烂货堆里最烂的”做挡箭牌，抗拒每一道责令他把房产修葺妥当的命令。从各自不同的立足点看，两人都没说错。造成差别的正是立足点——外加房客。

===Original===
What if I were to tell you that this alley, and more tenement property in “the Bend,” all of it notorious for years as the vilest and worst to be found anywhere, stood associated on the tax-books all through the long struggle to make its owners responsible, which has at last resulted in a qualified victory for the law, with the name of an honored family, one of the “oldest and best,” rich in possessions and in influence, and high in the councils of the city’s government? It would be but the plain truth. Nor would it be the only instance by very many that stand recorded on the Health Department’s books of a kind that has come near to making the name of landlord as odious in New York as it has become in Ireland.

===Chinese===
倘若我告诉你：这条小巷，连同「弯道区」里更多的廉租公寓产业——它们多年来一直以天下随处可寻的最卑污、最恶劣而声名狼藉——在那场责令业主尽责的长期斗争的全过程中（这场斗争最终以法律的一场打了折扣的胜利收场），始终在税册上同一个受人敬重的家族的名字连在一起——一个位列“最古老、最优秀”的家族，家赀豪富，势力煊赫，在市政府的议事席上高踞要位——你会怎么说？这不过是明明白白的实情罢了。而且这也远远不是仅有的一例；卫生局的簿册上记着许许多多同一类的案子，多得几乎叫「房东」这个名字在纽约变得同它在爱尔兰一样臭不可闻。

===Original===
Bottle Alley is around the corner in Baxter Street; but it is a fair specimen of its kind, wherever found. Look into any of these houses, everywhere the same piles of rags, of malodorous bones and musty paper, all of which the sanitary police flatter themselves they have banished to the dumps and the warehouses. Here is a “flat” of “parlor” and two pitch-dark coops called bedrooms. Truly, the bed is all there is room for. The family teakettle is on the stove, doing duty for the time being as a wash-boiler. By night it will have returned to its proper use again, a practical illustration of how poverty in “the Bend” makes both ends meet. One, two, three beds are there, if the old boxes and heaps of foul straw can be called by that name; a broken stove with crazy pipe from which the smoke leaks at every joint, a table of rough boards propped up on boxes, piles of rubbish in the corner. The closeness and smell are appalling. How many people sleep here? The woman with the red bandana shakes her head sullenly, but the bare-legged girl with the bright face counts on her fingers﻿—five, six!

===Chinese===
瓶子巷（Bottle Alley）就在拐角上的巴克斯特街里；不过它是同类中的一个像样样本，长在哪里都一样。随便走进这些房子中的哪一座看看：处处是同样成堆的破布、气味熏人的骨头和发霉的纸——这些东西，卫生警察们满心以为早叫他们赶进垃圾场和仓库里去了。这里是一套“公寓”：一间“客厅”，加两个漆黑一团、号称卧室的鸽笼。说实在的，屋里只搁得下一张床。家里的茶壶架在炉子上，眼下正充当洗衣锅。到夜里它自会回归本业——这是「弯道区」的贫穷如何让物件一身兼两任、把日子两头对付过去的活例证。床有一张、两张、三张——如果那些旧木箱和一堆堆发臭的稻草也配叫这个名字的话；还有一台破炉子，接着歪歪扭扭的烟囱管，每一个接缝都往外漏烟；一张糙木板钉的桌子，底下用木箱支着；墙角里一堆堆的破烂。屋里的憋闷和那股气味骇人。这里睡多少人？裹红头巾的女人倔着头只是摇，光着腿、脸庞鲜亮的姑娘却扳着指头数——五、六！

===Original===
“Six, sir!” Six grown people and five children.

===Chinese===
“六位，先生！”六个大人和五个孩子。

===Original===
“Only five,” she says with a smile, swathing the little one on her lap in its cruel bandage. There is another in the cradle﻿—actually a cradle. And how much the rent?

===Chinese===
“只有五个，”她微笑着说，一面把膝上的小家伙往那残忍的襁褓里缠裹。摇篮里还有一个——货真价实的摇篮。那么，房租是多少呢？

===Original===
Nine and a half, and “please, sir! he won’t put the paper on.”

===Chinese===
九块半，还外带一句“求求您，先生！他不肯糊墙纸”。

===Original===
“He” is the landlord. The “paper” hangs in musty shreds on the wall.

===Chinese===
“他”是房东。那“墙纸”正一条条发霉的破片挂在墙上。

===Original===
Well do I recollect the visit of a health inspector to one of these tenements on a July day when the thermometer outside was climbing high in the nineties; but inside, in that awful room, with half a dozen persons washing, cooking, and sorting rags, lay the dying baby alongside the stove, where the doctor’s thermometer ran up to 115°! Perishing for the want of a breath of fresh air in this city of untold charities! Did not the manager of the Fresh Air Fund write to the pastor of an Italian Church only last year9 that “no one asked for Italian children,” and hence he could not send any to the country?

===Chinese===
我至今清楚地记得一位卫生视察员七月里的一天到这些廉租公寓中的一座查看的情形：那天，室外的温度计正向着九十多度的高处爬；可是在室内，在那间可怕的屋子里，六七个人在洗涮、做饭、分拣破布，垂死的婴儿就躺在炉子旁边——医生的体温计在那里蹿到了 115°（华氏）！在这座慈善事业多得数也数不清的城市里，竟因缺少一口新鲜空气而活活枯萎！就在去年9，新鲜空气基金（Fresh Air Fund）的干事不还写信给一座意大利教会的牧师，说“没有人来要意大利孩子”，所以他没法送任何孩子下乡吗？

===Original===
Half a dozen blocks up Mulberry Street there is a ragpicker’s settlement, a sort of overflow from “the Bend,” that exists today in all its pristine nastiness. Something like forty families are packed into five old two-story and attic houses that were built to hold five, and out in the yards additional crowds are, or were until very recently, accommodated in sheds built of all sorts of old boards and used as drying racks for the Italian tenants’ “stock.” I found them empty when I visited the settlement while writing this. The last two tenants had just left. Their fate was characteristic. The “old man,” who lived in the corner coop, with barely room to crouch beside the stove﻿—there would not have been room for him to sleep had not age crooked his frame to fit his house﻿—had been taken to the “crazy-house,” and the woman who was his neighbor and had lived in her shed for years had simply disappeared. The agent and the other tenants “guessed,” doubtless correctly, that she might be found on the “island,” but she was decrepit anyhow from rheumatism, and “not much good,” and no one took the trouble to inquire for her. They had all they could do attending to their own business and raising the rent. No wonder; I found that for one front room and two “bedrooms” in the shameful old wrecks of buildings the tenant was paying $10 a month, for the backroom and one bedroom $9, and for the attic rooms, according to size, from $3.75 to $5.50.

===Chinese===
从桑树街往上城方向隔六个街区，有一片拾荒者的聚居地——算是「弯道区」漫溢出来的一泓——今天仍以其全须全尾的原初腌臜存在着。大约四十户人家塞在五座带阁楼的两层老屋里——这些屋子原本是按五户的规模盖的；院子当中，另外的人群栖身在一间间用各色旧木板搭起的棚子里——直到不久前还是如此——那些棚子本是给意大利房客晾晒“存货”的晾架。我为写本章踏访那片聚居地时，发现棚子都空了。最后两户房客刚刚离开。他们的命运颇具典型。住在角落那间鸽笼里的“老头儿”——炉边勉强挤得下他蹲着；若不是岁月把他的骨架压弯得恰好合乎他那间屋，连睡觉的地方也没有——被送进“疯人院”去了；而住在隔壁棚屋里、在那棚子里住了好些年的那个女人，干脆就没了踪影。经租人和别的房客“猜”她多半能到“岛”上（指布莱克韦尔岛）找到，这十有八九猜得不错；但她反正风湿在身、老态龙钟，“派不上什么大用场”了，也就没有人肯费那个神去打听她的下落。他们也实在自顾不暇，光打理自己的营生、张罗房租钱，就已经竭尽全力了。这也不奇怪；我查明，在那几座丢人现眼的老破屋里，租一间前房带两间“卧室”，房客每月要付 $10；后房带一间卧室，$9；阁楼间视大小，从 $3.75 到 $5.50 不等。

===Original===
There is a standing quarrel between the professional﻿—I mean now the official﻿—sanitarian and the unsalaried agitator for sanitary reform over the question of overcrowded tenements. The one puts the number a little vaguely at four or five hundred, while the other asserts that there are thirty-two thousand, the whole number of houses classed as tenements at the census of two years ago, taking no account of the better kind of flats. It depends on the angle from which one sees it which is right. At best the term overcrowding is a relative one, and the scale of official measurement conveniently sliding. Under the pressure of the Italian influx the standard of breathing space required for an adult by the health officers has been cut down from six to four hundred cubic feet. The “needs of the situation” is their plea, and no more perfect argument could be advanced for the reformer’s position.

===Chinese===
在过度拥挤的廉租公寓问题上，职业的——我此刻指的是官方的——卫生学家同不领薪水、鼓吹卫生改革的鼓动者之间，存在着一场旷日持久的争吵。前者把数目说得含含糊糊，四五百座；后者则咬定有三万二千座——即两年前人口普查中列为廉租公寓的房屋总数，且还不把较好的那类公寓套房计算在内。谁对谁错，全看你打哪个角度看。「过度拥挤」这个词说到底是个相对的说法，而官方的量尺偏偏可以随意伸缩。在意大利移民涌入的压力之下，卫生官规定的成人呼吸空间标准，已经从六百立方英尺削减到了四百立方英尺。「形势的需要」是他们的辩词；而要替改革派的立场张目，再也举不出比这更完满的论据了。

===Original===
It is in “the Bend” the sanitary policeman locates the bulk of his four hundred, and the sanitary reformer gives up the task in despair. Of its vast homeless crowds the census takes no account. It is their instinct to shun the light, and they cannot be corralled in one place long enough to be counted. But the houses can, and the last count showed that in “the Bend” district, between Broadway and the Bowery and Canal and Chatham Streets, in a total of four thousand three hundred and sixty-seven “apartments” only nine were for the moment vacant, while in the old “Africa,” west of Broadway, that receives the overflow from Mulberry Street and is rapidly changing its character, the notice “standing room only” is up. Not a single vacant room was found there. Nearly a hundred and fifty “lodgers” were driven out of two adjoining Mulberry Street tenements, one of them aptly named “the House of Blazes,” during that census. What squalor and degradation inhabit these dens the health officers know. Through the long summer days their carts patrol “the Bend,” scattering disinfectants in streets and lanes, in sinks and cellars, and hidden hovels where the tramp burrows. From midnight till far into the small hours of the morning the policeman’s thundering rap on closed doors is heard, with his stern command, “*Apri port’!*” on his rounds gathering evidence of illegal overcrowding. The doors are opened unwillingly enough﻿—but the order means business, and the tenant knows it even if he understands no word of English﻿—upon such scenes as the one presented in the picture. It was photographed by flashlight on just such a visit. In a room not thirteen feet either way slept twelve men and women, two or three in bunks set in a sort of alcove, the rest on the floor. A kerosene lamp burned dimly in the fearful atmosphere, probably to guide other and later arrivals to their “beds,” for it was only just past midnight. A baby’s fretful wail came from an adjoining hall-room, where, in the semidarkness, three recumbent figures could be made out. The “apartment” was one of three in two adjoining buildings we had found, within half an hour, similarly crowded. Most of the men were lodgers, who slept there for five cents a spot.

===Chinese===
卫生警察把他那四百座的大头，恰恰就定位在「弯道区」；而卫生改革家则绝望地撂下了这桩差事。人口普查对它那一支庞大的无家可归的人群不置一录。避光躲亮是他们的本能，休想把他们在同一处圈得够久、久到点清数目。房子却圈得住：最近一次清点表明，在百老汇（Broadway）与鲍厄里之间、运河街（Canal Street）与查塔姆街（Chatham Street）之间的「弯道区」地带，总计四千三百六十七套“住房”里，眼下空着的只有九套；而在百老汇以西的老「非洲」（Africa，当时对黑人聚居区的代称）——它承接桑树街漫出的人口，性格正在迅速改变——那里已经挂出了“只有站票”的告示。那里连一间空房也没有找到。就在那次普查期间，将近一百五十名“搭铺客”被从桑树街上两座毗邻的廉租公寓里赶了出来，其中一座的名字倒起得贴切，唤作「火焰之屋」（the House of Blazes）。这些洞穴里栖身的是怎样的穷酸腌臜、怎样的堕落沉沦，卫生官们心里有数。漫长的夏日里，他们的洒药车在「弯道区」巡逻，在街上巷里、污水池和地窖里，在流浪汉打洞藏身的隐蔽窝棚里，喷洒消毒药水。从午夜直到凌晨深处，听得见警察擂响紧闭房门的咚咚声，同他那声声严厉的口令“*Apri port’!*”（开门！）——他正一路巡行，搜集非法过度拥挤的罪证。门开得老大不情愿——但这道命令是动真格的，房客心里明白，哪怕他一个英文字也不识——门开处，正是画图中展示的那一类场面。那是在一次这样的查访中用闪光灯拍下来的。一间横竖都不到十三英尺的屋子里睡着十二个男人和女人：两三个睡在一种半凹壁龛似的板铺上，其余的睡在地板上。一盏煤油灯在可怕的空气里幽幽地燃着，多半是给后来的、到得更晚的客人们照路，引他们各就各的“床”——因为那时刚过午夜不多一会儿。隔壁一间过道小屋里传来婴儿烦躁的啼哭；半明半暗之中，可以辨出三个横卧的人影。这套“住房”是我们半小时内在两座毗邻楼房里查见的三套之一，三套都同样拥挤。那些男人大多是搭铺客，睡一个位子花五分钱。

===Original===
Another room on the top floor, that had been examined a few nights before, was comparatively empty. There were only four persons in it, two men, an old woman, and a young girl. The landlord opened the door with alacrity, and exhibited with a proud sweep of his hand the sacrifice he had made of his personal interests to satisfy the law. Our visit had been anticipated. The policeman’s back was probably no sooner turned than the room was reopened for business.

===Chinese===
顶层还有一间屋子，几晚之前查看过，比较起来算是空的。屋里只有四个人：两个男人，一个老妇人，一个年轻姑娘。房东手脚麻利地开了门，得意地把手一挥，展示他为迁就法律而在个人利益上作出的牺牲。我们的到访早叫人家料着了。多半是警察前脚刚一转背，那间屋子后脚就重新开张营业了。

## VII A Raid on the Stale-Beer Dives / VII 突击坏啤酒窟

===Original===
Midnight roll-call was over in the Elizabeth Street police-station, but the reserves were held under orders. A raid was on foot, but whether on the Chinese fan-tan games, on the opium joints of Mott and Pell Streets, or on dens of even worse character, was a matter of guesswork in the men’s room. When the last patrolman had come in from his beat, all doubt was dispelled by the brief order “To the Bend!” The stale-beer dives were the object of the raid. The policemen buckled their belts tighter, and with expressive grunts of disgust took up their march toward Mulberry Street. Past the heathen temples of Mott Street﻿—there was some fun to be gotten out of a raid *there*﻿—they trooped, into “the Bend,” sending here and there a belated tramp scurrying in fright toward healthier quarters, and halted at the mouth of one of the hidden alleys. Squads were told off and sent to make a simultaneous descent on all the known tramps’ burrows in the block. Led by the sergeant, ours﻿—I went along as a kind of war correspondent﻿—groped its way in single file through the narrow rift between slimy walls to the tenements in the rear. Twice during our trip we stumbled over tramps, both women, asleep in the passage. They were quietly passed to the rear, receiving sundry prods and punches on the trip, and headed for the station in the grip of a policeman as a sort of advance guard of the coming army. After what seemed half a mile of groping in the dark we emerged finally into the alley proper, where light escaping through the cracks of closed shutters on both sides enabled us to make out the contour of three rickety frame tenements. Snatches of ribald songs and peals of coarse laughter reached us from now this, now that of the unseen burrows.

===Chinese===
伊丽莎白街（Elizabeth Street）警察局的午夜点名已经结束，后备警力却仍奉命待命。一场突查行动正在酝酿，但对象究竟是华人的番摊（fan tan）赌局，是莫特街（Mott Street）和派尔街（Pell Street）的鸦片馆，还是性质更坏的窟穴，在警员室里还只是众说纷纭的猜测。等最后一名巡警巡完管段回来，一句简短的命令把一切疑问都打消了：“到弯道区（the Bend）去！”这次突查的对象是坏啤酒窟（stale-beer dive）。警察们把腰带勒得更紧，带着意味深长的厌恶哼哧声，列队向桑树街（Mulberry Street）进发。他们成群结队走过莫特街那些异教庙宇——到那边突查一场，倒有些乐子可寻——进入了「弯道区」，沿途不时惊得某个夜归的流浪汉仓皇逃向卫生些的去处，最后在一条隐秘小巷的巷口停了下来。一支支小队被分派出去，对这一街区所有已知的流浪汉巢穴同时下手。我们这一队由警佐（sergeant）带队——我跟着去，算是当一名随军记者——在黏滑墙壁之间的狭窄夹缝里鱼贯摸索，走向后方的廉租公寓。一路上我们两次被流浪汉绊到，都是女人，就睡在过道里。她们被悄没声地传到队尾，一路上吃了不少捅戳推搡，由一名警察押着往警察局去了，算是这支即将开到的大军的先遣队。在黑暗中摸索了约莫半英里之后，我们终于走进了小巷正身，两侧紧闭的窗板缝里漏出的一点光，让我们勉强辨认出三座摇摇欲坠的木板廉租公寓的轮廓。下流小调的片段和一阵阵粗野的哄笑，从一个个看不见的巢穴里，忽而这面、忽而那面地传进我们耳中。

===Original===
“School is in,” said the Sergeant drily as we stumbled down the worn steps of the next cellar-way. A kick of his boot-heel sent the door flying into the room.

===Chinese===
“开学了。”我们跌跌撞撞走下又一座地窖入口的破台阶时，警佐干巴巴地说。他一脚后跟踹去，房门便轰的一声飞进屋里。

===Original===
A room perhaps a dozen feet square, with walls and ceiling that might once have been clean﻿—assuredly the floor had not in the memory of man, if indeed there was other floor than hard-trodden mud﻿—but were now covered with a brown crust that, touched with the end of a club, came off in shuddering showers of crawling bugs, revealing the blacker filth beneath. Grouped about a beer-keg that was propped on the wreck of a broken chair, a foul and ragged host of men and women, on boxes, benches, and stools. Tomato-cans filled at the keg were passed from hand to hand. In the centre of the group a sallow, wrinkled hag, evidently the ruler of the feast, dealt out the hideous stuff. A pile of copper coins rattled in her apron, the very pennies received with such showers of blessings upon the giver that afternoon; the faces of some of the women were familiar enough from the streets as those of beggars forever whining for a penny, “to keep a family from starving.” Their whine and boisterous hilarity were alike hushed now. In sullen, cowed submission they sat, evidently knowing what to expect. At the first glimpse of the uniform in the open door some in the group, customers with a record probably, had turned their heads away to avoid the searching glance of the officer; while a few, less used to such scenes, stared defiantly.

===Chinese===
一间大约十二英尺见方的屋子，墙壁和天花板或许也曾干净过——至于地板，在世人的记忆里断断是没有干净过的，倘若那踩得硬实的泥地之外当真另有地板的话——如今墙壁和天花板上都覆着一层棕色的硬壳，用警棍头一碰，便簌簌落下令人发麻的爬虫阵雨，露出底下更黑的污垢。一只啤酒桶架在一把破椅子的残骸上，围着它的是一大群污秽褴褛的男女，或坐木箱，或坐长凳板凳。在桶里灌满啤酒的番茄罐头盒一手一手传过去。人群正中是一个面色蜡黄、满脸皱纹的老妖婆，显然是这场宴席的主宰，正把那可怕的玩意儿一份份分出去。她的围裙兜里哗啦作响地堆着一把铜板，正是那天下午才收进来的那些——收的时候还对施主千恩万谢、祝福连连；其中几个女人的面孔，在街上早看熟了，是些没完没了哀告一个铜板的乞丐，说是“好救一家人不挨饿”。此刻，她们的哀告也好，放肆的哄笑也好，一齐哑了。她们阴沉而畏怯地屈服着，坐在那里，显然知道等着自己的是什么。敞开的门口刚一露出制服，人群里的几个——多半是有案底的客人——便扭过头去，躲避警官搜索的目光；另有几个不惯见这种场面的，则挑衅地瞪着眼。
===Original===
A single stride took the sergeant into the middle of the room, and with a swinging blow of his club he knocked the faucet out of the keg and the half-filled can from the boss hag’s hand. As the contents of both splashed upon the floor, half a dozen of the group made a sudden dash, and with shoulders humped above their heads to shield their skulls against the dreaded locust broke for the door. They had not counted upon the policemen outside. There was a brief struggle, two or three heavy thumps, and the runaways were brought back to where their comrades crouched in dogged silence.

===Chinese===
警佐一步跨到屋子中央，抡圆警棍一记横扫，敲掉了啤酒桶的桶塞，也打飞了老妖婆手里那只灌了一半的罐头盒。桶里罐里的东西泼洒在地板上，人群中的半打子人猛然发难，肩膀高高耸过头顶护住脑袋，防着那令人胆寒的洋槐木警棍（locust），直奔门口冲去。他们没料到外面还有警察。短暂地扭打了一阵，挨了两三记重拳，逃跑的人被押了回来，回到同伴们中间——那些人正死一般沉默地缩着。

===Original===
“Thirteen!” called the sergeant, completing his survey. “Take them out. ‘Revolvers’ all but one. Good for six months on the island, the whole lot.” The exception was a young man not much if any over twenty, with a hard look of dissipation on his face. He seemed less unconcerned than the rest, but tried hard to make up for it by putting on the boldest air he could. “Come down early,” commented the officer, shoving him along with his stick. “There is need of it. They don’t last long at this. That stuff is brewed to kill at long range.”

===Chinese===
“十三个！”警佐清点完毕，喊道。“带出去。除了一个，全是‘回炉客’（“Revolvers”，警察行话，指屡进屡出监狱的惯犯）。整批人都够在岛上（布莱克韦尔岛，Blackwell's Island）蹲六个月。”那个例外是个年轻人，二十岁出头顶了天，脸上带着纵欲沉溺的冷硬相。他看上去不如其余人满不在乎，却竭力摆出他所能装出的最大胆的神气来弥补。“沦落得早啊，”警官用警棍捅着他往前走，点评道。“正用得着。干这一行的都撑不长。那玩意儿酿出来，就是要慢慢把人杀掉的。”

===Original===
At the head of the cellar-steps we encountered a similar procession from farther back in the alley, where still another was forming to take up its march to the station. Out in the street was heard the tramp of the hosts already pursuing that well-trodden path, as with a fresh complement of men we entered the next stale-beer alley. There were four dives in one cellar here. The filth and the stench were utterly unbearable; even the sergeant turned his back and fled after scattering the crowd with his club and starting them toward the door. The very dog in the alley preferred the cold flags for a berth to the stifling cellar. We found it lying outside. Seventy-five tramps, male and female, were arrested in the four small rooms. In one of them, where the air seemed thick enough to cut with a knife, we found a woman, a mother with a newborn babe on a heap of dirty straw. She was asleep and was left until an ambulance could be called to take her to the hospital.

===Chinese===
在地窖台阶口，我们迎面遇上另一支类似的队伍，来自小巷更深处；那边还有一支正在集合，就要开拔去警察局。街上传来大队人马的脚步声，他们已经在走那条踩熟了的路。这时我们带着新补来的一队警力，走进下一条坏啤酒小巷。这里一个地窖里开着四家啤酒窟。污秽和恶臭简直无法忍受；警佐用警棍把人群驱散、赶向门口之后，自己也背转身逃了出来。连巷子里的那条狗，都宁肯把冰冷的石板当铺位，也不进那闷死人的地窖。我们发现它趴在外面。四个小房间里一共逮捕了七十五名男女流浪汉。在其中一间——那里的空气浓稠得仿佛可以用刀切——我们发现一个女人，一个母亲，怀抱着新生的婴儿，躺在一堆脏稻草上。她睡熟了，大家便留下她没动，等叫了救护车再送她去医院。

===Original===
Returning to the station with this batch, we found every window in the building thrown open to the cold October wind, and the men from the sergeant down smoking the strongest cigars that could be obtained by way of disinfecting the place. Two hundred and seventy-five tramps had been jammed into the cells to be arraigned next morning in the police court on the charge of vagrancy, with the certain prospect of six months “on the Island.” Of the sentence at least they were sure. As to the length of the men’s stay the experienced official at the desk was sceptical, it being then within a month of an important election. If tramps have nothing else to call their own they have votes, and votes that are for sale cheap for cash. About election time this gives them a “pull,” at least by proxy. The sergeant observed, as if it were the most natural thing in the world, that he had more than once seen the same tramp sent to Blackwell’s Island twice in twenty-four hours for six months at a time.

===Chinese===
我们押着这一批人回到警察局，发现楼里每一扇窗都敞开着，任十月的冷风灌进来；从警佐往下，人人抽着能弄到的最冲的雪茄，权当给这地方消毒。二百七十五名流浪汉被塞进牢房，等着第二天早晨在治安法庭以流浪罪过堂，前景笃定是「岛上」六个月。至少刑期这一样，他们是拿得稳的。至于这些人能关多久，柜台后那位老练的官员就不大信了——那时离一场重要的选举不到一个月。流浪汉们哪怕别无长物，总还有选票，而且是肯贱价现钱出卖的选票。临近选举，这就给了他们一点「门路」，至少是可以由人代劳的门路。警佐说起来，就像在说天下再自然不过的事：他不止一次见过同一个流浪汉在二十四小时之内两度被送去布莱克韦尔岛，一次判六个月。
===Original===
As a thief never owns to his calling, however devoid of moral scruples, preferring to style himself a speculator, so this real home-product of the slums, the stale-beer dive, is known about “the Bend” by the more dignified name of the two-cent restaurant. Usually, as in this instance, it is in some cellar giving on a back alley. Doctored, unlicensed beer is its chief ware. Sometimes a cup of “coffee” and a stale roll may be had for two cents. The men pay the score. To the women﻿—unutterable horror of the suggestion﻿—the place is free. The beer is collected from the kegs put on the sidewalk by the saloonkeeper to await the brewer’s cart, and is touched up with drugs to put a froth on it. The privilege to sit all night on a chair, or sleep on a table, or in a barrel, goes with each round of drinks. Generally an Italian, sometimes a negro, occasionally a woman, “runs” the dive. Their customers, alike homeless and hopeless in their utter wretchedness, are the professional tramps, and these only. The meanest thief is infinitely above the stale-beer level. Once upon that plane there is no escape. To sink below it is impossible; no one ever rose from it. One night spent in a stale-beer dive is like the traditional putting on of the uniform of the caste, the discarded rags of an old tramp. That stile once crossed, the lane has no longer a turn; and contrary to the proverb, it is usually not long either.

===Chinese===
正如贼哪怕毫无道德顾忌，也从不认自己的行当，宁可自称投机商一样，这贫民窟土生土长的特产——坏啤酒窟——在「弯道区」一带还有个体面些的名号，叫两分钱饭馆（two-cent restaurant）。它通常——就像眼前这一处——开在某个通向后巷的地窖里。掺了假的无照啤酒是它的主要货色。有时两分钱还能买到一杯「咖啡」加一个隔夜的陈面包卷。男人付账。对女人——这层意思里有说不出的恐怖——这地方分文不取。啤酒是从各酒馆老板摆在人行道上、等酿酒厂货车来收的酒桶里汇集来的，再加药物点化，让它起一层泡沫。每喝一轮，便附送一项特权：在椅子上坐一整夜，或者睡在桌子上、睡在木桶里。经营啤酒窟的通常是意大利人，有时是个黑人，偶尔是个女人。他们的主顾，在彻底的悲惨中同样无家可归、同样无望，是职业流浪汉，而且仅此一种。最下三滥的贼也远在坏啤酒的水准之上。一旦落到那个层面，就再无逃生之路。往下沉是不可能的；也从来没有人从那里浮起来过。在坏啤酒窟里过一夜，就像照老规矩穿上这个种姓的制服——某个老流浪汉丢弃的破衣烂衫。那道梯蹬一旦跨过，小巷便再无转弯；而且与谚语唱反调的是，这条巷子通常也不长。

===Original===
With the gravitation of the Italian tramp landlord toward the old stronghold of the African on the West Side, a share of the stale-beer traffic has left “the Bend;” but its headquarters will always remain there, the real home of trampdom, just as Fourteenth Street is its limit. No real tramp crosses that frontier after nightfall and in the daytime only to beg. Repulsive as the business is, its profits to the Italian dive-keeper are considerable; in fact, barring a slight outlay in the ingredients that serve to give “life” to the beer-dregs, it is all profit. The “banker” who curses the Italian colony does not despise taking a hand in it, and such a thing as a stale-beer trust on a Mulberry Street scale may yet be among the possibilities. One of these bankers, who was once known to the police as the keeper of one notorious stale-beer dive and the active backer of others, is today an extensive manufacturer of macaroni, the owner of several big tenements and other real estate; and the capital, it is said, has all come out of his old business. Very likely it is true.

===Chinese===
随着专做流浪汉生意的意大利房东们被吸向西区（West Side）「非洲」（Africa，当时对黑人聚居区的代称）的老据点，一部分坏啤酒买卖已经离开了「弯道区」；但它的总部将永远留在那里——流浪界的真正老家，正如第十四街（Fourteenth Street）是它的边界。天黑以后，真正的流浪汉绝不越过那条界线，白天过去也只为行乞。这门营生尽管可憎，给开啤酒窟的意大利人带来的利润却相当可观；事实上，除了在那点能给啤酒渣添「生气」的配料上稍稍破费，全是净赚。祸害意大利人聚居区的「银行家」（“banker”）并不嫌弃插上一手，而桑树街规模的坏啤酒托拉斯（trust），说不定也终有问世的一天。这些银行家中有一位，当年在警察局是挂了号的：当过一家臭名昭著的坏啤酒窟的老板，还积极资助着别家；如今已是大宗通心粉制造商，好几座大廉租公寓和其他产业的主人；据说那笔资本，全是从他的老营生里滚出来的。多半确有其事。

===Original===
On hot summer nights it is no rare experience when exploring the worst of the tenements in “the Bend” to find the hallways occupied by rows of “sitters,” tramps whom laziness or hard luck has prevented from earning enough by their day’s “labor” to pay the admission fee to a stale-beer dive, and who have their reasons for declining the hospitality of the police station lodging-rooms. Huddled together in loathsome files, they squat there over night, or until an inquisitive policeman breaks up the congregation with his club, which in Mulberry Street has always free swing. At that season the woman tramp predominates. The men, some of them at least, take to the railroad track and to camping out when the nights grow warm, returning in the fall to prey on the city and to recruit their ranks from the lazy, the shiftless, and the unfortunate. Like a foul loadstone, “the Bend” attracts and brings them back, no matter how far they have wandered. For next to idleness the tramp loves rum; next to rum stale beer, its equivalent of the gutter. And the first and last go best together.

===Chinese===
盛夏的夜晚，到「弯道区」最糟糕的几座廉租公寓里去探查，撞见走廊里坐满一排排「坐客」（“sitters”）并不稀奇——这些流浪汉，或因懒惰，或因运气太坏，一天「劳作」下来挣不够付坏啤酒窟门票的钱，而又自有苦衷，不肯领受警察局寄宿室的那份殷勤。他们挤作一堆，排成令人作呕的行列，在那里蹲上一夜，直到哪个多事的警察挥棍驱散这场聚会——在桑树街，警棍向来是挥得自由的。那个季节，女流浪汉占了上风。男人们——至少其中一部分——趁夜色转暖，跑到铁路沿线露营去了，到秋天再回来靠这座城市过活，并从懒汉、没出息的人和倒霉蛋中间补充自己的队伍。像一块污浊的磁石，「弯道区」把他们吸引回来，任他们漂泊多远也逃不脱。因为仅次于懒惰，流浪汉最爱朗姆酒；仅次于朗姆酒，就是坏啤酒——阴沟里的等价物。而头一样和末一样，凑在一起最是相宜。

===Original===
As “sitters” they occasionally find a job in the saloons about Chatham and Pearl Streets on cold winter nights, when the hallway is not practicable, that enables them to pick up a charity drink now and then and a bite of an infrequent sandwich. The barkeeper permits them to sit about the stove and by shivering invite the sympathy of transient customers. The dodge works well, especially about Christmas and election time, and the sitters are able to keep comfortably filled up to the advantage of their host. But to look thoroughly miserable they must keep awake. A tramp placidly dozing at the fire would not be an object of sympathy. To make sure that they do keep awake, the wily bartender makes them sit constantly swinging one foot like the pendulum of a clock. When it stops the slothful “sitter” is roused with a kick and “fired out.” It is said by those who profess to know that habit has come to the rescue of oversleepy tramps and that the old rounders can swing hand or foot in their sleep without betraying themselves. In some saloons “sitters” are let in at these seasons in fresh batches every hour.

===Chinese===
寒冬的夜晚，走廊里蹲不住人了，「坐客」们偶尔还能在查塔姆街（Chatham Street）和珍珠街（Pearl Street）一带的酒馆里寻到一份差事，让他们隔三差五蹭到一杯慈善酒，间或还能咬上一口难得一遇的三明治。酒保容许他们围坐在火炉四周，靠瑟瑟发抖去博过路客人的怜悯。这花招很灵，尤其在圣诞节前后和选举时节；「坐客」们得以舒舒服服地灌满自己，便宜了他们的东家。可是要显得够凄惨，就得保持清醒。一个在炉火边安然打盹的流浪汉，是不会成为怜悯的对象的。为确使他们果真醒着，狡黠的酒保让他们坐着时像钟摆一样不停地晃一只脚。脚一停，这个犯懒的「坐客」就被一脚踢醒、“轰”出门去。据自诩内行的人说，习惯帮了瞌睡过头的流浪汉们的忙：那些老油子睡梦里也能晃手晃脚而不露马脚。有些酒馆在这种时节，每逢钟点就放进来新的一批「坐客」。
===Original===
On one of my visits to “the Bend” I came across a particularly ragged and disreputable tramp, who sat smoking his pipe on the rung of a ladder with such evident philosophic contentment in the busy labor of a score of ragpickers all about him, that I bade him sit for a picture, offering him ten cents for the job. He accepted the offer with hardly a nod, and sat patiently watching me from his perch until I got ready for work. Then he took the pipe out of his mouth and put it in his pocket, calmly declaring that it was not included in the contract, and that it was worth a quarter to have it go in the picture. The pipe, by the way, was of clay, and of the two-for-a-cent kind. But I had to give in. The man, scarce ten seconds employed at honest labor, even at sitting down, at which he was an undoubted expert, had gone on strike. He knew his rights and the value of “work,” and was not to be cheated out of either.

===Chinese===
我有一回走访「弯道区」，碰到一个格外褴褛、格外不像话的流浪汉，坐在一架梯子的横档上抽着烟斗；他四周二十来个拾荒者正忙着干活，他却显出一副哲学家式的怡然自得。我便请他坐着让我拍张照片，出十美分雇他干这桩活。他几乎连头也懒得点一下便应承了，从他的高座上耐心地瞅着我做准备。等我要动手了，他却把烟斗从嘴里拿出来，揣进了口袋，还心平气和地声明：烟斗不在合同之内，要让它上相，得另加二十五美分。那支烟斗不过是陶土烧的，一分钱两支的那种货色。可我只好让步。这个人，干诚实的营生——哪怕是坐着干活这一样，他无疑是行家——还不满十秒钟，就罢工了。他清楚自己的权利，也清楚「劳动」的价值，两样都不容人骗了去。

===Original===
Whence these tramps, and why the tramping? are questions oftener asked than answered. Ill-applied charity and idleness answer the first query. They are the whence, and to a large extent the why also. Once started on the career of a tramp, the man keeps to it because it is the laziest. Tramps and toughs profess the same doctrine, that the world owes them a living, but from standpoints that tend in different directions. The tough does not become a tramp, save in rare instances, when old and broken down. Even then usually he is otherwise disposed of. The devil has various ways of taking care of his own. Nor is the tramps’ army recruited from any certain class. All occupations and most grades of society yield to it their contingent of idleness. Occasionally, from one cause or another, a recruit of a better stamp is forced into the ranks; but the first acceptance of alms puts a brand on the able-bodied man which his moral nature rarely hold out to efface. He seldom recovers his lost caste. The evolution is gradual, keeping step with the increasing shabbiness of his clothes and corresponding loss of self-respect, until he reaches the bottom in “the Bend.”

===Chinese===
这些流浪汉打哪里来？又为什么流浪？这两个问题，问的人多，答得上的人少。用错了地方的慈善和懒惰，回答了第一个问题。它们就是来处，在很大程度上也是缘由。一旦踏上流浪汉的生涯，人就一条道走到黑，因为这是最省力的营生。流浪汉和恶棍信奉同一条教义——世界欠他们一口饭吃——立足点却朝着不同的方向。恶棍不会变成流浪汉，除非年老垮掉，而那样的情形也少见。即便到那时，他通常也另有归宿。魔鬼照料自家人，有的是各式法子。流浪汉大军也不是从哪个固定阶层征募来的。各行各业和社会的大多数等级，都向它缴出自己那份懒散的名额。偶尔，由于这样那样的缘故，也有一个成色稍好的新兵被逼进队伍；可是头一回接受施舍，就在一个身强力壮的人身上打下了烙印，他的道德天性极少撑得住把它磨掉。他很难再赢回失去的身份。这场蜕变是渐进的，同他衣衫日见褴褛、自尊随之流失合着步子，直到他在「弯道区」落到了底。

===Original===
Of the tough the tramp doctrine that the world owes him a living makes a thief; of the tramp a coward. Numbers only make him bold unless he has to do with defenceless women. In the city the policemen keep him straight enough. The women rob an occasional clothesline when no one is looking, or steal the pail and scrubbing-brush with which they are set to clean up in the station-house lodging-rooms after their night’s sleep. At the police station the roads of the tramp and the tough again converge. In midwinter, on the coldest nights, the sanitary police corral the tramps here and in their lodging-houses and vaccinate them, despite their struggles and many oaths that they have recently been “scraped.” The station-house is the sieve that sifts out the chaff from the wheat, if there be any wheat there. A man goes from his first night’s sleep on the hard slab of a police station lodging-room to a deckhand’s berth on an outgoing steamer, to the recruiting office, to any work that is honest, or he goes “to the devil or the dives, same thing,” says my friend, the Sergeant, who knows.

===Chinese===
「世界欠他一口饭吃」这条流浪汉信条，落在恶棍身上，造出的是贼；落在流浪汉身上，造出的是懦夫。只有人多才壮得起他的胆，除非他打交道的是毫无防护的女人。在城里，警察足以叫他循规蹈矩。女人们趁没人看见，偶尔偷一回晾衣绳上的衣物，或者干脆偷走那副水桶和板刷——那是她们在警察局寄宿室里睡过一夜之后，被派来打扫用的。在警察局，流浪汉和恶棍的路又汇到一处。隆冬最冷的夜里，卫生警察把流浪汉们在这里、在他们的寄宿屋里圈拢起来，给他们种牛痘，任凭他们挣扎，任凭他们赌咒发誓，说自己新近才「刮过」（“scraped”，即种过痘）。警察分局是一面筛子，筛去糠秕，留下麦粒——倘若那里头当真有麦粒的话。一个人从警察局寄宿室硬石板上的头一夜睡起，或是到出港的轮船上占一个甲板水手的铺位，或是走进募兵站，或是去干任何诚实的营生；再不然，就照我那位深谙此道的警佐朋友所说，“下地狱，或者进啤酒窟——反正是一回事”。

## VIII The Cheap Lodging-Houses / VIII 廉价寄宿所

===Original===
When it comes to the question of numbers with this tramps’ army, another factor of serious portent has to be taken into account: the cheap lodging-houses. In the caravansaries that line Chatham Street and the Bowery, harboring nightly a population as large as that of many a thriving town, a homemade article of tramp and thief is turned out that is attracting the increasing attention of the police, and offers a field for the missionary’s labors beside which most others seem of slight account. Within a year they have been stamped as nurseries of crime by the chief of the Secret Police,10 the sort of crime that feeds especially on idleness and lies ready to the hand of fatal opportunity. In the same strain one of the justices on the police court bench sums up his long experience as a committing magistrate: “The ten-cent lodging-houses more than counterbalance the good done by the free reading-room, lectures, and all other agencies of reform. Such lodging-houses have caused more destitution, more beggary and crime than any other agency I know of.” A very slight acquaintance with the subject is sufficient to convince the observer that neither authority overstates the fact. The two officials had reference, however, to two different grades of lodging-houses. The cost of a night’s lodging makes the difference. There is a wider gap between the “hotel”﻿—they are all hotels﻿—that charges a quarter and the one that furnishes a bed for a dime than between the bridal suite and the everyday hall bedroom of the ordinary hostelry.

===Chinese===
说起这支流浪汉大军的数目问题，还有一个兆头不祥的重要因素必须计入：廉价寄宿所（cheap lodging-house）。沿查塔姆街（Chatham Street）和鲍厄里（the Bowery）一字排开的这一家家大客栈，每夜收容的人口抵得上一座兴旺市镇，里面源源出产一种本地土造的流浪汉和窃贼，正日益引来警方愈来愈多的注意；在它们为传教士提供的这片劳作园地面前，大多数别的园地都显得无足轻重。一年之内，秘密警察（Secret Police）的首脑（10）已给它们盖上了「罪恶温床」的印记——那种尤其以懒惰为养料、一遇到致命的机会便唾手可得的罪恶。同一种腔调，治安法庭法官席上的一位法官这样总结他作为羁押法官的漫长经历：“十分钱寄宿所造下的恶，超过了免费阅览室、讲座以及一切其他改良机构行善的总和。这类寄宿所造成的贫困、行乞和罪恶，比我所知道的任何别的机构都多。”对这个问题稍作涉猎，便足以使观察者相信：两位权威谁也没有夸大事实。不过，两位官员所指的，是两个不同等级的寄宿所。差别就在一夜铺位的价钱上。收二十五分钱的那家「旅馆」——它们全都自称旅馆——和出十分钱便给一张床的那家之间，隔着的鸿沟，比普通客店的新婚套房与日常的走廊小单间之间的还要宽。

===Original===
The metropolis is to lots of people like a lighted candle to the moth. It attracts them in swarms that come year after year with the vague idea that they can get along here if anywhere; that something is bound to turn up among so many. Nearly all are young men, unsettled in life, many﻿—most of them, perhaps﻿—fresh from good homes, beyond a doubt with honest hopes of getting a start in the city and making a way for themselves. Few of them have much money to waste while looking around, and the cheapness of the lodging offered is an object. Fewer still know anything about the city and its pitfalls.
