Every fresh persecution of the Russian or Polish Jew on his native soil starts greater hordes hitherward to confound economical problems, and recruit the sweater’s phalanx. The curse of bigotry and ignorance reaches halfway across the world, to sow its bitter seed in fertile soil in the East Side tenements. If the Jew himself was to blame for the resentment he aroused over there, he is amply punished. He gathers the firstfruits of the harvest here.

===Chinese===
包工头心里明白：工人孤立无援、愚昧无知，这正是他稳当的根基；于是凡是他做得到的，他都做了——凡是狠得下心的地方，绝不容情——务必要把他这些奴隶身上每一丝智力苏醒的迹象扼杀在萌芽里。在他要把这份专制传之长久的事业里，实效卓著地帮了他忙的，有他自己那套把工人钉死在饥饿工资上的制度和惨烈的竞争；有这些人骨子里的贪婪——哪怕再微薄的眼前便宜，也不肯拿来换取长久的好处；而最主要的，是那一批一批饥饿的移民，对他们讲什么道理都不中用，只有讨面包的哭喊听得进去。不过近年以来，他到底被迫作了局部的投降：工人们在相当程度上组织进了工会，又有明智领导之下的合作经营试验，都预示着包工头的末日。可是只要无知的人潮继续涌来、继续成群挤在这些廉租公寓里，他的钳制就永远挣不脱。而大洋彼岸的这种货源，看上去是取之不竭的。俄国或波兰的犹太人在本土每遭到一场新的迫害，便有更大批的人马启程来此，把经济问题搅得更加难解，也给包工头的方阵添补兵员。偏执与愚昧的诅咒，远达半个地球之外，把它的苦种撒进下东区廉租公寓这片沃土。倘若犹太人当年在那边招来的怨恨要怪他自己，那么眼下的惩罚已经够他受了——他正在这里收取那茬庄稼的头一批果子。

===Original===
The bulk of the sweater’s work is done in the tenements, which the law that regulates factory labor does not reach. To the factories themselves that are taking the place of the rear tenements in rapidly growing numbers, letting in bigger day-crowds than those the health officers banished, the tenement shops serve as a supplement through which the law is successfully evaded. Ten hours is the legal workday in the factories, and nine o’clock the closing hour at the latest. Forty-five minutes at least must be allowed for dinner, and children under sixteen must not be employed unless they can read and write English; none at all under fourteen. The very fact that such a law should stand on the statute book, shows how desperate the plight of these people. But the tenement has defeated its benevolent purpose. In it the child works unchallenged from the day he is old enough to pull a thread. There is no such thing as a dinner hour; men and women eat while they work, and the “day” is lengthened at both ends far into the night. Factory hands take their work with them at the close of the lawful day to eke out their scanty earnings by working overtime at home. Little chance on this ground for the campaign of education that alone can bring the needed relief; small wonder that there are whole settlements on this East Side where English is practically an unknown tongue, though the people be both willing and anxious to learn. “When shall we find time to learn?” asked one of them of me once. I owe him the answer yet.

===Chinese===
包工头的活计，绝大部分是在廉租公寓里做出来的，而管制工厂劳工的法律伸不进那里。正规工厂正以飞快的速度成批顶替后院廉租公寓（rear tenement），白天放进厂门的人潮，比当年卫生官员们从后院公寓里撵走的还要庞大；对这些工厂，公寓作坊成了一种补充，法律便经由它被顺顺当当地规避过去。工厂的法定工作日是十个钟头，最晚九点收工。用饭时间至少得给足四十五分钟；十六岁以下的儿童，非既识英文又能写英文不得雇用，十四岁以下则一概不得雇用。这样一条法律居然会写进法典，这件事本身便显出这些人的境况已何等绝望。可是廉租公寓挫败了它的善意。在公寓里，孩子从能把一根线拉直的那天起便做起工来，无人过问。用饭钟点是根本谈不上的；男男女女边干活边吃饭，「工作日」的两头一齐拉长，深深入夜。工厂工人法定的一天收工之后，还要把活计带回家去，靠在家加班熬点外快，贴补菲薄的工钱。在这样一块地面上，那唯一能带来真正解救的教育运动，几乎没有立足的余地；怪不得下东区整片整片的居民区里，英语实际上成了一门无人通晓的语言，尽管这里的人既肯学、又巴望学。“我们哪有工夫学呢？”他们中间有一个人曾经这样问我。这笔答案的债，我至今还欠着他。

===Original===
Take the Second Avenue Elevated Railroad at Chatham Square and ride up half a mile through the sweaters’ district. Every open window of the big tenements, that stand like a continuous brick wall on both sides of the way, gives you a glimpse of one of these shops as the train speeds by. Men and women bending over their machines, or ironing clothes at the window, half-naked. Proprieties do not count on the East Side; nothing counts that cannot be converted into hard cash. The road is like a big gangway through an endless workroom where vast multitudes are forever laboring. Morning, noon, or night, it makes no difference; the scene is always the same. At Rivington Street let us get off and continue our trip on foot. It is Sunday evening west of the Bowery. Here, under the rule of Mosaic law, the week of work is under full headway, its first day far spent. The hucksters’ wagons are absent or stand idle at the curb; the saloons admit the thirsty crowds through the side-door labelled “Family Entrance;” a tin sign in a store-window announces that a “Sunday School” gathers in stray children of the new dispensation; but beyond these things there is little to suggest the Christian Sabbath. Men stagger along the sidewalk groaning under heavy burdens of unsewn garments, or enormous black bags stuffed full of finished coats and trousers. Let us follow one to his home and see how Sunday passes in a Ludlow Street tenement.

===Chinese===
在查塔姆广场（Chatham Square）登上第二大道高架铁路（Second Avenue Elevated Railroad），往北坐上半英里，一路穿行包工头们的地界。铁道两旁，高大的廉租公寓像一堵连绵不断的砖墙矗立着；列车飞驰而过时，每一扇敞开的窗口都让你瞥见这样一间作坊：男男女女俯在缝纫机上，或是半裸着身子在窗前熨衣裳。体统在下东区不值钱；凡是变不成响当当现钱的东西，一概不值钱。这条铁道就像一条穿过无边大工场的中央走道，黑压压的人群在工场里永远劳作不歇。早晨、中午、黑夜，全无分别；景象总是一个模样。到里文顿街（Rivington Street）我们下车，步行把游历继续下去。此刻，鲍厄里以西正是星期日傍晚。而在这里，摩西律法统治之下，一周的劳作正开足马力，头一天已经过去大半。小贩的货车不见踪影，或是空歇在路边石旁；酒馆从那扇标着「家庭入口」的边门放进口渴的人群；一家店铺橱窗里的白铁招牌昭告说，有一所「主日学校」专门收拢这个新约时代流落街头的孩童；但除了这些，几乎没有什么叫人想起基督教的安息日。男人们沿着人行道踉跄而行，被一捆捆未缝的衣裳压得直哼哼，或是扛着塞满成品上衣和裤子的黑色大口袋。让我们跟着其中一个回家，看看勒德洛街（Ludlow Street）的廉租公寓里，星期日是怎样打发的。

===Original===
Up two flights of dark stairs, three, four, with new smells of cabbage, of onions, of frying fish, on every landing, whirring sewing machines behind closed doors betraying what goes on within, to the door that opens to admit the bundle and the man. A sweater, this, in a small way. Five men and a woman, two young girls, not fifteen, and a boy who says unasked that he is fifteen, and lies in saying it, are at the machines sewing knickerbockers, “knee-pants” in the Ludlow Street dialect. The floor is littered ankle-deep with half-sewn garments. In the alcove, on a couch of many dozens of “pants” ready for the finisher, a bare-legged baby with pinched face is asleep. A fence of piled-up clothing keeps him from rolling off on the floor. The faces, hands, and arms to the elbows of everyone in the room are black with the color of the cloth on which they are working. The boy and the woman alone look up at our entrance. The girls shoot sidelong glances, but at a warning look from the man with the bundle they tread their machines more energetically than ever. The men do not appear to be aware even of the presence of a stranger.

===Chinese===
沿黑洞洞的楼梯上去两层、三层、四层，每到一个楼梯平台，都涌来一股新的气味——卷心菜味、洋葱味、煎鱼味；关严的门后缝纫机嗡嗡作响，把门里正干着什么招认了出来；一直走到那扇门跟前——门开了，迎进包袱和那个男人。这是一位小本经营的包工头。屋里五个男人和一个女人、两个不满十五岁的年轻姑娘，还有一个不等人问就自称十五岁的男孩——说的时候还在撒谎——都在机器上缝灯笼裤（knickerbockers），用勒德洛街的方言说，叫「男童短裤（knee-pants）」。地板上缝了一半的衣裳撂得没了脚踝。壁龛里，几十打候交拷边工的「裤子」堆成一张小榻，一个光着腿、小脸瘦削的婴孩正睡在上面。一圈码起来的衣裳权当围栏，拦着他不从榻上滚到地板上。屋里每个人的脸、双手和直到臂肘的双臂，都叫他们手头那料子的颜色染得乌黑。我们进门时，只有男孩和那女人抬头望了一眼。姑娘们斜着眼瞟，可扛包袱的男人一递眼色，她们踩机器的劲头登时比先前更足。那几个男人呢，仿佛连场子里多了个生人都觉察不到。

===Original===
They are “learners,” all of them, says the woman, who proves to be the wife of the boss, and have “come over” only a few weeks ago. She is disinclined to talk at first, but a few words in her own tongue from our guide15 set her fears, whatever they are, at rest, and she grows almost talkative. The learners work for week’s wages, she says. How much do they earn? She shrugs her shoulders with an expressive gesture. The workers themselves, asked in their own tongue, say indifferently, as though the question were of no interest: from two to five dollars. The children﻿—there are four of them﻿—are not old enough to work. The oldest is only six. They turn out one hundred and twenty dozen “knee-pants” a week, for which the manufacturer pays seventy cents a dozen. Five cents a dozen is the clear profit, but her own and her husband’s work brings the family earnings up to twenty-five dollars a week, when they have work all the time. But often half the time is put in looking for it. They work no longer than to nine o’clock at night, from daybreak. There are ten machines in the room; six are hired at two dollars a month. For the two shabby, smoke-begrimed rooms, one somewhat larger than ordinary, they pay twenty dollars a month. She does not complain, though “times are not what they were, and it costs a good deal to live.” Eight dollars a week for the family of six and two boarders. How do they do it? She laughs, as she goes over the bill of fare, at the silly question: Bread, fifteen cents a day, of milk two quarts a day at four cents a quart, one pound of meat for dinner at twelve cents, butter one pound a week at “eight cents a quarter of a pound.” Coffee, potatoes, and pickles complete the list. At the least calculation, probably, this sweater’s family hoards up thirty dollars a month, and in a few years will own a tenement somewhere and profit by the example set by their landlord in rent-collecting. It is the way the savings of Jewtown are universally invested, and with the natural talent of its people for commercial speculation the investment is enormously profitable.

===Chinese===
“都是学手艺的，”那女人说——原来她是老板娘——他们几个星期前才“过来”。起先她不大肯开口，可我们的向导（15）用她本族的话讲了几句，她那不知打哪儿来的疑惧便放下了，人也变得几乎健谈起来。学手艺的按周薪做活，她说。他们挣多少？她耸了耸肩膀，那姿势再达意不过。工人们本人，用本族话被问到时，淡淡地答一声，仿佛这问题同自己毫不相干：两块到五块。孩子们——共有四个——还不到做工的年纪，顶大的才六岁。他们一星期做出一百二十打「男童短裤」，制造商每打付七毛钱。每打五分钱是净利，再加上她自己和她丈夫的活计，全家的收入便上了每星期二十五块——只要有活不断的话。可常常有一半工夫是花在找活上的。他们从天亮做起，至多做到夜里九点。屋里共有十台机器，六台是按每月两块钱租的。这两间烟熏火燎的破屋——一间比寻常的稍大些——月租二十块。她并不诉苦，虽说“世道不比从前，过日子花销大”。一家六口，外加两个搭伙房客，一星期吃用八块钱。这日子是怎么过的？她报着食谱，被这个傻问题逗笑了：面包每天一毛五；牛奶每天两夸脱，一夸脱四分钱；正餐的肉每天一磅，一毛二；黄油每星期一磅，按“四分之一磅八分钱”的价钱；咖啡、土豆、腌菜，单子到此为止。往最少里算，这一家包工头每月怕也要攒下三十块，再过几年，就会在什么地方置下一座廉租公寓，效法他们房东收租的榜样生利去了。「犹太镇」的积蓄向来一概是这样投资的，凭着这地方的人做买卖投机的天生本事，这笔投资获利极丰。

===Original===
On the next floor, in a dimly lighted room with a big red-hot stove to keep the pressing irons ready for use, is a family of man, wife, three children, and a boarder. “Knee-pants” are made there too, of a still lower grade. Three cents and a half is all he clears, says the man, and lies probably out of at least two cents. The wife makes a dollar and a half finishing, the man about nine dollars at the machine. The boarder pays sixty-five cents a week. He is really only a lodger, getting his meals outside. The rent is two dollars and twenty-five cents a week, cost of living five dollars. Every floor has at least two, sometimes four, such shops. Here is one with a young family for which life is bright with promise. Husband and wife work together; just now the latter, a comely young woman, is eating her dinner of dry bread and green pickles. Pickles are favorite food in Jewtown. They are filling, and keep the children from crying with hunger. Those who have stomachs like ostriches thrive in spite of them and grow strong﻿—plain proof that they are good to eat. The rest? “Well, they die,” says our guide, dryly. No thought of untimely death comes to disturb this family with life all before it. In a few years the man will be a prosperous sweater. Already he employs an old man as ironer at three dollars a week, and a sweet-faced little Italian girl as finisher at a dollar and a half. She is twelve, she says, and can neither read nor write; will probably never learn. How should she? The family clears from ten to eleven dollars a week in brisk times, more than half of which goes into the bank.

===Chinese===
再上一层楼，一间昏暗的屋里烧着一只通红的大火炉，好让熨斗随取随用；屋里是一户人家：男人、妻子、三个孩子，外加一个搭伙的房客。这里也做「男童短裤」，档次还要更低一等。他每打只净落三分半，男人说——这话里八成至少瞒下了两分钱。妻子做拷边挣一块半，男人踩机器约莫挣九块。房客每星期交六毛五。他其实不过是个搭铺客，饭在外面吃。房租每星期两块二毛五，吃用开销五块。每层楼至少有两间这样的作坊，有时有四间。这一间里是一户年轻人家，日子正被前程照得亮堂。夫妻俩一同做活；这会儿，那位模样周正的年轻媳妇正吃着她的午饭：干面包加青腌菜。腌菜是「犹太镇」顶受欢迎的吃食。它顶饱，又免得孩子们饿得直哭。凡长着鸵鸟一般胃口的，吃它照样越长越壮——明明白白证明这东西滋养。其余的呢？“唔，死了呗，”我们的向导干巴巴地说。夭亡的念头休想来搅扰这户人家——他们的日子全在前头呢。过不了几年，男人就是个体面发迹的包工头了。如今他已经雇着一个老头当熨烫工，每星期三块；又雇着一个甜甜脸蛋的意大利小姑娘当拷边工，一块半。她说她十二岁，不识字，也不会写，多半永远学不会了。她怎么学呢？旺季里这户人家每星期净挣十块到十一块，一多半都送进了银行。

===Original===
A companion picture from across the hall. The man works on the machine for his sweater twelve hours a day, turning out three dozen “knee-pants,” for which he receives forty-two cents a dozen. The finisher who works with him gets ten, and the ironer eight cents a dozen; buttonholes are extra, at eight to ten cents a hundred. This operator has four children at his home in Stanton Street, none old enough to work, and a sick wife. His rent is twelve dollars a month; his wages for a hard week’s work less than eight dollars. Such as he, with their consuming desire for money thus smothered, recruit the ranks of the anarchists, won over by the promise of a general “divide;” and an enlightened public sentiment turns up its nose at the vicious foreigner for whose perverted notions there is no room in this land of plenty.

===Chinese===
走廊对面，是又一幅图景。这个男人替他的包工头踩机器，一天十二个钟头，做出三打「男童短裤」，每打得四毛二。同他搭档的拷边工每打得一毛，熨烫工每打八分；锁扣眼是外加的，一百个八分到一毛。这个操机工在斯坦顿街（Stanton Street）的家里有四个孩子，个个不到做工的年纪，还有个害病的妻子。他的房租每月十二块；苦苦干满一星期的工钱，不到八块。正是他这样的人，挣钱的欲火被这样活活闷熄之后，听着“大家平分”的许诺，便去补充无政府主义者的队伍；而开明的公论却对这个邪恶的外国人嗤之以鼻——在这片丰饶富足的土地上，可容不下他那套走了样的念头。

===Original===
Turning the corner into Hester Street, we stumble upon a nest of cloak-makers in their busy season. Six months of the year the cloak-maker is idle, or nearly so. Now is his harvest. Seventy-five cents a cloak, all complete, is the price in this shop. The cloak is of cheap plush, and might sell for eight or nine dollars over the store-counter. Seven dollars is the weekly wage of this man with wife and two children, and nine dollars and a half rent to pay per month. A boarder pays about a third of it. There was a time when he made ten dollars a week and thought himself rich. But wages have come down fearfully in the last two years. Think of it: “come down” to this. The other cloak-makers aver that they can make as much as twelve dollars a week, when they are employed, by taking their work home and sewing till midnight. One exhibits his account-book with a Ludlow Street sweater. It shows that he and his partner, working on first-class garments for a Broadway house in the four busiest weeks of the season, made together from $15.15 to $19.20 a week by striving from 6 a.m. to 11 p.m., that is to say, from $7.58 to $9.60 each.16 The sweater on this work probably made as much as fifty percent at least on their labor. Not far away is a factory in a rear yard where the factory inspector reports teams of tailors making men’s coats at an average of twenty-seven cents a coat, all complete except buttons and buttonholes.

===Chinese===
拐进海斯特街（Hester Street），我们一头撞见一窝正赶上旺季的披风工。一年里有六个月，披风工（cloak-maker）闲着，或者差不多闲着。眼下正是他的收成时节。在这家作坊里，一件披风连工带料全部做齐，作价七毛五。披风是廉价长毛绒的，摆上店铺柜台，兴许能卖八块九块。这个人家里有妻子和两个孩子，周薪七块，每月却要付九块半的房租，一个搭伙房客替他摊上约莫三分之一。从前有一阵，他每星期挣十块，自以为发了财。可这两年工钱一落千丈。想想吧：竟“落”到了这步田地。别的披风工一口咬定，只要有活干，把活计带回家去缝到半夜，一星期能挣到十二块。有一位拿出他同一个勒德洛街包工头来往的账本。账上写着：他和他的搭档在旺季最忙的四个星期里，替一家百老汇商号做头等货，从清晨6点拼到夜里11点，两人合计每星期挣 $15.15 到 $19.20——也就是说，每人 $7.58 到 $9.60（16）。包工头在他们这份劳动上抽的头，恐怕至少也有百分之五十。不远处的后院里有一家工厂，据工厂检查员报告，一队队裁缝在那里做男式上衣，平均每件两毛七，纽扣扣眼除外，一应俱全。

===Original===
Turning back, we pass a towering double tenement in Ludlow Street, owned by a well-known Jewish liquor dealer and politician, a triple combination that bodes ill for his tenants. As a matter of fact, the cheapest “apartment,” three rear rooms on the sixth floor, only one of which deserves the name, is rented for $13 a month. Here is a reminder of the Bend, a hallway turned into a shoemaker’s shop. Two hallways side by side in adjoining tenements, would be sinful waste in Jewtown, when one would do as well by knocking a hole in the wall. But this shoemaker knows a trick the Italian’s ingenuity did not suggest. He has his “flat” as well as his shop there. A curtain hung back of his stool in the narrow passage half conceals his bed that fills it entirely from wall to wall. To get into it he has to crawl over the footboard, and he must come out the same way. Expedients more odd than this are born of the East Side crowding. In one of the houses we left, the coal-bin of a family on the fourth floor was on the roof of the adjoining tenement. A quarter of a ton of coal was being dumped there while we talked with the people.

===Chinese===
往回走时，我们经过勒德洛街一座高耸的双联廉租公寓，业主是一位有名的犹太酒商兼政客——这三合一的身份，对他的房客们可不是好兆头。事实是：这里最便宜的「套房」——六楼上的三间后屋，其中配得上屋子之名的只有一间——月租要 $13。这里有一处叫人想起弯道区的景致：一条过道改作了鞋匠铺。毗邻两座公寓若各留一条过道并肩而立，在「犹太镇」便是罪过的浪费，既然在墙上凿一个洞、两家合用一条也一样管用。可这位鞋匠还懂得一个花样，连意大利人的机灵也未曾想到：他不但把铺子安在过道里，连他的「寓所」也一并安在那里。窄过道里，他座凳背后挂着一幅布帘，半掩着一张床；那张床把过道从这面墙到那面墙塞得严严实实。上床，他得从床尾板上爬过去；出来，还得照原样爬出来。比这更古怪的权宜之计，下东区的拥挤还生得出来。在我们方才走过的一座楼里，四楼上一户人家的煤箱，竟安在隔壁公寓的屋顶上。我们同人们攀谈的当儿，四分之一吨煤正往那屋顶上卸。

===Original===
We have reached Broome Street. The hum of industry in this six-story tenement on the corner leaves no doubt of the aspect Sunday wears within it. One flight up, we knock at the nearest door. The grocer, who keeps the store, lives on the “stoop,” the first floor in East Side parlance. In this room a suspender-maker sleeps and works with his family of wife and four children. For a wonder there are no boarders. His wife and eighteen years old daughter share in the work, but the girl’s eyes are giving out from the strain. Three months in the year, when work is very brisk, the family makes by united efforts as high as fourteen and fifteen dollars a week. The other nine months it averages from three to four dollars. The oldest boy, a young man, earns from four to six dollars in an Orchard Street factory, when he has work. The rent is ten dollars a month for the room and a miserable little coop of a bedroom where the old folks sleep. The girl makes her bed on the lounge in the front room; the big boys and the children sleep on the floor. Coal at ten cents a small pail, meat at twelve cents a pound, one and a half pound of butter a week at thirty-six cents, and a quarter of a pound of tea in the same space of time, are items of their housekeeping account as given by the daughter. Milk at four and five cents a quart, “according to quality.” The sanitary authorities know what that means, know how miserably inadequate is the fine of fifty or a hundred dollars for the murder done in cold blood by the wretches who poison the babes of these tenements with the stuff that is half water, or swill. Their defence is that the demand is for “cheap milk.” Scarcely a wonder that this suspender-maker will hardly be able to save up the dot for his daughter, without which she stands no chance of marrying in Jewtown, even with her face that would be pretty had it a healthier tinge.

===Chinese===
我们到了布鲁姆街（Broome Street）。街角这座六层的廉租公寓里，工业的嗡嗡声不绝于耳，里面的星期日是一副什么面貌，已再无可疑。上一层楼，我们敲响离楼梯口最近的一扇门。开铺子的杂货商就住在「门阶」（stoop）层——按下东区的说法，就是底层。这间屋里，一个做吊裤带的工人带着妻子和四个孩子又睡又做活。难得的是，屋里没有搭伙房客。他的妻子和十八岁的女儿帮着干活，可姑娘的眼睛快熬坏了。一年里有三个月活计极旺，全家合力，一星期能挣到十四五块；其余九个月，平均只有三四块。大儿子已经是个青年，在奥查德街（Orchard Street）一家工厂做工，有活干的时候挣四块到六块。房租每月十块，租的是这间屋，外加一间老两口睡的、鸽子笼似的破卧室。姑娘的铺就搭在前屋的长沙发上；大男孩们和孩子们睡地板。煤一小桶一毛钱，肉一磅一毛二，黄油每星期一磅半、三毛六，同期再买四分之一磅茶叶——这些都是女儿报出来的持家账目。牛奶一夸脱四分五分，“看货论价”。卫生当局明白这是什么意思，也明白对那班用一半是水、一半是泔水的玩意儿毒害这些公寓婴孩的恶棍所犯下的冷血谋杀，五十块一百块的罚金是多么可怜、多么不抵罪。他们的辩词是：市场要的就是「便宜牛奶」。怪不得这位做吊裤带的工人多半攒不出女儿的嫁妆；没有嫁妆，她便休想在「犹太镇」嫁人，哪怕她那张脸只要多几分健康的血色，本可以相当俊俏。

===Original===
Up under the roof three men are making boys’ jackets at twenty cents a piece, of which the sewer takes eight, the ironer three, the finisher five cents, and the buttonhole-maker two and a quarter, leaving a cent and three-quarters to pay for the drumming up, the fetching and bringing back of the goods. They bunk together in a room for which they pay eight dollars a month. All three are single here, that is: their wives are on the other side yet, waiting for them to earn enough to send for them. Their breakfast, eaten at the workbench, consists of a couple of rolls at a cent a piece, and a draught of water, milk when business has been very good, a square meal at noon in a restaurant, and the morning meal over again at night. This square meal, that is the evidence of a very liberal disposition on the part of the consumer, is an affair of more than ordinary note; it may be justly called an institution. I know of a couple of restaurants at the lower end of Orchard Street that are favorite resorts for the Polish Jews, who remember the injunction that the ox that treadeth out the corn shall not be muzzled. Being neighbors, they are rivals of course, and cutting under. When I was last there one gave a dinner of soup, meat-stew, bread, pie, pickles, and a “schooner” of beer for thirteen cents; the other charged fifteen cents for a similar dinner, but with two schooners of beer and a cigar, or a cigarette, as the extra inducement. The two cents had won the day, however, and the thirteen-cent restaurant did such a thriving business that it was about to spread out into the adjoining store to accommodate the crowds of customers. At this rate the lodger of Jewtown can “live like a lord,” as he says himself, for twenty-five cents a day, including the price of his bed, that ranges all the way from thirty to forty and fifty cents a week, and save money, no matter what his earnings. He does it, too, so long as work is to be had at any price, and by the standard he sets up Jewtown must abide.

===Chinese===
屋顶底下，三个男人在做男童上衣，每件两毛钱，其中缝纫工（sewer）拿八分，熨烫工三分，拷边工五分，锁扣眼的两分二厘五，剩下一分七厘五，算作兜揽活计、来回取送货物的花销。他们合睡一间屋，月租八块。三个人在这里都算单身，也就是说：老婆还在大洋那一头，只等他们挣够了钱寄去接来。他们的早饭在工作台上吃：两个小面包卷，一分钱一个，再灌一口凉水——生意特别好的时候，换成牛奶；中午在馆子里吃一顿正餐，夜里再把早晨那顿重演一遍。这顿正餐，作为食客天性慷慨大度的凭证，是桩非同小可的事，说它是本地一项制度也不为过。我知道奥查德街靠下的一头有两家馆子，是波兰犹太人爱去的去处——他们记得那条古训：牛在场上踹谷的时候，不可笼住它的嘴。两家既是近邻，自然就是冤家，彼此杀价。我上次去的时候，一家开出的晚餐是一毛三：汤、炖肉、面包、馅饼、腌菜，外加一大杯啤酒（schooner）；另一家同样的晚餐收一毛五，可是奉送两大杯啤酒，外加一支雪茄或纸烟，作为额外的诱饵。然而还是那两分钱占了上风：一毛三的馆子生意兴隆得很，眼看要扩进隔壁店面，好容下成群的食客。照这样的吃法，「犹太镇」的搭铺客照他自己的话说，一天两毛五就能“活得像个老爷”，床钱也算在内——床钱每星期从三毛、四毛到五毛高低不等——而且不管挣多少，总还能攒下钱。他也真办得到，只要肯出什么价都有活可做；而按他立下的这个标准，「犹太镇」也非这样活下去不可。

===Original===
It has thousands upon thousands of lodgers who help to pay its extortionate rents. At night there is scarce a room in all the district that has not one or more of them, some above half a score, sleeping on cots, or on the floor. It is idle to speak of privacy in these “homes.” The term carries no more meaning with it than would a lecture on social ethics to an audience of Hottentots. The picture is not overdrawn. In fact, in presenting the home life of these people I have been at some pains to avoid the extreme of privation, taking the cases just as they came to hand on the safer middle-ground of average earnings. Yet even the direst apparent poverty in Jewtown, unless dependent on absolute lack of work, would, were the truth known, in nine cases out of ten have a silver lining in the shape of a margin in bank.

===Chinese===
这里有着成千上万的搭铺客，替它凑付那敲骨吸髓的房租。到了夜里，全区几乎找不出一间屋里没有一位或几位这样的客人，有的多达十个开外，睡在折叠小床上，或者干脆睡地板。在这些「家」里谈什么私居独处，纯属白费口舌。这个词到了这里，含义不比给一厅霍屯督人（Hottentots）讲社会伦理学多出多少。这幅图画并没有画过头。事实上，在呈现这些人的家庭生活时，我还颇费了一番心思避开赤贫的极端，专拣平均收入这条较稳妥的中间地带，把手头碰到的情形原样录下。然而即便是「犹太镇」表面最骇人的贫穷，除非真到了活计断绝的地步，一旦真相大白，十有八九都镶着一道银边——银行里一笔存款的形状。

===Original===
These are the economical conditions that enable my manufacturing friend to boast that New York can “beat the world” on cheap clothing. In support of his claim he told me that a single Bowery firm last year sold fifteen thousand suits at $1.95 that averaged in cost $1.12½. With the material at fifteen cents a yard, he said, children’s suits of assorted sizes can be sold at wholesale for seventy-five cents, and boys’ cape overcoats at the same price. They are the same conditions that have perplexed the committee of benevolent Hebrews in charge of Baron de Hirsch’s munificent gift of ten thousand dollars a month for the relief of the Jewish poor in New York. To find proper channels through which to pour this money so that it shall effect its purpose without pauperizing, and without perpetuating the problem it is sought to solve, by attracting still greater swarms, is indeed no easy task. Colonization has not in the past been a success with these people. The great mass of them are too gregarious to take kindly to farming, and their strong commercial instinct hampers the experiment. To herd them in model tenements, though it relieve the physical suffering in a measure, would be to treat a symptom of the disease rather than strike at its root, even if land could be got cheap enough where they gather to build on a sufficiently large scale to make the plan a success. Trade schools for manual training could hardly be made to reach the adults, who in addition would have to be supported for months while learning. For the young this device has proved most excellent under the wise management of the United Hebrew Charities, an organization that gathers to its work the best thought and effort of many of our most public-spirited citizens. One, or all, of these plans may be tried, probably will. I state but the misgivings as to the result of some of the practical minds that have busied themselves with the problem. Its keynote evidently is the ignorance of the immigrants. They must be taught the language of the country they have chosen as their home, as the first and most necessary step. Whatever may follow, that is essential, absolutely vital. That done, it may well be that the case in its new aspect will not be nearly so hard to deal with.

===Chinese===
正是这些经济条件，让我那位制造业朋友敢于夸口：在廉价服装上，纽约「打遍天下无敌手」。为给这句话作证，他告诉我，光是鲍厄里一家商号，去年便卖出一万五千套成衣，售价 $1.95，而平均成本不过 $1.12½。他说，料子按一码一毛五计，各种尺码搭配的童装可以按批发价七毛五一套出售，男童披肩大衣也是同样的价。也正是这些条件，难住了那班主持赫希男爵（Baron de Hirsch）义举的乐善好施的希伯来人委员会——男爵慷慨捐资每月一万美元，救济纽约的犹太穷人。要替这笔钱找到正当的渠道灌下去，既不把人救济成倚靠施舍的乞丐，又不因招来更大群的人而使这个本待解决的问题长存不去，实在不是桩容易事。移民垦殖，在这族人过去的经验里并不成功。他们绝大多数太爱群居，安不下心务农，他们强烈的经商本能也拖着这项试验的后腿。把他们圈进模范廉租公寓，虽说能多少纾解身体上的苦楚，却只是医病的症候，没有铲到病根——即便能在他们聚居的地方买到足够便宜的地皮，把楼盖到足以成事的规模。传授手工技艺的职业学校，很难办到成年人头上，何况学艺期间还得白养他们好几个月。对年轻人，这一法子倒已在联合希伯来慈善会（United Hebrew Charities）的明智办理下证明十分出色——这个组织把我们许多最有公益精神的公民最好的头脑与力气，都聚到了它的工作里来。这些方案之一，或者全部，都可能拿来一试，多半也真会一试。我不过是把几位一直为这个问题操劳的务实之士对结果的疑虑，陈述出来罢了。问题的主调，显然就在移民的无知。必须教他们学会这门语言——他们已选定作为家园的这个国家的语言——这是第一步，也是最不可少的一步。往后无论怎样，这一步是根本，性命攸关。这一步做到了，事情换上一副新面目之后，未必还这样难办。

===Original===
Evening has worn into night as we take up our homeward journey through the streets, now no longer silent. The thousands of lighted windows in the tenements glow like dull red eyes in a huge stone wall. From every door multitudes of tired men and women pour forth for a half-hour’s rest in the open air before sleep closes the eyes weary with incessant working. Crowds of half-naked children tumble in the street and on the sidewalk, or doze fretfully on the stone steps. As we stop in front of a tenement to watch one of these groups, a dirty baby in a single brief garment﻿—yet a sweet, human little baby despite its dirt and tatters﻿—tumbles off the lowest step, rolls over once, clutches my leg with unconscious grip, and goes to sleep on the flagstones, its curly head pillowed on my boot.

===Chinese===
待我们动身回家时，暮色已磨成了黑夜，街道再不似先前沉寂。廉租公寓里成千上万扇亮着的窗，像一堵巨大石墙上无数只暗红的眼睛，幽幽发着光。每一扇门里都涌出大群疲惫的男男女女，趁睡意还没有合上那双叫不息的劳作熬乏了的眼睛，先到露天里歇上半个钟头。成群半裸的孩子在街心、在人行道上打滚，或在石头台阶上烦躁地打着盹。我们在一座廉租公寓门前停下，看这样一群孩子：一个浑身只挂一件小短衫的脏娃娃——尽管又脏又破，仍是个甜甜的、有生人气的小娃娃——从最下一级台阶上一个跟头翻下来，打了个滚，睡梦似的一把攥住我的腿，就在石板路上睡着了，一头卷发枕在我的靴子上。

## XII The Bohemians﻿—Tenement-House Cigarmaking / XII 波希米亚人——廉租公寓里的雪茄制造

===Original===
Evil as the part is which the tenement plays in Jewtown as the pretext for circumventing the law that was made to benefit and relieve the tenant, we have not far to go to find it in even a worse role. If the tenement is here continually dragged into the eye of public condemnation and scorn, it is because in one way or another it is found directly responsible for, or intimately associated with, three-fourths of the miseries of the poor. In the Bohemian quarter it is made the vehicle for enforcing upon a proud race a slavery as real as any that ever disgraced the South. Not content with simply robbing the tenant, the owner, in the dual capacity of landlord and employer, reduces him to virtual serfdom by making his becoming *his* tenant, on such terms as he sees fit to make, the condition of employment at wages likewise of his own making. It does not help the case that this landlord employer, almost always a Jew, is frequently of the thrifty Polish race just described.

===Chinese===
在「犹太镇」（Jewtown），廉租公寓扮演的角色已经够坏了——充当规避那部为造福、解救房客而立的法律的口实——但无须走远，我们就能发现它还扮演着一个更坏的角色。如果说廉租公寓在本书里不断被拖到公众谴责与鄙夷的目光之前，那是因为人们发现，穷人的种种苦难，四分之三不是直接由它造成，就是同它纠缠难解。在波希米亚人聚居区，它被制成一件工具，用来把一种奴役强加给一个骄傲的民族——这奴役之真实，不下于任何曾玷辱过南方的奴役。房主并不满足于单单劫掠房客；他以房东兼雇主的双重身份，把「按他自定的条件当『他的』房客」定为受雇的前提，工钱也同样由他单方面定夺，就这样把工人贬为事实上的农奴。这位房东兼雇主几乎无一例外是犹太人，且往往就是前文刚描写过的那种勤俭的波兰人；这一层并不能使事情有半分改观。

===Original===
Perhaps the Bohemian quarter is hardly the proper name to give to the colony, for though it has distinct boundaries it is scattered over a wide area on the East Side, in wedge-like streaks that relieve the monotony of the solid German population by their strong contrasts. The two races mingle no more on this side of the Atlantic than on the rugged slopes of the Bohemian mountains; the echoes of the thirty years’ war ring in New York, after two centuries and a half, with as fierce a hatred as the gigantic combat bred among the vanquished Czechs. A chief reason for this is doubtless the complete isolation of the Bohemian immigrant. Several causes operate to bring this about: his singularly harsh and unattractive language, which he can neither easily himself unlearn nor impart to others, his stubborn pride of race, and a popular prejudice which has forced upon him the unjust stigma of a disturber of the public peace and an enemy of organized labor. I greatly mistrust that the Bohemian on our shores is a much-abused man. To his traducer, who casts up anarchism against him, he replies that the last census (1880) shows his people to have the fewest criminals of all in proportion to numbers. In New York a Bohemian criminal is such a rarity that the case of two firebugs of several years ago is remembered with damaging distinctness. The accusation that he lives like the “rat” he is, cutting down wages by his underpaid labor, he throws back in the teeth of the trades unions with the countercharge that they are the first cause of his attitude to the labor question.

===Chinese===
把这个聚居区叫作波希米亚人聚居区，也许并不十分恰当；因为虽有明确的边界，它却散布在下东区（East Side）一片广大的地面上，成一道道楔子似的窄条，以强烈的反差，调剂着整片德意志人口的单调。这两个种族在大西洋此岸不相掺杂，一如在波希米亚群山崎岖的山坡上；三十年战争的回声，隔了两个半世纪，仍在纽约鸣响，恨意之烈，不减当年那场大厮杀在被征服的捷克人心中养出的仇恨。个中的主要原因，无疑在于波希米亚移民与外界隔绝之彻底。促成这种隔绝的原因有好几个：他那门出奇生硬、不讨人喜欢的语言——他自己既难以抛开，也难以传授给人；他那股顽固的民族自尊；还有一种流行的成见，把「公共安宁的搅扰者」和「有组织的劳工之敌」这顶不公道的污名强扣到他头上。我深深疑心，我们这片海岸上的波希米亚人，实在是个横遭谤毁的人。对拿无政府主义责难他的诽谤者，他回答：最近一次人口普查（1880 年）显示，按人口比例计，他的族人是所有族群中罪犯最少的。在纽约，波希米亚罪犯罕见到这般地步，以至几年前两个纵火贼的案子至今仍被人记得一清二楚，大有损于他的名声。说他活得就像「老鼠」本人，靠甘受低酬的劳动拉低工钱——这项指控，他迎面摔回工会的口中，反控道：他之所以对劳工问题抱持这种态度，第一原因正是工会。
===Original===
A little way above Houston Street the first of his colonies is encountered, in Fifth Street and thereabouts. Then for a mile and a half scarce a Bohemian is to be found, until Thirty-eighth Street is reached. Fifty-fourth and Seventy-third Streets in their turn are the centres of populous Bohemian settlements. The location of the cigar factories, upon which he depends for a living, determines his choice of home, though there is less choice about it than with any other class in the community, save perhaps the colored people. Probably more than half of all the Bohemians in this city are cigarmakers, and it is the herding of these in great numbers in the so-called tenement factories, where the cheapest grade of work is done at the lowest wages, that constitutes at once their greatest hardship and the chief grudge of other workmen against them. The manufacturer who owns, say, from three or four, to a dozen or more tenements contiguous to his shop, fills them up with these people, charging them outrageous rents, and demanding often even a preliminary deposit of five dollars “key money;” deals them out tobacco by the week, and devotes the rest of his energies to the paring down of wages to within a peg or two of the point where the tenant rebels in desperation. When he does rebel, he is given the alternative of submission, or eviction with entire loss of employment. His needs determine the issue. Usually he is not in a position to hesitate long. Unlike the Polish Jew, whose example of untiring industry he emulates, he has seldom much laid up against a rainy day. He is fond of a glass of beer, and likes to live as well as his means will permit. The shop triumphs, and fetters more galling than ever are forged for the tenant. In the opposite case, the newspapers have to record the throwing upon the street of a small army of people, with pitiful cases of destitution and family misery.

===Chinese===
在休斯敦街（Houston Street）以北不远处，便遇上他的第一片聚居地，位于第五街（Fifth Street）一带。往下一英里半，几乎寻不见一个波希米亚人，直到了第三十八街（Thirty-eighth Street）。第五十四街和第七十三街又相继成为人烟稠密的波希米亚聚居地的中心。雪茄工场的位置决定他对住家的选择——他的生计全仰仗于它——虽说在这上头，他的选择余地比社会上任何别的阶层都小，或许只有有色人种除外。本城的波希米亚人，恐怕半数以上是雪茄匠；而正是这些人大群大群地被圈在所谓的「廉租公寓工场」里，以最低的工钱做最低档的活计，这一下子既构成了他们最大的苦楚，也构成了其他工人对他们最主要的怨气。制造商拥有——比方说——三四处到一打甚至更多的廉租公寓，紧挨着他的工场；他把这些人装满这些屋子，收他们敲竹杠似的房租，往往还要先勒一笔五块钱的「钥匙钱」（“key money”）；按星期把烟叶分发给他们，然后把余下的精力全用在削砍工钱上，削到离房客绝望反抗的那一点只差一两档的地步。他一旦反抗，摆在他面前的选择便是：要么屈服，要么被逐出家门，连同活计一并丢光。他的需要裁决了这桩事。通常他也没有长久犹豫的本钱。他不像波兰犹太人——尽管他效法后者不知疲倦的勤勉——他难得攒下多少积蓄以防阴雨的日子。他爱喝上一杯啤酒，也喜欢在财力许可之内把日子过得像样些。工场得胜，给房客锻出比先前更勒人的镣铐。若是相反的情形，报纸便不得不记载一小队人马被抛上街头，附带着种种穷愁潦倒、家庭破碎的惨状，令人心酸。

===Original===
Men, women and children work together seven days in the week in these cheerless tenements to make a living for the family, from the break of day till far into the night. Often the wife is the original cigarmaker from the old home, the husband having adopted her trade here as a matter of necessity, because, knowing no word of English, he could get no other work. As they state the cause of the bitter hostility of the trades unions, she was the primary bone of contention in the day of the early Bohemian immigration. The unions refused to admit the women, and, as the support of the family depended upon her to a large extent, such terms as were offered had to be accepted. The manufacturer has ever since industriously fanned the antagonism between the unions and his hands, for his own advantage. The victory rests with him, since the Court of Appeals decided that the law, passed a few years ago, to prohibit cigarmaking in tenements was unconstitutional, and thus put an end to the struggle. While it lasted, all sorts of frightful stories were told of the shocking conditions under which people lived and worked in these tenements, from a sanitary point of view especially, and a general impression survives to this day that they are particularly desperate. The Board of Health, after a careful canvass, did not find them so then. I am satisfied from personal inspection, at a much later day, guided in a number of instances by the union cigarmakers themselves to the tenements which they considered the worst, that the accounts were greatly exaggerated. Doubtless the people are poor, in many cases very poor; but they are not uncleanly, rather the reverse; they live much better than the clothing-makers in the Tenth Ward, and in spite of their sallow look, that may be due to the all-pervading smell of tobacco, they do not appear to be less healthy than other indoor workers. I found on my tours of investigation several cases of consumption, of which one at least was said by the doctor to be due to the constant inhalation of tobacco fumes. But an examination of the death records in the Health Department does not support the claim that the Bohemian cigarmakers are peculiarly prone to that disease. On the contrary, the Bohemian percentage of deaths from consumption appears quite low. This, however, is a line of scientific inquiry which I leave others to pursue, along with the more involved problem whether the falling off in the number of children, sometimes quite noticeable in the Bohemian settlements, is, as has been suggested, dependent upon the character of the parents’ work. The sore grievances I found were the miserable wages and the enormous rents exacted for the minimum of accommodation. And surely these stand for enough of suffering.

===Chinese===
男人、女人和孩子在这些阴惨惨的廉租公寓里一同做工，一周七天，从破晓直干到深夜，为一家人挣出生计。妻子往往原本就是老家做雪茄的匠人，丈夫到这里改操她的手艺，实出无奈：因为一个英文字也不识，他寻不到别的活计。照他们自己陈述的缘由，工会那股恶毒的敌意正是打她身上起的：波希米亚人早期移民的日子里，她便是头一块争执的骨头。工会不肯收纳女工，而一家的生计既大半指望她，人家开出的条件再差也只得接受。制造商从此孜孜不倦地煽着工会与他的工人之间的敌对，好为自己渔利。胜利落在他手里：上诉法院（Court of Appeals）裁定，几年前通过的那部禁止在廉租公寓里制造雪茄的法律违宪，就此了结了这场争斗。争斗持续期间，人们讲过种种吓人的故事，讲这些廉租公寓里的人起居做工的环境如何骇人听闻，卫生方面尤其如此；一种笼统的印象一直留存到今天，说那里情形格外绝望。卫生委员会（Board of Health）做过一番仔细的查访，当时并未发现如此。我个人在晚得多的日子里实地查访过——好几回还是由工会的雪茄匠亲自领路，去他们认为最糟的那几座廉租公寓——我由此确信，那些说法大大夸张了。无疑，这些人是穷，许多场合穷得很；但他们并不腌臜，毋宁说恰恰相反；他们过得比第十选区（Tenth Ward）做衣裳的人家好得多；而且尽管面色蜡黄——那可能是烟草气味无孔不入之故——他们看上去并不比其他室内工人更不健康。我在巡查途中撞见几例肺痨，其中至少一例，据医生说，是常年吸入烟草烟气所致。但翻查卫生局（Health Department）的死亡记录，并不支持波希米亚雪茄匠特别易染此病的说法。恰恰相反，波希米亚人死于肺痨的百分比显得相当低。不过，这条科学考究的路子，我留给旁人去走；连同那个更纠缠的问题——波希米亚聚居地里孩子数目的减少（有时相当惹眼），是否如有人提示的，系于父母所操营生的性质。我寻访所得的切肤之怨，是那点可怜的工钱，和为最小限度的栖身之所勒索的巨额房租。而这两样所代表的苦难，确确实实已经够多了。
===Original===
Take a row of houses in East Tenth Street as an instance. They contained thirty-five families of cigarmakers, with probably not half a dozen persons in the whole lot of them, outside of the children, who could speak a word of English, though many had been in the country half a lifetime. This room with two windows giving on the street, and a rear attachment without windows, called a bedroom by courtesy, is rented at $12.25 a month. In the front room man and wife work at the bench from six in the morning till nine at night. They make a team, stripping the tobacco leaves together; then he makes the filler, and she rolls the wrapper on and finishes the cigar. For a thousand they receive $3.75, and can turn out together three thousand cigars a week. The point has been reached where the rebellion comes in, and the workers in these tenements are just now on a strike, demanding $5.00 and $5.50 for their work. The manufacturer having refused, they are expecting hourly to be served with notice to quit their homes, and the going of a stranger among them excites their resentment, until his errand is explained. While we are in the house, the ultimatum of the “boss” is received. He will give $3.75 a thousand, not another cent. Our host is a man of seeming intelligence, yet he has been nine years in New York and knows neither English nor German. Three bright little children play about the floor.

===Chinese===
拿东第十街（East Tenth Street）的一排房子作个例子。里面住着三十五户雪茄匠，除孩子不算，这一大群人里恐怕凑不齐半打能讲一句英语的，虽然许多人在这个国家已经住了半辈子。这间朝着大街开两扇窗的屋子，外加后头一间不带窗的附属小间——姑且称之为卧室——月租 $12.25。前屋里，夫妻二人从早晨六点到晚上九点伏在工作台前做工。他们是一组搭档，一道剥烟叶；随后他做烟芯，她卷上外包叶，把雪茄收尾。每做一千支，他们得 $3.75，两人合力一周能出三千支雪茄。眼下已经到了反叛临头的地方：这些廉租公寓里的工人正在罢工，要求活计按 $5.00 和 $5.50 计酬。制造商已经回绝，他们时时刻刻等着被送来腾房的告示；生人走到他们中间会惹起他们的怨愤，直到说明来意。我们还在屋里的时候，「老板」（“boss”）的最后通牒到了。一千支给 $3.75，再无一分。我们的东家看着是个明白人，然而他在纽约住了九年，英语德语一概不识。三个亮眼睛的小孩子在地板上玩耍。

===Original===
His neighbor on the same floor has been here fifteen years, but shakes his head when asked if he can speak English. He answers in a few broken syllables when addressed in German. With $11.75 rent to pay for like accommodation, he has the advantage of his oldest boy’s work besides his wife’s at the bench. Three properly make a team, and these three can turn out four thousand cigars a week, at $3.75. This Bohemian has a large family; there are four children, too small to work, to be cared for. A comparison of the domestic bill of fare between Tenth and Ludlow Streets result, in the discovery that this Bohemian’s butcher’s bill for the week, with meat at twelve cents a pound as in Ludlow Street, is from two dollars and a half to three dollars. The Polish Jew fed as big a family on one pound of meat a day. The difference proves to be typical. Here is a suit of three rooms, two dark, three flights up. The ceiling is partly down in one of the rooms. “It is three months since we asked the landlord to fix it,” says the oldest son, a very intelligent lad who has learned English in the evening school. His father has not had that advantage, and has sat at his bench, deaf and dumb to the world about him except his own, for six years. He has improved his time and become an expert at his trade. Father, mother, and son together, a full team, make from fifteen to sixteen dollars a week.

===Chinese===
同一层楼的邻居来了十五年，问他会不会讲英语，他直摇头。用德语问他话，他断断续续吐出几个音节作答。同样的屋子，房租 $11.75，他的便宜是大儿子也能上工作台，同妻子一道。三个人才算一组齐整的搭档，这三个人一周能出四千支雪茄，按 $3.75 计。这个波希米亚人家里人口多；还有四个孩子太小，做不得工，须人照看。把第十街与勒德洛街（Ludlow Street）两下的家常饭谱作个比较，结果发现：肉价照勒德洛街的行情，十二美分一磅，这个波希米亚人一周的肉账是两块半到三块钱。同样大的一家，波兰犹太人一天一磅肉就打发了。这个差别证明是带典型性的。这是一套三间的屋子，两间暗的，需上三段楼梯。其中一间屋的天花板塌了半边。“我们请房东来修，已经三个月了，”大儿子说；他是个十分伶俐的小伙子，在夜校学会了英语。他父亲没有这份造化，六年来坐在工作台前，对周遭的世界又聋又哑——只除了他自家的那个。他把光阴用足了，练成了本行的行家。父亲、母亲、儿子合起来，一组满员的搭档，一周挣十五到十六块钱。
===Original===
A man with venerable beard and keen eyes answers our questions through an interpreter, in the next house. Very few brighter faces would be met in a day’s walk among American mechanics, yet he has in nine years learned no syllable of English. German he probably does not want to learn. His story supplies the explanation, as did the stories of the others. In all that time he has been at work grubbing to earn bread. Wife and he by constant labor make three thousand cigars a week, earning $11.25 when there is no lack of material; when in winter they receive from the manufacturer tobacco for only two thousand, the rent of $10 for two rooms, practically one with a dark alcove, has nevertheless to be paid in full, and six mouths to be fed. He was a blacksmith in the old country, but cannot work at his trade here because he does not understand “Engliska.” If he could, he says, with a bright look, he could do better work than he sees done here. It would seem happiness to him to knock off at 6 o’clock instead of working, as he now often has to do, till midnight. But how? He knows of no Bohemian blacksmith who can understand him; he should starve. Here, with his wife, he can make a living at least. “Aye,” says she, turning, from listening, to her household duties, “it would be nice for sure to have father work at his trade.” Then what a home she could make for them, and how happy they would be. Here is an unattainable ideal, indeed, of a workman in the most prosperous city in the world! There is genuine, if unspoken, pathos in the soft tap she gives her husband’s hand as she goes about her work with a half-suppressed little sigh.

===Chinese===
隔壁房子里，一个须髯苍然、目光锐利的汉子由译员代答我们的问题。在美洲工匠中间走上一天，也难得撞见几张比他更明亮的脸；然而九年之间，他没有学会一个音节的英语。德语他多半是不想学的。他的经历自能说明缘故，旁人的经历也都一样。这整整九年，他一直在埋头刨食挣面包。妻子和他靠不停歇的劳作一周做三千支雪茄，原料不缺的时候挣 $11.25；冬天从制造商那里只领得到两千支的烟叶时，两间屋——实在是一间带一个暗凹间——$10 的房租却照数要付，还有六张嘴要喂。他在故国本是铁匠，在这里却干不成本行，因为他听不懂“Engliska”（英语）。他说，要是干得成——说着眼睛一亮——他做的活计能比他在这城里见人做的强。对他来说，六点钟收工，而不是像他如今常常不得不的那样干到半夜，就算是幸福了。可是怎么成呢？他认不得一个能听懂他话的波希米亚铁匠；他只好挨饿。在这里，同妻子一起，他至少挣得出一家活路。“是啊，”她说着，从旁听着转过身去料理家务，“孩子他爹能干本行，那可真是再好也没有了。”那样的话，她能给他们操持出怎样一个家，他们会多么快活。瞧，这竟是世界最繁华城市里一个工人够不着的理想！她含着半按下去的一声轻叹做着家务，轻轻拍了拍丈夫的手——那里面有一种虽未出口、却真真切切的哀婉。

===Original===
The very ash-barrels that stand in front of the big rows of tenements in Seventy-first and Seventy-third Streets advertise the business that is carried on within. They are filled to the brim with the stems of stripped tobacco leaves. The rank smell that waited for us on the corner of the block follows us into the hallways, penetrates every nook and cranny of the houses. As in the settlement farther downtown, every room here has its workbench with its stumpy knife and queer pouch of bed-tick, worn brown and greasy, fastened in front the whole length of the bench to receive the scraps of waste. This landlord-employer at all events gives three rooms for $12.50, if two be dark, one wholly and the other getting some light from the front room. The mother of the three barefooted little children we met on the stairs was taken to the hospital the other day when she could no longer work. She will never come out alive. There is no waste in these tenements. Lives, like clothes, are worn through and out before put aside. Her place at the bench is taken already by another who divides with the head of the household his earnings of $15.50 a week. He has just come out successful of a strike that brought the pay of these tenements up to $4.50 per thousand cigars. Notice to quit had already been served on them, when the employer decided to give in, frightened by the prospective loss of rent. Asked how long he works, the man says: “from they can see till bedtime.” Bedtime proves to be eleven o’clock. Seventeen hours a day, seven days in the week, at thirteen cents an hour for the two, six cents and a half for each! Good average earnings for a tenement-house cigarmaker in summer. In winter it is at least one-fourth less. In spite of it all, the rooms are cleanly kept. From the bedroom farthest back the woman brings out a pile of moist tobacco-leaves to be stripped. They are kept there, under cover lest they dry and crack, from Friday to Friday, when an accounting is made and fresh supplies given out. The people sleep there too, but the smell, offensive to the unfamiliar nose, does not bother them. They are used to it.

===Chinese===
第七十一街和第七十三街成排大廉租公寓门前立着的炉灰桶，本身就在替里面做的营生做广告。桶里满到齐口，全是剥过的烟叶叶梗。在街区拐角上迎候我们的那股冲鼻气味，跟进过道，钻进房子的每一处孔窍缝隙。同下城那边更远的聚居地一样，这里每间屋都有自己的工作台，配着那把短粗的刀，还有一条古怪的床袋布口袋——磨得发褐、油亮亮的——钉在工作台正面全长上，承接废料碎屑。这位房东兼雇主好歹三间屋只收 $12.50——虽说两间是暗的，一间全黑，另一间靠前屋漏进点光。我们在楼梯上碰见的三个赤脚小孩子的母亲，前几天再也干不动活的时候进了医院。她不会再活着出来了。这些廉租公寓里没有浪费。性命同衣裳一样，磨到穿破了、磨尽了才搁下。她在工作台前的位子已经有人顶上，同这一家的家长分他每周 $15.50 的进项。他刚从一场罢工里得了胜，那场罢工把这些廉租公寓的工钱抬到每千支雪茄 $4.50。腾房的告示本来已经送到他们手上，雇主却叫眼看要损失房租的前景吓住了，决定让步。问他做多长时间的工，那汉子说：“从看得见做到睡觉。”睡觉原来是指十一点钟。一天十七个钟头，一周七天，两人合计每小时十三美分，一人六美分半！这还是廉租公寓雪茄匠夏天蛮好的平均进项。冬天至少还得少四分之一。任凭这样，屋子却收拾得干干净净。女人从最里头的卧室里搬出一堆待剥的湿烟叶。烟叶存在那里面，盖着，怕它们干裂，从星期五存到星期五；到日子结一回账，发下新烟料。人也睡在那里面，可那股气味——外人的鼻子闻着刺鼻——并不搅扰他们。他们惯了。
===Original===
In a house around the corner that is not a factory-tenement, lives now the cigarmaker I spoke of as suffering from consumption which the doctor said was due to the tobacco-fumes. Perhaps the lack of healthy exercise had as much to do with it. His case is interesting from its own standpoint. He too is one with a﻿—for a Bohemian﻿—large family. Six children sit at his table. By trade a shoemaker, for thirteen years he helped his wife make cigars in the manufacturer’s tenement. She was a very good hand, and until his health gave out two years ago they were able to make from $17 to $25 a week, by lengthening the day at both ends. Now that he can work no more, and the family under the doctor’s orders has moved away from the smell of tobacco, the burden of its support has fallen upon her alone, for none of the children are old enough to help. She has work in the shop at eight dollars a week, and this must go round; it is all there is. Happily, this being a tenement for revenue only, unmixed with cigars, the rent is cheaper: seven dollars for two bright rooms on the top floor. No housekeeping is attempted. A woman in Seventy-second Street supplies their cooking, which the wife and mother fetches in a basket, her husband being too weak. Breakfast of coffee and hardtack, or black bread, at twenty cents for the whole eight; a good many, the little woman says with a brave, patient smile, and there is seldom anything to spare, but﻿—. The invalid is listening, and the sentence remains unfinished. What of dinner? One of the children brings it from the cook. Oh! it is a good dinner, meat, soup, greens and bread, all for thirty cents. It is the principal family meal. Does she come home for dinner? No; she cannot leave the shop, but gets a bite at her bench. The question: A bite of what? seems as merciless as the surgeon’s knife, and she winces under it as one shrinks from physical pain. Bread, then. But at night they all have supper together﻿—sausage and bread. For ten cents they can eat all they want. Can they not? she says, stroking the hair of the little boy at her knee; his eyes glisten hungrily at the thought, as he nods stoutly in support of his mother. Only, she adds, the week the rent is due, they have to shorten rations to pay the landlord.

===Chinese===
拐角上一座并非工场廉租公寓的房子里，如今住着我提过的那个害肺痨的雪茄匠——医生说他的病是烟草烟气闹下的。兴许缺乏健康的活动也有同等的干系。他这桩情况，就它本身而论，颇可玩味。他也是那种——就波希米亚人而言——人丁兴旺的一家。六个孩子坐在他的饭桌边。他本行是鞋匠，十三年来在制造商的廉租公寓里帮妻子做雪茄。她是把很好的快手，直到两年前他的身体垮下来，夫妻俩靠把白天向两头抻长，一周挣得到 $17 到 $25。如今他再做不得工，一家人奉医生之命搬开了烟草的气味，养家的担子便落在她一个人身上，因为没有一个孩子大到帮得上手。她在工场里有活，一周八块钱，这笔钱得匀着全家用；全部指望就这一点。幸而这所廉租公寓只管收租，不与雪茄相混，房租便宜些：顶楼两间敞亮屋七块钱。伙食是无从自理的。第七十二街的一个女人包他们的饭，由这妻子兼母亲提篮去取，她丈夫太虚弱了。早饭是咖啡和硬面饼，或者黑面包，全家八口拢共二十美分；“人口可不少呢，”瘦小的妇人带着勇敢而忍耐的微笑说，而且难得有什么富余，可是——。病人正听着，这句话便停在那里没有说完。午饭呢？一个孩子从掌勺的那里取来。哦！那是顿好饭：肉、汤、青菜和面包，三十美分全有了。这是家里一天的正餐。她回家吃午饭吗？不；她离不开工场，只好在工作台旁对付一口。问一句：对付一口什么？这话跟外科医生的刀一样不容情，她在底下瑟缩，像人躲闪皮肉的疼痛似的。面包罢了。可是夜里一家人聚在一起吃晚饭——香肠和面包。十美分，尽他们吃个够。可不是么？她说着，抚摩膝上小男孩的头发；那孩子想到这里，眼睛饿得发亮，一面使劲点头，替母亲帮腔。只是，她又说，逢上交房租的那个星期，他们得把口粮减一减，好付房东。

===Original===
But what of his being an Anarchist, this Bohemian﻿—an infidel﻿—I hear somebody say. Almost one might be persuaded by such facts as these﻿—and they are everyday facts, not fancy﻿—to retort: what more natural? With every hand raised against him in the old land and the new, in the land of his hoped-for freedom, what more logical than that his should be turned against society that seems to exist only for his oppression? But the charge is not half true. Naturally the Bohemian loves peace, as he loves music and song. As someone has said: He does not seek war, but when attacked knows better how to die than how to surrender. The Czech is the Irishman of Central Europe, with all his genius and his strong passions, with the same bitter traditions of landlord-robbery, perpetuated here where he thought to forget them; like him ever and on principle in the opposition, “agin the government” wherever he goes. Among such a people, ground by poverty until their songs have died in curses upon their oppressors, hopelessly isolated and ignorant of our language and our laws, it would not be hard for bad men at any time to lead a few astray. And this is what has been done. Yet, even with the occasional noise made by the few, the criminal statistics already alluded to quite dispose of the charge that they incline to turbulence and riot. So it is with the infidel propaganda, the legacy perhaps of the fierce contention through hundreds of years between Catholics and Protestants on Bohemia’s soil, of bad faith and savage persecutions in the name of the Christians’ God that disgrace its history. The Bohemian clergyman, who spoke for his people at the Christian Conference held in Chickering Hall two years ago, took even stronger ground. “They are Roman Catholics by birth, infidels by necessity, and Protestants by history and inclination,” he said. Yet he added his testimony in the same breath to the fact that, though the Freethinkers had started two schools in the immediate neighborhood of his church to counteract its influence, his flock had grown in a few years from a mere handful at the start to proportions far beyond his hopes, gathering in both Anarchists and Freethinkers, and making good church members of them.

===Chinese===
可是这个波希米亚人还是个无政府主义者——还是个不信神的人——我听见有人说了。面对这样的事实——而且它们是天天有的事实，不是凭空想象——几乎叫人忍不住回敬一句：还有比这更自然的吗？在故国和新土，在他盼望来享自由的这片土地上，人人举手与他为敌，他转而举起自己的手，反抗那个似乎专为压迫他而存在的社会，还有比这更合逻辑的吗？但这项指控连一半也不是事实。波希米亚人天性爱好和平，正如他爱好音乐和歌。正如有人说过：他不寻战争，可一旦遭到攻击，他更懂得怎样去死，不懂得怎样投降。捷克人是中欧的爱尔兰人，有他全部的天才和炽烈的激情，有同样辛酸的地主劫掠的旧恨——在这里，在他以为可以忘掉它们的地方，那旧恨反倒被延续了下去；同爱尔兰人一样，永远而且出于原则地站在反对派一边，走到哪里都「跟政府作对」（“agin the government”）。在这样一个民族中间——被贫穷碾磨，直到他们的歌都死在诅咒压迫者的声里；与世隔绝得无望，对我们的语言和我们的法律一无所知——坏人在任何时候把少数人引入歧途，都不是难事。而这种事也确实做下了。然而，即便有那少数人偶尔闹出的动静，前文已经提过的犯罪统计，也足以撇清他们倾向于骚乱暴动的指控。无神论的宣传也是如此——那宗遗产，或许得自天主教徒与新教徒几百年来在波希米亚土地上的激烈争斗，得自那以基督徒的上帝之名干下的背信弃义和野蛮迫害，桩桩件件玷污着它的历史。两年前在奇克林音乐厅（Chickering Hall）举行的基督教大会上代表他的族人发言的那位波希米亚牧师，站的立场甚至更硬。“他们生来是罗马天主教徒，出于境况是不信神的人，凭历史和倾向是新教徒，”他说。然而他紧接着又作证说：尽管自由思想者（Freethinkers）曾在他教堂紧邻的地方办了两所学校以抵消它的影响，他的羊群却在几年之内从起初寥寥的一小撮，壮大到远远超出他期望的规模，把无政府主义者和自由思想者都收了进来，并把他们造就成了好教民。

===Original===
Thus the whole matter resolves itself once more into a question of education, all the more urgent because these people are poor, miserably poor almost to a man. “There is not,” said one of them, who knew thoroughly what he was speaking of, “there is not one of them all, who, if he were to sell all he was worth tomorrow, would have money enough to buy a house and lot in the country.”

===Chinese===
于是整个问题再一次归结为一个教育问题，而且因为这些人穷——几乎人人穷到悲惨的田地——而愈发紧迫。“他们全部人当中，”他们中间一个深知自己所言的人说，“找不出一个，明天把全部家当变卖了，就能凑出在乡下买一所宅子连带一块地皮的钱的。”

## XIII The Color Line in New York / XIII 纽约的肤色界线

===Original===
The color line must be drawn through the tenements to give the picture its proper shading. The landlord does the drawing, does it with an absence of pretence, a frankness of despotism, that is nothing if not brutal. The Czar of all the Russias is not more absolute upon his own soil than the New York landlord in his dealings with colored tenants. Where he permits them to live, they go; where he shuts the door, stay out. By his grace they exist at all in certain localities; his ukase banishes them from others. He accepts the responsibility, when laid at his door, with unruffled complacency. It is business, he will tell you. And it is. He makes the prejudice in which he traffics pay him well, and that, as he thinks it quite superfluous to tell you, is what he is there for.

===Chinese===
肤色界线（color line）必须画进廉租公寓（tenement）里来，这幅图景才能有它应有的浓淡。执笔作画的正是房东，他画得毫无虚饰，专制得坦率之至——那做派不是别的，正是赤裸裸的粗暴。全俄罗斯的沙皇在自己国土上的绝对权威，也不过如此：纽约房东对付有色房客，正有这般绝对。他准他们住到哪里，他们便住到哪里；他把门一关，他们便只得待在门外。在某些地段，他们能立足全凭他的恩典；他的一道敕令，又把他们从别的地段放逐出去。责任追到他门上，他不动声色、踌躇满志地领受下来。他会告诉你：这是生意。也确是生意。他让他贩运的那份偏见付给他优厚的报酬——至于这一层，他觉得犯不着告诉你：他待在那儿，为的正是这个。

===Original===
That his pencil does not make quite as black a mark as it did, that the hand that wields it does not bear down as hard as only a short half dozen years ago, is the hopeful sign of an awakening public conscience under the stress of which the line shows signs of wavering. But for this the landlord deserves no credit. It has come, is coming about despite him. The line may not be wholly effaced while the name of the negro, alone among the world’s races, is spelled with a small *n*. Natural selection will have more or less to do beyond a doubt in every age with dividing the races; only so, it may be, can they work out together their highest destiny. But with the despotism that deliberately assigns to the defenceless Black the lowest level for the purpose of robbing him there that has nothing to do. Of such slavery, different only in degree from the other kind that held him as a chattel, to be sold or bartered at the will of his master, this century, if signs fail not, will see the end in New York.

===Chinese===
他的铅笔如今划出的印痕，已不似往日那样乌黑；执笔的手也不像短短六年前那样死命下压——这是公众良知正在苏醒的可喜迹象，在那份压力之下，界线已显出动摇的征象。但这一功记不到房东名下。转机是撇开他而来的，也正撇开他推进着。只要「黑人」（negro）之名——举世诸族之名中独它一个——仍用小写的 *n* 起拼，这条界线便难望全然抹去。毫无疑问，无论哪个时代，自然选择都多少要在种族的分野里插一手；也许唯有如此，各族才能协力成就他们至高的命运。可是，那种处心积虑把无自卫之力的黑人指派到最低一层、好在那里劫掠他的专制，同自然选择毫不相干。这样的奴役，与旧日那种把他视作动产、听凭主人之意发卖典押的奴役，只有程度之差；本世纪——倘若种种迹象不曾骗人——将在纽约亲见它的终结。

===Original===
Ever since the war New York has been receiving the overflow of colored population from the Southern cities. In the last decade this migration has grown to such proportions that it is estimated that our Blacks have quite doubled in number since the Tenth Census. Whether the exchange has been of advantage to the negro may well be questioned. Trades of which he had practical control in his Southern home are not open to him here. I know that it may be answered that there is no industrial proscription of color; that it is a matter of choice. Perhaps so. At all events he does not choose then. How many colored carpenters or masons has anyone seen at work in New York? In the South there are enough of them and, if the testimony of the most intelligent of their people is worth anything, plenty of them have come here. As a matter of fact the colored man takes in New York, without a struggle, the lower level of menial service for which his past traditions and natural love of ease perhaps as yet fit him best. Even the colored barber is rapidly getting to be a thing of the past. Along shore, at any unskilled labor, he works unmolested; but he does not appear to prefer the job. His sphere thus defined, he naturally takes his stand among the poor, and in the homes of the poor. Until very recent times﻿—the years since a change was wrought can be counted on the fingers of one hand﻿—he was practically restricted in the choice of a home to a narrow section on the West Side, that nevertheless had a social top and bottom to it﻿—the top in the tenements on the line of Seventh Avenue as far north as Thirty-second Street, where he was allowed to occupy the houses of unsavory reputation which the police had cleared and for which decent white tenants could not be found; the bottom in the vile rookeries of Thompson Street and South Fifth Avenue, the old “Africa” that is now fast becoming a modern Italy. Today there are black colonies in Yorkville and Morrisania. The encroachment of business and the Italian below, and the swelling of the population above, have been the chief agents in working out his second emancipation, a very real one, for with his cutting loose from the old tenements there has come a distinct and gratifying improvement in the tenant, that argues louder than theories or speeches the influence of vile surroundings in debasing the man. The colored citizen whom this year’s census man found in his Ninety-ninth Street “flat” is a very different individual from the “nigger” his predecessor counted in the black-and-tan slums of Thompson and Sullivan Streets. There is no more clean and orderly community in New York than the new settlement of colored people that is growing up on the East Side from Yorkville to Harlem.

===Chinese===
自内战以来，纽约便一直在承接南方各城溢出的有色人口。最近十年，这场迁徙的规模已涨到如此地步：据估计，自第十次人口普查（Tenth Census）以来，我们的黑人已足足翻了一番。这场置换于黑人是否有利，大可置疑。他在南方老家实际执掌的那些行当，这里不向他敞开。我知道有人会答辩：这里并没有对肤色的行业排斥，那纯属各人自择。或许如此。反正他并没有去择。在纽约，谁见过几个有色木匠或泥瓦匠上工？在南方他们为数甚众；而且，倘若他们族中那些最有智识者的证言还作得数，其中大批已经到了这里。事实上，在纽约，有色男子不作任何抗争便领受了下等仆役的低位；以他过往的传统与天生的好逸而论，这个位置眼下或许正最合他。连有色理发师都在迅速沦为过眼旧物。在滨海码头，凡不需技艺的粗活，他做工无人搅扰；可他似乎并不偏爱这份差事。谋生范围既经如此划定，他自然而然站到了穷人中间，住进了穷人的家宅。直到晚近——变化发生以来的年头，一只手数得过来——他在择居上实际被局限在西区（West Side）一段狭窄的地段里；但那地段自有其社会的顶层与底层——顶层在第七大道（Seventh Avenue）沿线、向北远抵第三十二街（Thirty-second Street）的廉租公寓，那里准他住进名声腌臜的宅子，那些宅子经警察清剿之后，体面的白人房客再无一人肯来；底层在汤普森街（Thompson Street）与南第五大道（South Fifth Avenue）污秽的破楼烂屋里，那是旧日的「非洲」（Africa，当时对黑人聚居区的代称），如今正飞快变成一个新式的意大利。今天，约克维尔（Yorkville）和莫里桑尼亚（Morrisania）都有了黑人聚居地。下城商业与意大利人的步步进逼，加上上城人口的膨胀，是促成他第二次解放的两员主将——一场实实在在的解放；因为随着他同旧廉租公寓一刀两断，房客本身也出现了分明而可喜的改观，这比任何理论或演说都更响亮地证明：污浊的环境足以使人堕落。今年人口普查员在第九十九街（Ninety-ninth Street）那套「公寓」里查到的那位有色公民，同他的前任在汤普森街与萨利文街（Sullivan Street）黑白混杂贫民窟里点数过的那个「黑鬼」（nigger，当时蔑称），已是截然不同的两个人。纽约再没有比正在下东区（East Side）从约克维尔到哈莱姆（Harlem）成长起来的这片有色人种新聚居地更干净、更有秩序的社区了。

===Original===
Cleanliness is the characteristic of the negro in his new surroundings, as it was his virtue in the old. In this respect he is immensely the superior of the lowest of the whites, the Italians and the Polish Jews, below whom he has been classed in the past in the tenant scale. Nevertheless, he has always had to pay higher rents than even these for the poorest and most stinted rooms. The exceptions I have come across, in which the rents, though high, have seemed more nearly on a level with what was asked for the same number and size of rooms in the average tenement, were in the case of tumbledown rookeries in which no one else would live, and were always coupled with the condition that the landlord should “make no repairs.” It can readily be seen, that his profits were scarcely curtailed by his “humanity.” The reason advanced for this systematic robbery is that white people will not live in the same house with colored tenants, or even in a house recently occupied by negroes, and that consequently its selling value is injured. The prejudice undoubtedly exists, but it is not lessened by the house agents, who have set up the maxim “once a colored house, always a colored house.”

===Chinese===
清洁是黑人在新环境里的特征，一如它是他在旧环境里的美德。在这一层上，他远远胜过白人中最底层的那些人——意大利人和波兰犹太人；而在过去的房客等级表上，他竟被划到这些人之下。饶是如此，即便是向这些人，他也一直得为最贫陋、最寒酸的房间付出更高的租金。我偶尔撞见的几处例外——那里的租金虽然也高，却似乎比较接近寻常廉租公寓里同样间数、同样大小的房间的要价——全都发生在摇摇欲坠、除他无人肯住的破楼里，而且总附带一个条件：房东「概不修缮」。不难看出，他这点「人道」几乎不曾削减他的利润。为这种成体系的劫掠辩解的理由是：白人不肯与有色房客同居一屋，甚至连黑人新近住过的房子也不肯住，房子的售价因此受损。这种偏见无疑存在，但房屋中介们不曾使它减轻分毫——他们立下了一条格言：「一朝有色屋，永远有色屋。」

===Original===
There is method in the maxim, as shown by an inquiry made last year by the *Real Estate Record*. It proved agents to be practically unanimous in the endorsement of the negro as a clean, orderly, and “profitable” tenant. Here is the testimony of one of the largest real estate firms in the city: “We would rather have negro tenants in our poorest class of tenements than the lower grades of foreign white people. We find the former cleaner than the latter, and they do not destroy the property so much. We also get higher prices. We have a tenement on Nineteenth Street, where we get $10 for two rooms which we could not get more than $7.50 for from white tenants previously. We have a four-story tenement on our books on Thirty-third Street, between Sixth and Seventh Avenues, with four rooms per floor﻿—a parlor, two bedrooms, and a kitchen. We get $20 for the first floor, $24 for the second, $23 for the third and $20 for the fourth, in all $87 or $1,044 per annum. The size of the building is only 21+55.” Another firm declared that in a specified instance they had saved fifteen to twenty percent on the gross rentals since they changed their white tenants for colored ones. Still another gave the following case of a front and rear tenement that had formerly been occupied by tenants of a “low European type,” who had been turned out on account of filthy habits and poor pay. The negroes proved cleaner, better, and steadier tenants. Instead, however, of having their rents reduced in consequence, the comparison stood as follows:

===Chinese===
这条格言自有其算盘，去年《房地产记录》（*Real Estate Record*）的一项调查便是明证。它证明，中介们在称赞黑人房客干净、守秩序、「有利可图」这一点上几乎众口一词。以下是本市数一数二的房地产公司之一的证词：「在我们最下等的几类廉租公寓里，我们宁可要黑人房客，也不要较低等的外国白人。我们发现前者比后者干净，对房子的毁损也少。我们还能要到更高的价钱。我们在第十九街（Nineteenth Street）有一栋廉租公寓，两间房我们收 10 美元；而先前从白人房客那里，这样的房至多收不到 7.50 美元。我们账上在第三十三街（Thirty-third Street）、第六大道与第七大道之间有一栋四层廉租公寓，每层四间房——一间客厅、两间卧室、一间厨房。一层我们收 20 美元，二层 24 美元，三层 23 美元，四层 20 美元，合计 87 美元，即每年 1,044 美元。楼房的占地尺寸不过 21+55。」另一家公司宣称，在一个指明的实例中，自打把白人房客换成有色房客，总租金上省下了百分之十五到二十。又有一家举出如下案例：一栋前楼带后楼的廉租公寓，从前住着「下等欧洲型」的房客，因习性龌龊、交租疲沓而被扫地出门。黑人房客证明自己更干净、更好、更安稳。然而他们的房租非但没有因此得到减免，两相对照竟是如下：

===Original===
An increased rental of $17 per month, or $204 a year, and an advance of nearly thirteen and one-half percent on the gross rental “in favor” of the colored tenant. Profitable, surely!

===Chinese===
月租增加 17 美元，合每年 204 美元；总租金上涨将近百分之十三点五——这便是「有利于」有色房客的优待。有利可图，可不是么！

===Original===
I have quoted these cases at length in order to let in light on the quality of this landlord despotism that has purposely confused the public mind, and for its own selfish ends is propping up a waning prejudice. It will be cause for congratulation if indeed its time has come at last. Within a year, I am told by one of the most intelligent and best informed of our colored citizens, there has been evidence, simultaneous with the colored hegira from the low downtown tenements, of a movement toward less exorbitant rents. I cannot pass from this subject without adding a leaf from my own experience that deserves a place in this record, though, for the credit of humanity, I hope as an extreme case. It was last Christmas that I had occasion to visit the home of an old colored woman in Sixteenth Street, as the almoner of generous friends out of town who wished me to buy her a Christmas dinner. The old woman lived in a wretched shanty, occupying two mean, dilapidated rooms at the top of a sort of hen-ladder that went by the name of stairs. For these she paid ten dollars a month out of her hard-earned wages as a scrub-woman. I did not find her in and, being informed that she was “at the agent’s,” went around to hunt her up. The agent’s wife appeared, to report that Ann was out. Being in a hurry it occurred to me that I might save time by making her employer the purveyor of my friend’s bounty, and proposed to entrust the money, two dollars, to her to be expended for Old Ann’s benefit. She fell in with the suggestion at once, and confided to me in the fullness of her heart that she liked the plan, inasmuch as “I generally find her a Christmas dinner myself, and this money﻿—she owes Mr. —﻿—﻿— (her husband, the agent) a lot of rent.” Needless to state that there was a change of programme then and there, and that Ann was saved from the sort of Christmas cheer that woman’s charity would have spread before her. When I had the old soul comfortably installed in her own den, with a chicken and “fixin’s” and a bright fire in her stove, I asked her how much she owed of her rent. Her answer was that she did not really owe anything, her month not being quite up, but that the amount yet unpaid was﻿—two dollars!

===Chinese===
我不惮其烦地援引这些案例，为的是让光照见这种房东专制的底细——它存心搅浑公众的头脑，又为一己之私利撑持着一桩日见凋萎的偏见。它的时日若果真到了头，那倒是值得称幸的事。我们一位最有智识、消息最灵通的有色公民告诉我：一年之内，随着有色人种从下城低等廉租公寓里成批迁离，房租已同时显出回落的动向。这个题目我不能过门不入，还要从自己的经历里添上一页——它在这份记录中理应占一个位置，尽管为人类的体面计，我但愿它只是极端的孤例。去年圣诞节，我有事登门拜访第十六街（Sixteenth Street）一位老黑人妇女的家——几位外地的慷慨朋友委我做他们的施赈人，想让我替她置办一顿圣诞晚餐。老太太住在一座破败的棚屋里，占着顶上两间寒碜破烂的屋子，爬上去要走一道号称楼梯的鸡梯。就为这两间屋，她每月从擦洗女工（scrub-woman）的血汗工资里掏出十美元。我上门没碰见人，听说她「到中介家去了」，便绕过去找她。出面的是中介太太，来通报说安不在家。我赶时辰，忽然想到不妨让她的东家来充当朋友善举的采办，也好省些工夫，便提议把那两块钱托付给她，为老安置办圣诞吃食。她当即赞成这个主意，还掏心窝子告诉我，说她喜欢这个安排，因为「往年的圣诞晚餐，一般总是我自己给她置办的，而这笔钱嘛——她欠———先生（她丈夫，就是那位中介）好些房租呢。」不必说，章程当场便改了，安也因此得免于领受那位太太的慈善要给她张罗的那一路「圣诞快活」。等我把这把老骨头安顿得舒舒服服，坐在她自己的小窝里，炉子里火光明亮，一只鸡和「配料」（“fixin's”，佐餐配菜）都摆了上来，我问她房租欠了多少。她的回答是：其实一个子儿也不欠，她这个月还没有住满；只是尚未付讫的数目是——两美元！

===Original===
Poverty, abuse, and injustice alike the negro accepts with imperturbable cheerfulness. His philosophy is of the kind that has no room for repining. Whether he lives in an Eighth Ward barrack or in a tenement with a brownstone front and pretensions to the title of “flat,” he looks at the sunny side of life and enjoys it. He loves fine clothes and good living a good deal more than he does a bank account. The proverbial rainy day it would be rank ingratitude, from his point of view, to look for when the sun shines unclouded in a clear sky. His home surroundings, except when he is utterly depraved, reflect his blithesome temper. The poorest negro housekeeper’s room in New York is bright with gaily-colored prints of his beloved “Abe Linkum,” General Grant, President Garfield, Mrs. Cleveland, and other national celebrities, and cheery with flowers and singing birds. In the art of putting the best foot foremost, of disguising his poverty by making a little go a long way, our negro has no equal. When a fair share of prosperity is his, he knows how to make life and home very pleasant to those about him. Pianos and parlor furniture abound in the uptown homes of colored tenants and give them a very prosperous air. But even where the wolf howls at the door, he makes a bold and gorgeous front. The amount of “style” displayed on fine Sundays on Sixth and Seventh Avenues by colored holidaymakers would turn a pessimist black with wrath. The negro’s great ambition is to rise in the social scale to which his color has made him a stranger and an outsider, and he is quite willing to accept the shadow for the substance where that is the best he can get. The claw-hammer coat and white tie of a waiter in a first-class summer hotel, with the chance of taking his ease in six months of winter, are to him the next best thing to mingling with the white quality he serves, on equal terms. His festive gatherings, preeminently his cakewalks, at which a sugared and frosted cake is the proud prize of the couple with the most aristocratic step and carriage, are comic mixtures of elaborate ceremonial and the joyous abandon of the natural man. With all his ludicrous incongruities, his sensuality and his lack of moral accountability, his superstition and other faults that are the effect of temperament and of centuries of slavery, he has his eminently good points. He is loyal to the backbone, proud of being an American and of his newfound citizenship. He is at least as easily moulded for good as for evil. His churches are crowded to the doors on Sunday nights when the colored colony turns out to worship. His people own church property in this city upon which they have paid half a million dollars out of the depth of their poverty, with comparatively little assistance from their white brethren. He is both willing and anxious to learn, and his intellectual status is distinctly improving. If his emotions are not very deeply rooted, they are at least sincere while they last, and until the tempter gets the upper hand again.

===Chinese===
贫穷、虐待与不公，黑人一概以不为所动的快活领受。他的人生哲学里没有自怨自艾的余地。无论住在第八选区（Eighth Ward）的营房式大楼里，还是住在带褐砂石门面、僭称「公寓」（flat）的廉租公寓里，他都盯着生活向阳的一面，并且受用不尽。他爱漂亮衣裳、爱优裕生活，远胜过爱银行存款。谚语里念叨的那个雨天——在他的道理里，晴空万里、日头正亮的时候便去巴望它，未免是十足的忘恩负义。他的居家环境，除非他已堕落得不可救药，处处映照出他那副乐天知命的脾气。纽约最穷的黑人当家人的屋里，也亮堂堂挂着彩色鲜亮的画像：他心爱的「亚伯·林肯」（“Abe Linkum”，黑人方言里的昵称）、格兰特将军（General Grant）、加菲尔德总统（President Garfield）、克利夫兰夫人（Mrs. Cleveland），还有别的一众国家名流；鲜花与啼鸟也给屋里添着生趣。在把最好的那只脚迈在前头、以一块钱当三块花来遮掩贫穷这门艺术上，我们的黑人无出其右。一旦一份像样的宽裕到手，他便晓得把日子和家宅经营得让身边人皆大欢喜。上城有色房客的宅子里，钢琴与客厅家什比比皆是，透出一派兴旺气象。可哪怕饿狼已在门边嚎叫，他也要撑出一副堂皇鲜亮的门面。晴好的星期天，第六大道（Sixth Avenue）与第七大道上有色游人摆出的那份「排场」，能把悲观主义者活活气得脸色发黑。黑人的大志，是在社会的阶梯上往上走——他的肤色已使他在这架阶梯上沦为生人与局外之人；既然影子便是他能到手的至多，他便十分情愿拿影子当实体。一流夏季旅馆侍者的燕尾服（claw-hammer coat）与白领结，外加冬季六个月里赋闲享福的指望，在他看来，是仅次于同他所伺候的白人上流平起平坐、过从往来的次等美事。他的节庆聚会，尤其他的蛋糕舞（cakewalk）——舞步与仪态最具贵族气派的那一对，把一块挂霜撒糖的蛋糕捧作骄傲的彩头——是繁文缛礼与天然人之纵情狂欢的一种滑稽混合。任凭他有一身令人失笑的自相抵牾，有他的耽于肉欲、他的道德责任感的缺欠、他的迷信，以及其他种种出自禀性与几百年奴役的过失，他自有他分外可取的好处。他忠诚彻骨，以身为美国人、以新得的公民身份自豪。他向善的易塑，至少不亚于向恶。礼拜天晚上，有色聚居区倾巢而出做礼拜，他的教堂场场挤到门口。他的族人在本城拥有教会产业，那是他们从贫穷的深底里掏出五十万美元付下的，白人教友的援助相比之下寥寥无几。他既肯学又急学，他的智识境况正在明显改善。他的情感纵然扎根不深，至少存续之际是真诚的——直到诱惑者重新占了上风。

===Original===
Of all the temptations that beset him, the one that troubles him and the police most is his passion for gambling. The game of policy is a kind of unlawful penny lottery specially adapted to his means, but patronized extensively by poor white players as well. It is the meanest of swindles, but reaps for its backers rich fortunes wherever colored people congregate. Between the fortune-teller and the policy shop, closely allied frauds always, the wages of many a hard day’s work are wasted by the negro; but the loss causes him few regrets. Penniless, but with undaunted faith in his ultimate “luck,” he looks forward to the time when he shall once more be able to take a hand at “beating policy.” When periodically the negro’s lucky numbers, 4‒11‒44, come out on the slips of the alleged daily drawings, that are supposed to be held in some far-off Western town, intense excitement reigns in Thompson Street and along the Avenue, where someone is always the winner. An immense impetus is given then to the bogus business that has no existence outside of the cigar stores and candy shops where it hides from the law, save in some cunning Bowery “broker’s” back office, where the slips are printed and the “winnings” apportioned daily with due regard to the backer’s interests.

===Chinese===
在围困他的种种诱惑里，最叫他本人和警察头疼的，是他的赌瘾。「波利」彩票（policy）是一种非法的分币抽彩，专为他的钱袋量体裁衣，但穷苦的白人赌客也大量光顾。这是骗局中最下作的一种，但只要有色人种聚居的地方，它就替后台敛来滚滚横财。在算命婆与彩票店——两者向来是串通一气的骗局——之间，黑人把许多个苦日子挣来的工钱挥霍一空；可这笔损失并不叫他有多少懊恼。身无分文，却对最终的「手气」怀着百折不挠的信心，他巴望着有朝一日重新下场，去「打赢波利」。每隔一段时日，当黑人的幸运号码 4‒11‒44 在字条上开出——那号称每日一开的彩，据信设在某个遥远的西部城镇——汤普森街（Thompson Street）和大道沿线便一片鼎沸，那边总有某个人是赢家。于是这桩冒牌生意便得到一股强劲的推力——它除了藏身躲法的雪茄店和糖果铺，本无别的存在，唯一的例外是鲍厄里（the Bowery）某位狡狯「经纪人」的后屋写字间：字条在那里开印，「彩金」也每日在那里分派，并且对后台老板的利益给予应有的照拂。

===Original===
It is a question whether “Africa” has been improved by the advent of the Italian, with the tramp from the Mulberry Street Bend in his train. The moral turpitude of Thompson Street has been notorious for years, and the mingling of the three elements does not seem to have wrought any change for the better. The borderland where the white and black races meet in common debauch, the aptly-named black-and-tan saloon, has never been debatable ground from a moral standpoint. It has always been the worst of the desperately bad. Than this commingling of the utterly depraved of both sexes, white and black, on such ground, there can be no greater abomination. Usually it is some foul cellar dive, perhaps run by the political “leader” of the district, who is “in with” the police. In any event it gathers to itself all the lawbreakers and all the human wrecks within reach. When a fight breaks out during the dance a dozen razors are handy in as many bootlegs, and there is always a job for the surgeon and the ambulance. The black “tough” is as handy with the razor in a fight as his peaceably inclined brother is with it in pursuit of his honest trade. As the Chinaman hides his knife in his sleeve and the Italian his stiletto in the bosom, so the negro goes to the ball with a razor in his bootleg, and on occasion does as much execution with it as both of the others together. More than three-fourths of the business the police have with the colored people in New York arises in the black-and-tan district, now no longer fairly representative of their color.

===Chinese===
「非洲」（Africa）随着意大利人的到来——桑树街弯道（Mulberry Street Bend）的流浪汉又尾随于后——究竟是变好了还是变坏了，还是一个疑问。汤普森街的道德败坏多年来声名狼藉，三种成分的杂处似乎也没有带来任何起色。白黑两族在共同的荒淫里碰头的边地——那名号起得再贴切不过的黑白混杂酒馆（black-and-tan saloon）——在道德的立场上从来不是什么有待商榷的地界。它向来是坏到绝境里的最坏者。两性俱全、黑白掺杂、彻头彻尾的堕落者在这样的地面上交混杂处，天下再没有比这更可憎的丑物。它通常是某个污秽的地窖酒窟，开铺的兴许是本区的政治「头目」，同警察「沾亲带故」。无论如何，它总把够得着的一切违法之徒和一切人间残骸聚拢到自己身边。舞场里斗殴一起，十几只靴筒里便有十几把剃刀应手可取，外科医生和救护车也从不愁没有活干。黑人「恶棍」在斗殴里耍剃刀的娴熟，不输于他那爱好和平的兄弟拿剃刀操持本分营生的娴熟。正如华人（Chinaman，当时用语）把刀藏进袖筒、意大利人把匕首揣进怀里，黑人也是靴筒里别着剃刀去赴舞会，情急起来，一把剃刀的杀伤抵得上前两者加在一处。警察在纽约同有色人种打的交道，超过四分之三出在这黑白混杂地带——如今它已不复能公允地代表他们的肤色。

===Original===
I have touched briefly upon such facts in the negro’s life as may serve to throw light on the social condition of his people in New York. If, when the account is made up between the races, it shall be claimed that he falls short of the result to be expected from twenty-five years of freedom, it may be well to turn to the other side of the ledger and see how much of the blame is borne by the prejudice and greed that have kept him from rising under a burden of responsibility to which he could hardly be equal. And in this view he may be seen to have advanced much farther and faster than before suspected, and to promise, after all, with fair treatment, quite as well as the rest of us, his white-skinned fellow-citizens, had any right to expect.

===Chinese===
我已约略记下黑人生活中的若干事实，或可借此照亮他的族人在纽约的社会境况。倘若日后种族之间清算总账，有人指摘他辜负了二十五年自由应可期待的成果，那就不妨把账簿翻到另一面，看看这份罪责有多少该由偏见与贪婪承担——正是它们不让他起身，偏又把一副他几乎担不起的责任压在他身上。循着这个眼光看去，他或许比先前所疑心的走得更远、更快；而且归根结底，只要得到公道的对待，他所许下的前程，同我们其余这些人——他的白皮肤同胞——原本有权期待的，也相差无几。

## XIV The Common Herd / XIV 众生芸芸

===Original===
There is another line not always so readily drawn in the tenements, yet the real boundary line of the Other Half: the one that defines the “flat.” The law does not draw it at all, accounting all flats tenements without distinction. The health officer draws it from observation, lumping all those which in his judgment have nothing, or not enough, to give them claim upon the name, with the common herd, and his way is, perhaps, on the whole, the surest and best. The outside of the building gives no valuable clue. Brass and brownstone go well sometimes with dense crowds and dark and dingy rooms; but the first attempt to enter helps draw the line with tolerable distinctness. A locked door is a strong point in favor of the flat. It argues that the first step has been taken to secure privacy, the absence of which is the chief curse of the tenement. Behind a locked door the hoodlum is not at home, unless there be a jailor in place of a janitor to guard it. Not that the janitor and the doorbell are infallible. There may be a tenement behind a closed door; but never a “flat” without it. The hall that is a highway for all the world by night and by day is the tenement’s proper badge. The Other Half ever receives with open doors.

===Chinese===
廉租公寓里还有另一条界线，它不总是那么容易划出，却是「另一半人」真正的边界线：界定「公寓」（flat）的那一条。法律压根不划这条线，把一切公寓不分彼此地都算作廉租公寓。卫生官凭观察来划：凡照他的判断全然够不上、或不足以够上这个名号的，一概归入芸芸众生一类；他这法子，或许总的说来是最稳当也最好的。建筑的外观提供不出什么有价值的线索。黄铜门面、褐石墙身，有时同稠密的人潮、幽暗邋遢的房间正相般配；可是头一回想要进门，就帮着把这条线划得相当分明了。一把上了锁的门，是公寓一方的有力凭据。它说明保障隐私的第一步已经迈出——隐私的缺失，正是廉租公寓的头号祸根。上了锁的门后，小流氓（hoodlum）无家可安，除非有个狱卒顶了看门人的位置替它把守。倒不是说有看门人和门铃便万无一失。关着的门后头也许仍是一所廉租公寓；可不关门的「公寓」却绝不存在。那条昼夜任大千世界穿行的过道，正是廉租公寓名副其实的徽章。「另一半人」永远敞门迎客。

===Original===
With this introduction we shall not seek it long anywhere in the city. Below Houston Street the doorbell in our age is as extinct as the dodo. East of Second Avenue, and west of Ninth Avenue as far up as the Park, it is practically an unknown institution. The nearer the river and the great workshops the more numerous the tenements. The kind of work carried on in any locality to a large extent determines their character. Skilled and well-paid labor puts its stamp on a tenement even in spite of the open door, and usually soon supplies the missing bell. Gas-houses, slaughterhouses and the docks, that attract the roughest crowds and support the vilest saloons, invariably form slum-centres. The city is full of such above the line of Fourteenth Street, that is erroneously supposed by some to fence off the good from the bad, separate the chaff from the wheat. There is nothing below that line that can outdo in wickedness Hell’s Kitchen, in the region of three-cent whiskey, or its counterpoise at the other end of Thirty-ninth Street, on the East River, the home of the infamous Rag Gang. Cherry Street is not “tougher” than Battle Row in East Sixty-third Street, or “the village” at Twenty-ninth Street and First Avenue, where stores of broken bricks, ammunition for the nightly conflicts with the police, are part of the regulation outfit of every tenement. The Mulberry Street Bend is scarce dirtier than Little Italy in Harlem. Even across the Harlem River, Frog Hollow challenges the admiration of the earlier slums for the boldness and pernicious activity of its home gang. There are enough of these sore spots. We shall yet have occasion to look into the social conditions of some of them; were I to draw a picture of them here as they are, the subject, I fear, would outgrow alike the limits of this book and the reader’s patience.

===Chinese===
有了这番引子，在这座城市里无论何处，我们都无须久寻此物。休斯敦街（Houston Street）以南，门铃在我们这个时代已同渡渡鸟一样绝迹。第二大道以东，以及第九大道向西直达公园的地界，它实际上是一种闻所未闻的制度。离河流和大工场越近，廉租公寓便越密集。一地所营的活计，在很大程度上决定它们的品性。有手艺、工钱优的劳力，纵然门还敞着，也会在廉租公寓上打上自己的印记，而且通常很快就补齐那只缺失的门铃。煤气厂、屠宰场和码头招来最粗野的人群，养活最下流的酒馆，无一例外地构成贫民窟的中心。第十四街（Fourteenth Street）一线以北，这样的贫民窟满城皆是——有些人想当然地以为这条街把好人与坏人隔开、把糠秕与麦粒分清，其实大谬不然。这条线以南，没有任何地方能在邪恶上胜过卖三分钱威士忌的地狱厨房（Hell's Kitchen），也胜不过它在第三十九街另一端、东河之滨的对手——声名狼藉的「破布帮」（Rag Gang）的老巢。论「横」，樱桃街不比东六十三街的「厮杀排」（Battle Row）更甚，也不比第二十九街与第一大道交口的「村子」更凶——在那里，囤着的一堆堆碎砖头，也就是每夜同警察开仗的弹药，是每所廉租公寓的标配行头。桑树街弯道未必比哈莱姆的「小意大利」（Little Italy）更脏。就连哈莱姆河对岸，「蛙洼」（Frog Hollow）的本土帮派也以它的胆大妄为、为祸之勤，向早期的贫民窟叫板，争夺人们的叹服。这样的溃疡点已经够多了。其中几处的社会状况，我们往后还有机会细看；倘若我在这里就照实把它们描画一番，只怕这个题目要同时超出本书的篇幅和读者的耐性。

===Original===
It is true that they tell only one side of the story; that there is another to tell. A story of thousands of devoted lives, laboring earnestly to make the most of their scant opportunities for good; of heroic men and women striving patiently against fearful odds and by their very courage coming off victors in the battle with the tenement; of womanhood pure and undefiled. That it should blossom in such an atmosphere is one of the unfathomable mysteries of life. And yet it is not an uncommon thing to find sweet and innocent girls, singularly untouched by the evil around them, true wives and faithful mothers, literally “like jewels in a swine’s snout,” in the worst of the infamous barracks. It is the experience of all who have intelligently observed this side of life in a great city, not to be explained﻿—unless on the theory of my friend, the priest in the Mulberry Street Bend, that inherent purity revolts instinctively from the naked brutality of vice as seen in the slums﻿—but to be thankfully accepted as the one gleam of hope in an otherwise hopeless desert.

===Chinese===
诚然，这些只讲了故事的一面；另一面也有可讲的。那是成千上万颗赤诚生命的故事：他们兢兢业业，把手中微薄的行善之机用到了极致；是英勇的男男女女的故事：他们怀着惊人的耐心对抗悬殊的命运，单凭那股勇气，便在与廉租公寓的搏斗中得了胜；是纯洁无玷的女性品格的故事。这样的品格居然能在这样的气氛里开花，是人生无从索解的奥秘之一。然而，在最臭名昭著的营房式危楼的最深处，居然不乏甜美天真的姑娘，出淤泥而几乎一尘不染，做贤妻、为良母——名副其实地「如同金环戴在猪鼻上」。凡曾在大城市里留心观察过生活这一面的人，都有同样的经验：此事无从解释——除非采信我那位桑树街弯道的神父朋友的理论，说人天生的纯贞，对贫民窟里那种赤裸裸的兽性邪恶，自会本能地起而拒斥——而只能心怀感激地领受它，把它当作一片绝望荒漠中唯一的一线希望。

===Original===
But the relief is not great.
