In the dull content of life bred on the tenement-house dead level there is little to redeem it, or to calm apprehension for a society that has nothing better to offer its toilers; while the patient efforts of the lives finally attuned to it to render the situation tolerable, and the very success of these efforts, serve only to bring out in stronger contrast the general gloom of the picture by showing how much farther they might have gone with half a chance. Go into any of the “respectable” tenement neighborhoods﻿—the fact that there are not more than two saloons on the corner, nor over three or four in the block will serve as a fair guide﻿—where live the great body of hardworking Irish and German immigrants and their descendants, who accept naturally the conditions of tenement life, because for them there is nothing else in New York; be with and among its people until you understand their ways, their aims, and the quality of their ambitions, and unless you can content yourself with the scriptural promise that the poor we shall have always with us, or with the menagerie view that, if fed, they have no cause of complaint, you shall come away agreeing with me that, humanly speaking, life there does not seem worth the living. Take at random one of these uptown tenement blocks, not of the worst nor yet of the most prosperous kind, within hail of what the newspapers would call a “fine residential section.” These houses were built since the last cholera scare made people willing to listen to reason. The block is not like the one over on the East Side in which I actually lost my way once. There were thirty or forty rear houses in the heart of it, three or four on every lot, set at all sorts of angles, with odd, winding passages, or no passage at all, only “runways” for the thieves and toughs of the neighborhood. These yards are clear. There is air there, and it is about all there is. The view between brick walls outside is that of a stony street; inside, of rows of unpainted board fences, a bewildering maze of clothes-posts and lines; underfoot, a desert of brown, hard-baked soil from which every blade of grass, every stray weed, every speck of green, has been trodden out, as must inevitably be every gentle thought and aspiration above the mere wants of the body in those whose moral natures such home surroundings are to nourish. In self-defence, you know, all life eventually accommodates itself to its environment, and human life is no exception. Within the house there is nothing to supply the want thus left unsatisfied. Tenement-houses have no aesthetic resources. If any are to be brought to bear on them, they must come from the outside. There is the common hall with doors opening softly on every landing as the strange step is heard on the stairs, the air-shaft that seems always so busy letting out foul stenches from below that it has no time to earn its name by bringing down fresh air, the squeaking pumps that hold no water, and the rent that is never less than one week’s wages out of the four, quite as often half of the family earnings.

===Chinese===
可是这点宽慰并不算多。在廉租公寓一拉到底的死水准上养出来的那种钝然的安分里，几乎没有什么可赎之处，也无法让人对一个只拿得出这个来给劳力者的社会释然无忧；至于那些终于调适到此境的生命，他们那份把局面熬到可以忍受的坚忍努力，连同这些努力的成功本身，也无非是把整幅图画的阴沉衬托得更加刺目：它们表明，只要有半点机会，他们本来还能走出多远。随便走进一处「体面的」廉租公寓街区——街角上的酒馆不超过两家、整条街区里不超过三四家，这一条大可当作可靠的向导——勤劳的爱尔兰和德国移民及其后代的大多数便住在那里；他们自然而然地接受了廉租公寓生活的种种境况，因为在纽约，除此之外他们别无所有。同这些人相处，住到他们中间去，直到你懂得他们的做派、他们的奔头、他们抱负的成色；那时，除非你能拿圣经的应许自安——说穷人永远同我们在一处——或者抱着动物园的观点，认为只要喂饱了他们便无可抱怨，否则你离开时必会同我一样承认：就人情常理而言，那里的生活似乎不值得过。随手续一段上城的廉租公寓街区来看，不好不坏的那种，离报纸上所谓的「上等住宅区」一箭之遥。这些房子是上一次霍乱恐慌叫人肯听劝之后盖起来的。这个街区不像下东区那个我当真在里面迷过一回路的街区：那里面挤着三四十所后院廉租公寓，每块宅地上三四所，歪歪扭扭排成各种角度，夹着古里古怪的九曲过道，或者干脆没有过道，只有供本地窃贼与恶棍使用的「跑道」。这边的院子是敞亮的。那里有空气，而且差不多也就只有空气了。砖墙之外，映入眼帘的是一条石头街；墙内，是一排排没刷过漆的木板栅栏，晾衣柱与晾衣绳织成的迷宫让人眼晕；脚下，是一片烤得坚硬的褐色土荒漠，每一茎草、每一株野草、每一点绿色都已被踩灭——正如在这种家居环境中滋养道德天性的人们心里，每一缕超出肉体需求的温柔心念与向上之志，也必然要被踩灭一样。要知道，出于自卫，一切生命终究会去迁就它的环境，人命也不例外。屋子里面，没有任何东西可以填补由此留下的亏空。廉租公寓没有审美的资源。若有可言，也必得从外面带进来。那里有公共过道，楼梯上一响起生人的脚步，每一层平台的门便随着悄没声地开一条缝；有那似乎永远忙着从底下放臭气、没工夫靠送下新鲜空气来挣得自己名号的通风井（air-shaft）；有吱吱作响却存不住水的泵；还有房租——四个星期的工钱，它从不肯少占去一个星期，而且往往要占到全家收入的一半。

===Original===
Why complete the sketch? It is drearily familiar already. Such as it is, it is the frame in which are set days, weeks, months, and years of unceasing toil, just able to fill the mouth and clothe the back. Such as it is, it is the world, and all of it, to which these weary workers return nightly to feed heart and brain after wearing out the body at the bench, or in the shop. To it come the young with their restless yearnings, perhaps to pass on the threshold one of the daughters of sin, driven to the tenement by the police when they raided her den, sallying forth in silks and fine attire after her day of idleness. These in their coarse garments﻿—girls with the love of youth for beautiful things, with this hard life before them﻿—who shall save them from the tempter? Down in the street the saloon, always bright and gay, gathering to itself all the cheer of the block, beckons the boys. In many such blocks the census-taker found two thousand men, women, and children, and over, who called them home.

===Chinese===
何必把这幅速写补完？它早已令人厌倦地眼熟了。就是这样一副框架，嵌着日复一日、周复一周、月复一月、年复一年永不停歇的苦役，挣的刚够糊口蔽体。就是这样一副框架，就是这样一个世界，而且是全部的世界——疲惫的工人们白天在长凳上、在作坊里磨垮了身子，夜里回到这里来喂养心与脑。带着躁动渴望的年轻人也来到这里，也许刚跨进门槛，就同罪恶的女儿擦肩而过——警察查抄了她的巢穴，把她赶到廉租公寓里来，此刻她闲散了一天，正穿着绸缎盛装出门去。而这些粗布衣裳的姑娘——怀少女爱美之心的姑娘，面前摆着这样一场硬人生的姑娘——谁能救她们脱离那诱骗者？街那头的酒馆，永远明亮欢畅，把整条街区的欢快都聚到自己身上，正向男孩子们招手。人口普查员在这样的街区里数出过两千乃至更多的男人、女人和孩子，他们都管这里叫家。

===Original===
The picture is faithful enough to stand for its class wherever along both rivers the Irish brogue is heard. As already said, the Celt falls most readily victim to tenement influences since shantytown and its original free-soilers have become things of the past. If he be thrifty and shrewd his progress thenceforward is along the plane of the tenement, on which he soon assumes to manage without improving things. The German has an advantage over his Celtic neighbor in his strong love for flowers, which not all the tenements on the East Side have power to smother. His garden goes with him wherever he goes. Not that it represents any high moral principle in the man; rather perhaps the capacity for it. He turns his saloon into a shrubbery as soon as his backyard. But wherever he puts it in a tenement block it does the work of a dozen police clubs. In proportion as it spreads the neighborhood takes on a more orderly character. As the green dies out of the landscape and increases in political importance, the police find more to do. Where it disappears altogether from sight, lapsing into a mere sentiment, police-beats are shortened and the force patrols double at night. Neither the man nor the sentiment is wholly responsible for this. It is the tenement unadorned that is. The changing of Tompkins Square from a sand lot into a beautiful park put an end for good and all to the Bread and Blood riots of which it used to be the scene, and transformed a nest of dangerous agitators into a harmless, beer-craving band of Anarchists. They have scarcely been heard of since. Opponents of the small parks system as a means of relieving the congested population of tenement districts, please take note.

===Chinese===
这幅图画足够忠实，凡是两条河沿岸听得到爱尔兰土腔的地方，它都足以充当本阶级的写照。如前所述，自从棚户区连同它最早的「自由垦荒者」俱成陈迹，凯尔特人便最容易沦为廉租公寓势力的牺牲品。他若节俭而精明，此后的进取便沿着廉租公寓这一层面展开，而且不久便摆出管理者的做派，却不去改善什么。德国人比他的凯尔特邻居占一着优势：他对花草的强烈爱好，不是下东区全部的廉租公寓都压抑得住的。他的花园随他走到哪里跟到哪里。倒不是说这在人身上体现着什么高尚的道德原则；体现的毋宁是容纳它的那份禀赋。他把酒馆变成灌木林，同把后院变成灌木林一样勤快。可是不管他把它安插到哪个廉租公寓街区，它都顶得上十二根警棍。它铺开多少，街坊便相应添多少规矩。景观里的绿色凋零多少、它在政治上的分量便增长多少，警察也就有多少事可做。凡绿色从视野里绝迹、蜕化为一种单纯感伤的地方，警察的巡区就得划小，夜里的巡逻力量就得加倍。这种局面，人也好、感伤也好，都负不了全责。要负责的是那不加装饰的廉租公寓本身。汤普金斯广场（Tompkins Square）从一片沙地改造成美丽的公园，一劳永逸地终结了曾以它为舞台的「面包与鲜血」暴乱（Bread and Blood riots），把一窝危险的鼓动家改造成一伙无害的、只惦记啤酒的无政府主义者。从那以后，他们几乎销声匿迹。反对以小公园体系疏解廉租公寓街区拥挤人口的诸君，请就此记下一笔。

===Original===
With the first hot nights in June police despatches, that record the killing of men and women by rolling off roofs and windowsills while asleep, announce that the time of greatest suffering among the poor is at hand. It is in hot weather, when life indoors is well-nigh unbearable with cooking, sleeping, and working, all crowded into the small rooms together, that the tenement expands, reckless of all restraint. Then a strange and picturesque life moves upon the flat roofs. In the day and early evening mothers air their babies there, the boys fly their kites from the housetops, undismayed by police regulations, and the young men and girls court and pass the growler. In the stifling July nights, when the big barracks are like fiery furnaces, their very walls giving out absorbed heat, men and women lie in restless, sweltering rows, panting for air and sleep. Then every truck in the street, every crowded fire-escape, becomes a bedroom, infinitely preferable to any the house affords. A cooling shower on such a night is hailed as a heaven-sent blessing in a hundred thousand homes.

===Chinese===
六月里头几个闷热的夜晚，警务电讯记下熟睡中从屋顶、从窗台上滚落摔死的男男女女，宣告穷人最吃苦的时节到了。正是在炎热的天气里，做饭、睡觉、做工全挤在小屋里，屋里的生活简直没法忍受，廉租公寓便不顾一切约束地膨胀开来。于是一种奇特而富于画意的生命在平屋顶上活动起来。白天和傍晚，母亲们在屋顶上给婴儿透风，男孩子们无视警察的规章从房顶上放风筝，年轻男女在那里谈情说爱、传递「打酒桶」（growler）。在七月窒闷的夜里，大楼房热得像烧红的炉子，连墙壁都在吐出白天吸饱的热气，男男女女一排排焦躁地躺着，汗出如浆，喘着气乞求空气和睡眠。那时，街上每一辆卡车、每一架挤满人的太平梯（fire-escape）都成了卧室，比屋里拿得出的任何一处都强出千倍百倍。这样的夜里来一阵清凉的阵雨，十万家人家都要把它当作上天的恩典来欢迎。

===Original===
Life in the tenements in July and August spells death to an army of little ones whom the doctor’s skill is powerless to save. When the white badge of mourning flutters from every second door, sleepless mothers walk the streets in the gray of the early dawn, trying to stir a cooling breeze to fan the brow of the sick baby. There is no sadder sight than this patient devotion striving against fearfully hopeless odds. Fifty “summer doctors,” especially trained to this work, are then sent into the tenements by the Board of Health, with free advice and medicine for the poor. Devoted women follow in their track with care and nursing for the sick. Fresh-air excursions run daily out of New York on land and water; but despite all efforts the gravediggers in Calvary work overtime, and little coffins are stacked mountains high on the deck of the Charity Commissioners’ boat when it makes its semiweekly trips to the city cemetery.

===Chinese===
七八月间，廉租公寓里的生活对一大军小孩子来说就意味着死亡，医生的妙手也无力回天。当白色的丧徽在每隔一扇门上飘动的时候，彻夜无眠的母亲们在灰白的黎明里走上街头，想靠走动扇起一缕凉风，吹一吹病孩的额头。再没有比这更悲哀的景象了：这般坚忍的慈爱，对抗着可怕到无望的优势。这时，卫生委员会派出五十名专为此职训练有素的「夏季医生」进入廉租公寓，为穷人免费诊病送药。热忱的妇女们跟着他们的脚踪，带来对病人的照料与看护。新鲜空气旅行团每日从纽约出发，陆路水路都有；可是尽管有这一切努力，加尔瓦里墓园（Calvary）的掘墓人仍在加班加点，慈善专员们（Charity Commissioners）的船每逢一周两班开往市立公墓的时候，小棺材在甲板上堆得像山一样高。

===Original===
Under the most favorable circumstances, an epidemic, which the well-to-do can afford to make light of as a thing to be got over or avoided by reasonable care, is excessively fatal among the children of the poor, by reason of the practical impossibility of isolating the patient in a tenement. The measles, ordinarily a harmless disease, furnishes a familiar example. Tread it ever so lightly on the avenues, in the tenements it kills right and left. Such an epidemic ravaged three crowded blocks in Elizabeth Street on the heels of the grippe last winter, and, when it had spent its fury, the death-maps in the Bureau of Vital Statistics looked as if a black hand had been laid across those blocks, overshadowing in part the contiguous tenements in Mott Street, and with the thumb covering a particularly packed settlement of half a dozen houses in Mulberry Street. The track of the epidemic through these teeming barracks was as clearly defined as the track of a tornado through a forest district. There were houses in which as many as eight little children had died in five months. The records showed that respiratory diseases, the common heritage of the grippe and the measles, had caused death in most cases, discovering the trouble to be, next to the inability to check the contagion in those crowds, in the poverty of the parents and the wretched home conditions that made proper care of the sick impossible. The fact was emphasized by the occurrence here and there of a few isolated deaths from diphtheria and scarlet fever. In the case of these diseases, considered more dangerous to the public health, the health officers exercised summary powers of removal to the hospital where proper treatment could be had, and the result was a low death-rate.

===Chinese===
即便在最顺遂的情势下，富人尽可付之一笑、当作小心些就能躲过或熬过去的小事的传染病，在穷人的孩子中间也极其致命，原因就在于：在廉租公寓里把病人隔离开来，实际上是办不到的。麻疹本是一种平常无害的疾病，它提供了一个现成的例子。在大道上它来势再轻，一进廉租公寓便大开杀戒。去年冬天，这样一场时疫踩着流感的脚后跟，横扫了伊丽莎白街（Elizabeth Street）三个拥挤的街区；待它肆虐已尽，生命统计局（Bureau of Vital Statistics）的死亡地图上，那几个街区仿佛被一只黑手横覆住，部分掩及莫特街（Mott Street）比邻的廉租公寓，拇指正压着桑树街（Mulberry Street）上一处格外拥挤的六所房屋的聚居地。时疫穿过这些塞满人的营房的行迹，就像龙卷风穿过森林地带的路径一样分明。有的房子里，五个月中死去的小孩多达八个。档案表明，多数死亡病例的死因是呼吸道疾病——流感与麻疹共同的遗产——由此查明，病根除了无法在这些人群里遏止传染之外，便在于父母的贫穷和悲惨的家况，它们使病孩得不到应有的照料。这里那里零星发生的几起白喉和猩红热死亡，更凸显了这一事实。对这两种被认为对公众健康更危险的病，卫生官行使即决强制移送权，把病人送进能够得到妥善治疗的医院，结果是死亡率很低。

===Original===
These were tenements of the tall, modern type. A little more than a year ago, when a census was made of the tenements and compared with the mortality tables, no little surprise and congratulation was caused by the discovery that as the buildings grew taller the death-rate fell. The reason is plain, though the reverse had been expected by most people. The biggest tenements have been built in the last ten years of sanitary reform rule, and have been brought, in all but the crowding, under its laws. The old houses that from private dwellings were made into tenements, or were run up to house the biggest crowds in defiance of every moral and physical law, can be improved by no device short of demolition. They will ever remain the worst.

===Chinese===
这些是高大的新式廉租公寓。一年多以前，人们对廉租公寓做了一次普查，同死亡率表加以比对，发现楼越高死亡率越低，不免引起不小的惊讶与庆幸。缘故很明白，虽然多数人原先料想的恰是相反。最大的廉租公寓都是在卫生改革当道的最近十年里盖起来的，除拥挤一项之外，都已纳入它的法规之下。而那些由私人住宅改成的廉租公寓，或者罔顾一切道德与自然法则、专为塞进最多人而匆匆赶盖起来的老房子，除非拆掉，没有任何法子可以改良。它们将永远是糟糕透顶的那一类。

===Original===
That ignorance plays its part, as well as poverty and bad hygienic surroundings, in the sacrifice of life is of course inevitable. They go usually hand in hand. A message came one day last spring summoning me to a Mott Street tenement in which lay a child dying from some unknown disease. With the “charity doctor” I found the patient on the top floor, stretched upon two chairs in a dreadfully stifling room. She was gasping in the agony of peritonitis that had already written its death-sentence on her wan and pinched face. The whole family, father, mother, and four ragged children, sat around looking on with the stony resignation of helpless despair that had long since given up the fight against fate as useless. A glance around the wretched room left no doubt as to the cause of the child’s condition. “Improper nourishment,” said the doctor, which, translated to suit the place, meant starvation. The father’s hands were crippled from lead poisoning. He had not been able to work for a year. A contagious disease of the eyes, too long neglected, had made the mother and one of the boys nearly blind. The children cried with hunger. They had not broken their fast that day, and it was then near noon. For months the family had subsisted on two dollars a week from the priest, and a few loaves and a piece of corned beef which the sisters sent them on Saturday. The doctor gave direction for the treatment of the child, knowing that it was possible only to alleviate its sufferings until death should end them, and left some money for food for the rest. An hour later, when I returned, I found them feeding the dying child with ginger ale, bought for two cents a bottle at the pedlar’s cart down the street. A pitying neighbor had proposed it as the one thing she could think of as likely to make the child forget its misery. There was enough in the bottle to go round to the rest of the family. In fact, the wake had already begun; before night it was under way in dead earnest.

===Chinese===
无知同贫穷、同恶劣的卫生环境一样，在这场对生命的牺牲里尽着它的一份，这自然是免不了的。这三者通常携手同行。去年春天的一天，有个口信把我召到莫特街一所廉租公寓，里面躺着一个患不明疾病、垂死的孩子。我同「慈善医生」在顶层找到了病人：在一间闷得可怕的屋子里，她直挺挺躺在两把椅子上，腹膜炎的剧痛折磨得她直喘，那病早已把死刑判决书写上她憔悴干瘦的脸。一家子——父亲、母亲和四个衣衫褴褛的孩子——围坐四周，带着无可奈何、早已认定同命运搏斗无济于事的绝望所炼出的那种铁石般的认命，默默看着。往这间悲惨屋子里扫一眼，对孩子的病根便再无怀疑。「营养不当」，医生说。这话翻译成合乎此地的语言，就是饥饿。父亲的双手因铅中毒而残了，已经一年没能做工。一种拖得太久没治的眼睛传染病，把母亲和一个男孩折磨得几乎失明。孩子们饿得直哭。那天他们还没开过饭，而当时已近正午。几个月来，这一家就靠神父每星期给的两块钱过活，外加修女们星期六送来的几条面包和一块腌牛肉。医生嘱咐了孩子的治法，心里明白所能做的无非减轻她的苦楚、直到死神来终结它，又留下一点钱给其余人买吃的。一个钟头后我回来，看见他们正给垂死的孩子喂姜汁汽水——两分钱一瓶，在街那头的货郎车上买的。一位好心的街坊出的主意：她能想到的、兴许能叫孩子忘掉苦楚的，就数这个了。瓶里的汽水足够全家分一轮。其实，守灵已经开始了；不到天黑，它便一本正经地办将起来。

===Original===
Every once in a while a case of downright starvation gets into the newspapers and makes a sensation. But this is the exception. Were the whole truth known, it would come home to the community with a shock that would rouse it to a more serious effort than the spasmodic undoing of its purse-strings. I am satisfied from my own observation that hundreds of men, women, and children are every day slowly starving to death in the tenements with my medical friend’s complaint of “improper nourishment.” Within a single week I have had this year three cases of insanity, provoked directly by poverty and want. One was that of a mother who in the middle of the night got up to murder her child, who was crying for food; another was the case of an Elizabeth Street truck-driver whom the newspapers never heard of. With a family to provide for, he had been unable to work for many months. There was neither food, nor a scrap of anything upon which money could be raised, left in the house; his mind gave way under the combined physical and mental suffering. In the third case I was just in time with the police to prevent the madman from murdering his whole family. He had the sharpened hatchet in his pocket when we seized him. He was an Irish laborer, and had been working in the sewers until the poisonous gases destroyed his health. Then he was laid off, and scarcely anything had been coming in all winter but the oldest child’s earnings as cash-girl in a store, $2.50 a week. There were seven children to provide for, and the rent of the Mulberry Street attic in which the family lived was $10 a month. They had borrowed as long as anybody had a cent to lend. When at last the man got an odd job that would just buy the children bread, the week’s wages only served to measure the depth of their misery. “It came in so on the tail-end of everything,” said his wife in telling the story, with unconscious eloquence. The outlook worried him through sleepless nights until it destroyed his reason. In his madness he had only one conscious thought: that the town should not take the children. “Better that I take care of them myself,” he repeated to himself as he ground the axe to an edge. Help came in abundance from many almost as poor as they when the desperate straits of the family became known through his arrest. The readiness of the poor to share what little they have with those who have even less is one of the few moral virtues of the tenements. Their enormous crowds touch elbow in a closeness of sympathy that is scarcely to be understood out of them, and has no parallel except among the unfortunate women whom the world scorns as outcasts. There is very little professed sentiment about it to draw a sentimental tear from the eye of romantic philanthropy. The hard fact is that the instinct of self-preservation impels them to make common cause against the common misery.

===Chinese===
隔三差五，一桩彻头彻尾饿死人的案子会登上报纸，闹出一场轰动。可这只是例外。倘若全部真相为人所知，它落到社会头上的一击，会把社会震起来，去做比一阵风似的解开钱袋更认真的努力。我从自己的观察中确信：每天都有成百的男女和孩子，在廉租公寓里顶着那位医生朋友所说的「营养不当」的判词，慢慢地饿死。就在今年，一个星期之内，我经手了三起由贫穷匮乏直接引发的疯病。一起是一位母亲，半夜里起来要去杀死哭着要吃食的孩子；另一起是伊丽莎白街（Elizabeth Street）一个拉货车的，报纸从未听说过他。他要养活一家子，却已经好几个月做不了工。家里吃的没有了，能押出钱来的零星物件也没有一件剩下；肉体和精神的双重痛苦把他压疯了。第三起，我同警察赶到得正是时候，拦下了那个疯子屠杀全家的手。我们抓住他的时候，磨快的短柄斧就在他口袋里。他是个爱尔兰劳工，一直在下水道里做工，直到毒气毁了他的健康。随后他被裁了下来，一冬天几乎没有什么进项，只有最大的孩子在一个铺子里当收银女孩（cash girl），一星期挣 2.50 块。要抚养的孩子有七个，全家住的桑树街（Mulberry Street）阁楼，房租每月 10 块。能借的地方他们都借遍了，只要人家还有一分钱肯借。等到这人终于寻到一件零工，工钱刚够给孩子们买面包，那一周的工钱也只是量出了他们苦难的深浅。「什么都到了末梢，它才这么来了，」他妻子讲起这段事时说，出语无心却自有雄辩。前景熬过一个个不眠之夜熬坏了他，直到毁掉他的理智。疯癫中他只剩一个清醒的念头：不能让公家把孩子们带走。「还是我自己来照管他们好，」他一面把斧头磨出刃，一面反复对自己念叨。他一被捕，全家的绝境传了开去，援手便从许多几乎同他们一样穷的人那里源源而来。穷人乐于把自己仅有的一点点分给更少的人，这是廉租公寓里少有的几样道德美德之一。他们的人潮臂肘相接，那种同情的紧密，置身其外者几乎无从理解，也无可比拟——除了在那些被世人斥为弃妇的不幸女人中间。这里面几乎没有多少标榜出来的感情，引不来浪漫慈善事业的一滴感伤热泪。硬邦邦的事实是：自卫的本能驱使他们结成同盟，共御共同的苦难。

===Original===
No doubt intemperance bears a large share of the blame for it; judging from the standpoint of the policeman perhaps the greater share. Two such entries as I read in the police returns on successive days last March, of mothers in West Side tenements, who, in their drunken sleep, lay upon and killed their infants, go far to support such a position. And they are far from uncommon. But my experience has shown me another view of it, a view which the last report of the Society for Improving the Condition of the Poor seems more than half inclined to adopt in allotting to “intemperance the cause of distress, or distress the cause of intemperance,” forty percent of the cases it is called upon to deal with. Even if it were all true, I should still load over upon the tenement the heaviest responsibility. A single factor, the scandalous scarcity of water in the hot summer when the thirst of the million tenants must be quenched, if not in that in something else, has in the past years more than all other causes encouraged drunkenness among the poor. But to my mind there is a closer connection between the wages of the tenements and the vices and improvidence of those who dwell in them than, with the guilt of the tenement upon our heads, we are willing to admit even to ourselves. Weak tea with a dry crust is not a diet to nurse moral strength. Yet how much better might the fare be expected to be in the family of this “widow with seven children, very energetic and prudent”﻿—I quote again from the report of the Society for the Improvement of the Condition of the Poor﻿—whose “eldest girl was employed as a learner in a tailor’s shop at small wages, and one boy had a place as ‘cash’ in a store. There were two other little boys who sold papers and sometimes earned one dollar. The mother finishes pantaloons and can do three pairs in a day, thus earning thirty-nine cents. Here is a family of eight persons with rent to pay and an income of less than six dollars a week.”

===Chinese===
毫无疑问，纵酒在这件事上负着很大一份罪责；从警察的立足点看，恐怕还是最大的那一份。去年三月，我在警务日报上接连两天读到两条这样的记录：西区（West Side）廉租公寓里的两位母亲，醉睡中压死了自己的婴孩。这样的事例很能支撑上述立场，而且绝非罕见。但我的阅历让我看到了这件事的另一面——贫民状况改善协会（Society for Improving the Condition of the Poor）的最新报告，看来也已大半倾向于采纳这一面：它在经手的案子中，把百分之四十归为『纵酒为困苦之因，或困苦为纵酒之因』。即便这些全是实情，我仍要把最重的责任加到廉租公寓头上。单单一项——盛夏时节水荒得可耻、百万房客的干渴总得有东西浇灭的时候，不浇这个便浇那个——在过去几年里对穷人酗酒的怂恿，就超过了其余一切原因。但在我看来，廉租公寓的工钱同住客的种种恶习与只顾眼前的脾性之间，还有着一层更近的关系——只是廉租公寓之罪还压在我们自己头上，我们连对自己也未必肯承认。稀茶就干面包皮，不是滋养道德力气的伙食。可是，在这位「带着七个孩子、非常勤快也非常精细的寡妇」家里，还能指望伙食好到哪里去呢——我再引一段贫民状况改善协会的报告——「大女儿在一家裁缝铺当学徒，工钱微薄；一个男孩在铺子里当『收银』。还有两个小男孩卖报，有时能挣一块钱。母亲做裤子的收尾工序，一天能做三条，挣三毛九。一家八口，要付房租，每星期进项不到六块钱。」

===Original===
And yet she was better off in point of pay than this Sixth Street mother, who “had just brought home four pairs of pants to finish, at seven cents a pair. She was required to put the canvas in the bottom, basting and sewing three times around; to put the linings in the waistbands; to tack three pockets, three corners to each; to put on two stays and eight buttons, and make six buttonholes; to put the buckle on the back strap and sew on the ticket, all for seven cents.” Better off than the “churchgoing mother of six children,” and with a husband sick to death, who to support the family made shirts, averaging an income of one dollar and twenty cents a week, while her oldest girl, aged thirteen, was “employed downtown cutting out Hamburg edgings at one dollar and a half a week﻿—two and a half cents per hour for ten hours of steady labor﻿—making the total income of the family two dollars and seventy cents per week.” Than the Harlem woman, who was “making a brave effort to support a sick husband and two children by taking in washing at thirty-five cents for the lot of fourteen large pieces, finding coal, soap, starch, and bluing herself, rather than depend on charity in any form.” Specimen wages of the tenements these, seemingly inconsistent with the charge of improvidence.

===Chinese===
然而论工钱，她还是比这位第六街（Sixth Street）的母亲强：后者「刚领了四条裤子回家做收尾，每条七分钱。要求她给裤脚上帆布衬，粗缝加细缝绕三圈；给裤腰上衬里；钉三个口袋，每个口袋缝三个角；上两根裤襻、钉八颗扣子、锁六个扣眼；后腰带上装好带扣，再缝上货牌——统共七分钱。」也比这位「上教堂的、六个孩子的母亲」强：她丈夫病得要死，她为撑起这个家缝衬衫，平均每星期进项一块二毛钱；她十三岁的大女儿「在下城做工，裁剪汉堡花边，一星期一块五——十个钟头不间断的劳作，每小时两分半钱——全家每星期总收入两块七毛钱。」也比这位哈莱姆妇女强：她「正发狠养活害病的丈夫和两个孩子，替人洗衣服，十四大件收三毛五，煤、肥皂、浆粉和蓝靛都自备，宁可如此，也不肯沾任何形式的救济。」这些便是廉租公寓的工钱标本，同只顾眼前的指控放在一起，显得多么格格不入。

===Original===
But the connection on second thought is not obscure. There is nothing in the prospect of a sharp, unceasing battle for the bare necessaries of life, to encourage looking ahead, everything to discourage the effort. Improvidence and wastefulness are natural results. The instalment plan secures to the tenant who lives from hand to mouth his few comforts; the evil day of reckoning is put off till a tomorrow that may never come. When it does come, with failure to pay and the loss of hard-earned dollars, it simply adds another hardship to a life measured from the cradle by such incidents. The children soon catch the spirit of this sort of thing. I remember once calling at the home of a poor washerwoman, living in an East Side tenement, and finding the door locked. Some children in the hallway stopped their play and eyed me attentively while I knocked. The biggest girl volunteered the information that Mrs. Smith was out; but while I was thinking of how I was to get a message to her, the child put a question of her own: “Are you the spring man or the clock man?” When I assured her that I was neither one nor the other, but had brought work for her mother, Mrs. Smith, who had been hiding from the instalment collector, speedily appeared.

===Chinese===
但细想一层，这层关系并不含糊。前景既是一场为糊口之需而起的、尖锐而无休止的搏斗，其中便没有任何东西鼓励人往远处看，倒是一切都在打消这份努力。只顾眼前和大手大脚，是自然而然的结果。分期付款的办法，让吃了上顿没下顿的房客先得着他那几样小小的舒适；清算的凶日，则推迟到一个兴许永不会来的明天。待它当真来了——付不出款、辛苦挣来的血汗钱打了水漂——它无非又给一桩自摇篮起便以这类事故为刻度的人生，添上一重苦难。孩子们很快便沾上了这路事情的精气神。我记得有一回，到下东区一所廉租公寓里一位穷洗衣妇家中拜访，发现门锁着。过道里几个孩子停了玩耍，一面打量我一面听我敲门。最大的女孩主动告诉我，史密斯太太（Mrs. Smith）出去了；可正当我盘算怎么给她捎个信儿的时候，那孩子倒问起我一个她自己的问题：「您是收弹簧床钱的，还是收钟钱的？」我向她保证自己两样都不是，是给她母亲送活计来的；那位一直躲着分期付款收账人的史密斯太太，立刻就露面了。

===Original===
Perhaps of all the disheartening experiences of those who have devoted lives of unselfish thought and effort, and their number is not so small as often supposed, to the lifting of this great load, the indifference of those they would help is the most puzzling. They will not be helped. Dragged by main force out of their misery, they slip back again on the first opportunity, seemingly content only in the old rut. The explanation was supplied by two women of my acquaintance in an Elizabeth Street tenement, whom the city missionaries had taken from their wretched hovel and provided with work and a decent home somewhere in New Jersey. In three weeks they were back, saying that they preferred their dark rear room to the stumps out in the country. But to me the oldest, the mother, who had struggled along with her daughter making cloaks at half a dollar apiece, twelve long years since the daughter’s husband was killed in a street accident and the city took the children, made the bitter confession: “We do get so kind o’ downhearted living this way, that we have to be where something is going on, or we just can’t stand it.” And there was sadder pathos to me in her words than in the whole long story of their struggle with poverty; for unconsciously she voiced the sufferings of thousands, misjudged by a happier world, deemed vicious because they are human and unfortunate.

===Chinese===
在那些把无私的心思与力气奉献给搬掉这座大负担的人——这样的人并不像惯常以为的那么少——的一切令人灰心的阅历当中，最叫人摸不着头脑的，也许莫过于他们想帮助的那些人的冷漠了。他们不肯受助。被连拖带拽地从苦难里拉出来，一有机会又滑回原处，仿佛只有那条旧车辙才待着踏实。这个谜，让伊丽莎白街（Elizabeth Street）一所廉租公寓里我认识的两个女人解开了：城市传教士（city missionaries）把她们从悲惨的破窝里领出来，在新泽西什么地方给她们安排了活计和一处体面的住处。不出三个星期，她们回来了，说她们宁可要自己那间黑后屋，也不要乡下那些光秃秃的树桩。可是那位年长的、做母亲的，对我说出了一句辛酸的招供。自从女婿在一场街头事故中丧命、市里把孩子带走之后，她同女儿挣扎着做女外套、每件挣半块钱，已经熬了漫漫十二年：「这么过日子，人是真会闷得心灰意懒，非得待在有点动静的地方不可，不然简直撑不住。」在我看来，她这句话里的哀伤，比她们同贫穷搏斗的整部长篇故事还要深；因为她不自觉地道出了千千万万人的苦楚——他们被一个更有福的世界误解了，只因他们是人、是不幸的人，便被当作了恶棍。

===Original===
It is a popular delusion, encouraged by all sorts of exaggerated stories when nothing more exciting demands public attention, that there are more evictions in the tenements of New York every year “than in all Ireland.” I am not sure that it is doing much for the tenant to upset this fallacy. To my mind, to be put out of a tenement would be the height of good luck. The fact is, however, that evictions are not nearly as common in New York as supposed. The reason is that in the civil courts, the judges of which are elected in their districts, the tenant-voter has solid ground to stand upon at last. The law that takes his side to start with is usually twisted to the utmost to give him time and save him expense. In the busiest East Side court, that has been very appropriately dubbed the “Poor Man’s Court,” fully five thousand dispossess warrants are issued in a year, but probably not fifty evictions take place in the district. The landlord has only one vote, while there may be forty voters hiring his rooms in the house, all of which the judge takes into careful account as elements that have a direct bearing on the case. And so they have﻿—on his case. There are sad cases, just as there are “rounders” who prefer to be moved at the landlord’s expense and save the rent, but the former at least are unusual enough to attract more than their share of attention.

===Chinese===
有一个流行的错觉——每逢没有更刺激的事情要公众操心，便有种种言过其实的故事把它鼓吹一番——说什么纽约的廉租公寓里每年的逐客比「全爱尔兰」加起来还多。我不敢说戳破这个谬误对房客有多大好处。依我看，被人从廉租公寓里撵出去，简直是天大的好运。不过事实是，纽约的逐客远不像人们想的那样常见。缘故在于：民事法庭的法官由本选区选出，房客选民总算有了站稳脚跟的硬地面。本来一开始就偏向他的那部法律，往往被拧到极限，好给他腾挪的时间、替他省下花费。在下东区最繁忙的那家法庭——人称「穷人的法庭」，再贴切不过——一年签发的收房令状足有五千张，但本选区实际发生的逐客，恐怕不到五十起。房东只有一张选票，而租他房子的房客说不定有四十个选民；这一切，法官都仔细掂量，当作对案子有直接分量的要素来考量。确实有分量——对他自己的案子。伤心事自然是有的，正如也有那种宁可叫房东掏钱搬家、白省下房租的「老油子」；但前者至少已算稀罕，稀罕得引人注目，受的关注远超其应有的份额。

===Original===
If his very poverty compels the tenant to live at a rate if not in a style that would beggar a Vanderbilt, paying four prices for everything he needs, from his rent and coal down to the smallest item in his housekeeping account, fashion, no less inexorable in the tenements than on the avenue, exacts of him that he must die in a style that is finally and utterly ruinous. The habit of expensive funerals﻿—I know of no better classification for it than along with the opium habit and similar grievous plagues of mankind﻿—is a distinctively Irish inheritance, but it has taken root among all classes of tenement dwellers, curiously enough most firmly among the Italians, who have taken amazingly to the funeral coach, perhaps because it furnishes the one opportunity of their lives for a really grand turnout with a free ride thrown in. It is not at all uncommon to find the hoards of a whole lifetime of hard work and self denial squandered on the empty show of a ludicrous funeral parade and a display of flowers that ill comports with the humble life it is supposed to exalt. It is easier to understand the wake as a sort of consolation cup for the survivors for whom there is﻿—as one of them, doubtless a heathenish pessimist, put it to me once﻿—“no such luck.” The press and the pulpit have denounced the wasteful practice that often entails bitter want upon the relatives of the one buried with such pomp, but with little or no apparent result. Rather, the undertaker’s business prospers more than ever in the tenements since the genius of politics has seen its way clear to make capital out of the dead voter as well as of the living, by making him the means of a useful “show of strength” and count of noses.

===Chinese===
如果说贫穷逼着房客以一种足以叫一个范德比尔特（Vanderbilt）破产的气派过活——纵然说不上排场——样样东西都付四倍的价钱，从房租、煤块一直到家用账上最小的一项；那么时尚——它在廉租公寓里同在大道上一样不容通融——则要求他死得讲究，讲究到彻底破产方休。排场葬礼的习气——我想不出更好的归类法，只好把它同鸦片瘾和人类类似的沉重疫害归作一类——本是爱尔兰人独有的遗产，却已在廉租公寓的各色住户中扎下根来，说来奇怪，在意大利人中间扎得最牢；他们对灵车入迷得惊人，兴许因为这是他们一生里唯一的机会，能弄上一场真正风光的排场，外带一趟白坐的车。一生的辛苦与克己攒下的积蓄，糟蹋在一场滑稽的出殡游行的空排场和一场鲜花陈列上——那鲜花同它理应颂扬的卑微人生全不相称——这样的情形绝非罕见。守灵倒还容易理解些：它是给活下来的人的一种安慰杯，因为正如他们中间一个——八成是个异教徒式的悲观主义者——有回对我说的，他们「没这份福气」。报界和讲坛都谴责过这种铺张的习气——它常叫那位风光大葬者的亲属陷入困苦——却几乎不见什么成效。非但如此，自打政治的天才想通了怎样连死去的选民也一并拿来生利——拿他充当一场有用的「亮实力」兼点人头的手段——殡仪商的生意在廉租公寓里反倒空前兴隆。

===Original===
One free excursion awaits young and old whom bitter poverty has denied the poor privilege of the choice of the home in death they were denied in life, the ride up the Sound to the Potter’s Field, charitably styled the City Cemetery. But even there they do not escape their fate. In the common trench of the Poor Burying Ground they lie packed three stories deep, shoulder to shoulder, crowded in death as they were in life, to “save space;” for even on that desert island the ground is not for the exclusive possession of those who cannot afford to pay for it. There is an odd coincidence in this, that year by year the lives that are begun in the gutter, the little nameless waifs whom the police pick up and the city adopts as its wards, are balanced by the even more forlorn lives that are ended in the river. I do not know how or why it happens, or that it is more than a mere coincidence. But there it is. Year by year the balance is struck﻿—a few more, a few less﻿—substantially the same when the record is closed.

===Chinese===
对那些被赤贫剥夺了这一可怜特权的人——生前无家可选，死后亦然——老老少少还有一场免费出游在等着：乘船溯海峡而上，去乱葬岗（Potter's Field），那里被善意地称作市立公墓。可是即便到了那里，他们也没能逃脱自己的命运。在穷人墓园的公共壕沟里，他们肩并着肩、叠埋三层，死了同活着时一样拥挤，为的是「节省地皮」；因为即便在那座荒岛上，土地也不是给付不起钱的人独占的。这中间有一桩古怪的巧合：年复一年，那些始于阴沟的生命——警察捡起的、城市收为监护孤儿的小小无名弃儿——总有在河中了结的、甚至更凄凉的生命与之相抵。我不知道这是怎么发生、为何发生的，也不知道它是否不止是一个巧合。但事实就是这样。年复一年，这笔账总是轧平的——一边多几个，一边少几个——到卷宗合上的时候，两数大体相当。

## XV The Problem of the Children / XV 孩子们的问题

===Original===
The problem of the children becomes, in these swarms, to the last degree perplexing. Their very number make one stand aghast. I have already given instances of the packing of the child population in East Side tenements. They might be continued indefinitely until the array would be enough to startle any community. For, be it remembered, these children with the training they receive﻿—or do not receive﻿—with the instincts they inherit and absorb in their growing up, are to be our future rulers, if our theory of government is worth anything. More than a working majority of our voters now register from the tenements. I counted the other day the little ones, up to ten years or so, in a Bayard Street tenement that for a yard has a triangular space in the centre with sides fourteen or fifteen feet long, just room enough for a row of ill-smelling closets at the base of the triangle and a hydrant at the apex. There was about as much light in this “yard” as in the average cellar. I gave up my self-imposed task in despair when I had counted one hundred and twenty-eight in forty families. Thirteen I had missed, or not found in. Applying the average for the forty to the whole fifty-three, the house contained one hundred and seventy children. It is not the only time I have had to give up such census work. I have in mind an alley﻿—an inlet rather to a row of rear tenements﻿—that is either two or four feet wide according as the wall of the crazy old building that gives on it bulges out or in. I tried to count the children that swarmed there, but could not. Sometimes I have doubted that anybody knows just how many there are about. Bodies of drowned children turn up in the rivers right along in summer whom no one seems to know anything about. When last spring some workmen, while moving a pile of lumber on a North River pier, found under the last plank the body of a little lad crushed to death, no one had missed a boy, though his parents afterward turned up. The truant officer assuredly does not know, though he spends his life trying to find out, somewhat illogically, perhaps, since the department that employs him admits that thousands of poor children are crowded out of the schools year by year for want of room. There was a big tenement in the Sixth Ward, now happily appropriated by the beneficent spirit of business that blots out so many foul spots in New York﻿—it figured not long ago in the official reports as “an out-and-out hog-pen”﻿—that had a record of one hundred and two arrests in four years among its four hundred and seventy-eight tenants, fifty-seven of them for drunken and disorderly conduct. I do not know how many children there were in it, but the inspector reported that he found only seven in the whole house who owned that they went to school. The rest gathered all the instruction they received running for beer for their elders. Some of them claimed the “flat” as their home as a mere matter of form. They slept in the streets at night. The official came upon a little party of four drinking beer out of the cover of a milk-can in the hallway. They were of the seven good boys and proved their claim to the title by offering him some.

===Chinese===
在这一群群蜂拥的孩子身上，儿童问题变得万分棘手。单是他们的数目，就足以令人瞠目。我已举过下东区（East Side）廉租公寓里儿童人口如何塞挤打包的实例。这样的例子尽可以无限列举下去，直到这份单子足以令任何一个社会为之震惊。因为，请记住，这些孩子——连同他们所受的管教，或者所受管教的阙如，连同他们在成长中承袭下来、浸润而成的种种本能——将要成为我们未来的统治者，倘若我们那套政府理论还有半点价值的话。如今我们的选民，从廉租公寓里登记出来的，已经不止一个足以左右大局的多数。前几天，我在贝亚德街（Bayard Street）一座廉租公寓里数数十岁上下的小家伙。那楼充作院子的，是当中一块三角形空地，三边各长十四五英尺，刚够沿三角形的底边排一溜臭气熏天的茅厕，再在顶角安上一个水龙头。这块「院子」里的光亮，同寻常地窖里的差不多。数到四十户人家共一百二十八个孩子，我绝望地放弃了这项自己派给自己的差事。还有十三户我漏了数，或是上门没找到人。按这四十户的平均数推算全楼五十三户，这座楼里共住着一百七十个孩子。这类人口清点的活计，我不是头一回半途而废。我心里记得一条小巷——毋宁说是一排后院廉租公寓的进身通道——它宽也两英尺、窄也四英尺，全看面朝巷子那座歪歪斜斜的老楼的墙壁是往外鼓还是往里瘪。我想数一数在那里扎堆的孩子们，却数不过来。有时我竟疑心，究竟有没有人说得清这一带到底有多少孩子。夏天里，河上接连不断地浮起溺死孩童的尸体，似乎没有一个人说得出他们的来历。去年春天，几名工人在北河（North River）一座码头上搬动一堆木料，在最后一块木板底下发现一个被压死的小男孩的尸体；虽说他的父母后来露了面，此前却没有一家发觉少了男孩。逃学稽查员（truant officer）固然不得而知，尽管他为此耗尽一生去查——这也许有点不合逻辑，因为雇用他的那个部门自己就承认，成千上万的穷孩子年复一年因校舍不足而被挤出校门。第六选区从前有一座大廉租公寓——如今幸而被那扫除纽约无数污点的、慈善为怀的商业精神挪作了他用；不久之前，它还在官方报告里以「彻头彻尾的猪圈」之名挂过号——四年之间，它的四百七十八名房客共留下一百零二次被捕记录，其中五十七次是酗酒滋事。我不知道楼里有多少孩子，但稽查员报称，全楼他只找到七个孩子肯承认自己上学。其余的孩子所受的全部教育，都是替长辈跑腿打啤酒的路上捡来的。有几个把那套「公寓」（flat）权当作家宅，不过徒具形式。夜里，他们睡在街上。那位官员撞见一小伙四个孩子，正在过道里就着牛奶罐的罐盖喝啤酒。他们正属于那七个好孩子之列，还向他敬了一些，证明自己当得起这个名号。

===Original===
The old question, what to do with the boy, assumes a new and serious phase in the tenements. Under the best conditions found there, it is not easily answered. In nine cases out of ten he would make an excellent mechanic, if trained early to work at a trade, for he is neither dull nor slow, but the shortsighted despotism of the trades unions has practically closed that avenue to him. Trade-schools, however excellent, cannot supply the opportunity thus denied him, and at the outset the boy stands condemned by his own to low and ill-paid drudgery, held down by the hand that of all should labor to raise him. Home, the greatest factor of all in the training of the young, means nothing to him but a pigeonhole in a coop along with so many other human animals. Its influence is scarcely of the elevating kind, if it have any. The very games at which he takes a hand in the street become polluting in its atmosphere. With no steady hand to guide him, the boy takes naturally to idle ways. Caught in the street by the truant officer, or by the agents of the Children’s Societies, peddling, perhaps, or begging, to help out the family resources, he runs the risk of being sent to a reformatory, where contact with vicious boys older than himself soon develop the latent possibilities for evil that lie hidden in him. The city has no Truant Home in which to keep him, and all efforts of the children’s friends to enforce school attendance are paralyzed by this want. The risk of the reformatory is too great. What is done in the end is to let him take chances﻿—with the chances all against him. The result is the rough young savage, familiar from the street. Rough as he is, if anyone doubt that this child of common clay have in him the instinct of beauty, of love for the ideal of which his life has no embodiment, let him put the matter to the test. Let him take into a tenement block a handful of flowers from the fields and watch the brightened faces, the sudden abandonment of play and fight that go ever hand in hand where there is no elbow-room, the wild entreaty for “posies,” the eager love with which the little messengers of peace are shielded, once possessed; then let him change his mind. I have seen an armful of daisies keep the peace of a block better than a policeman and his club, seen instincts awaken under their gentle appeal, whose very existence the soil in which they grew made seem a mockery. I have not forgotten the deputation of ragamuffins from a Mulberry Street alley that knocked at my office door one morning on a mysterious expedition for flowers, not for themselves, but for “a lady,” and having obtained what they wanted, trooped off to bestow them, a ragged and dirty little band, with a solemnity that was quite unusual. It was not until an old man called the next day to thank me for the flowers that I found out they had decked the bier of a pauper, in the dark rear room where she lay waiting in her pine-board coffin for the city’s hearse. Yet, as I knew, that dismal alley with its bare brick walls, between which no sun ever rose or set, was the world of those children. It filled their young lives. Probably not one of them had ever been out of the sight of it. They were too dirty, too ragged, and too generally disreputable, too well hidden in their slum besides, to come into line with the Fresh Air summer boarders.

===Chinese===
「拿这男孩怎么办」——这个老问题，到了廉租公寓里，呈现出一种崭新而严重的面貌。即便拿那里所能见到的最好条件来说，它也不容易作答。十个里有九个，只要趁早受训去学一门手艺，本可以成为出色的工匠，因为他既不愚钝，也不迟缓；然而工会（trades unions）短视的专制，实际上已经把这条路对他堵死了。职业学校（trade school）无论多么出色，也无法补上这样被剥夺掉的机会；于是这孩子起跑便被自己人判处了低贱而报酬微薄的苦役，被那只本该比谁都卖力托举他的手死死摁住。家——青少年教养中头等重大的因素——在他心里无非是同许多别的人形牲口合住的一个鸽子笼格。这样的家纵然还有什么影响，也谈不上是使人向上的那一类。就连他在街头插上一手的那些游戏，浸在这种氛围里也变得污浊。没有一只稳定的手来指引他，这孩子自然而然便走上了闲荡的路。当他在街头被逃学稽查员或各儿童协会的查访员逮住时——他也许正沿街做小买卖，或是行乞，为的是贴补家用——他便担着被送进教养院（reformatory）的风险；而在那里，同比他年长的顽劣少年的接触，很快便会催醒他身上潜藏着的种种为恶的可能。市里没有一座可以安置他的逃学儿童收容所（Truant Home），儿童之友们督促入学的一切努力，都因这一空缺而陷于瘫痪。教养院的风险太大。到头来所能做的，无非是任他去碰运气——而所有的运气都同他作对。结果，便是街头司空见惯的那种粗野的少年野人。他固然粗野，可若有人怀疑，这泥土捏成的孩子身上缺少爱美的本能、缺少对理想的倾慕——那理想在他的生活里毫无着落——那就请他把这件事放到试验里去验证。请他带一把田野里的鲜花走进廉租公寓街区，看一看那一张张骤然发亮的脸，看一看玩耍与打斗怎样陡然停歇——在那种连转身余地都没有的地方，这两样向来形影不离——听一听孩子们怎样发狂似的死缠着讨「花儿」，再看这些小小的和平使者一旦到手，被何等热切地呵护珍守；然后，让他收回自己的成见。我亲眼见过一抱雏菊维持一个街区的治安，比一名警察加一根警棍还管用；见过种种本能在花儿的温柔恳求下苏醒过来，而它们生根的那片土壤，偏偏叫这些本能的存在显得像一场嘲弄。我没有忘记，一天早晨，桑树街（Mulberry Street）一条小巷里来的一支小叫花子代表团敲响了我办公室的门，为着一桩神秘的花卉使命——不是为他们自己，而是为「一位太太」；如愿以偿之后，这一小队衣衫褴褛、蓬头垢面的孩子便结伴而去献花，那份庄重劲儿实在少见。直到第二天一位老者登门为那束花向我道谢，我才明白，他们装点的是一个贫民的灵床——在那间幽暗的后屋里，她躺在松木板棺材中，等着市里的柩车。然而，正如我所知，那条阴惨的小巷，连同它两面赤裸的砖墙——太阳从不在那墙与墙之间升起，也不落下——就是那些孩子的整个世界。它填满了他们年幼的生命。恐怕没有一个孩子曾走出过它的视线。他们太脏、太破烂、太上不得台面，又太深地藏身于贫民窟中，因此排不进新鲜空气基金（Fresh Air Fund）暑期下乡寄宿生的队伍。
===Original===
With such human instincts and cravings, forever unsatisfied, turned into a haunting curse; with appetite ground to keenest edge by a hunger that is never fed, the children of the poor grow up in joyless homes to lives of wearisome toil that claims them at an age when the play of their happier fellows has but just begun. Has a yard of turf been laid and a vine been coaxed to grow within their reach, they are banished and barred out from it as from a heaven that is not for such as they. I came upon a couple of youngsters in a Mulberry Street yard a while ago that were chalking on the fence their first lesson in “writin’.” And this is what they wrote: “Keeb of te Grass.” They had it by heart, for there was not, I verily believe, a green sod within a quarter of a mile. Home to them is an empty name. Pleasure? A gentleman once catechized a ragged class in a downtown public school on this point, and recorded the result: Out of forty-eight boys twenty had never seen the Brooklyn Bridge that was scarcely five minutes’ walk away, three only had been in Central Park, fifteen had known the joy of a ride in a horsecar. The street, with its ash-barrels and its dirt, the river that runs foul with mud, are their domain. What training they receive is picked up there. And they are apt pupils. If the mud and the dirt are easily reflected in their lives, what wonder? Scarce half-grown, such lads as these confront the world with the challenge to give them their due, too long withheld, or﻿—. Our jails supply the answer to the alternative.

===Chinese===
怀着这些永远不得满足、终于化作纠缠不去的诅咒的人性本能与渴望；带着被永不果腹的饥饿磨得无比锋利的胃口——穷人的孩子们就在这样的境况里，在毫无欢乐的家中长大成人，一入世便被令人倦怠的苦役征用，而那个年纪，他们更幸运的同伴们的游戏才刚刚开场。哪怕铺下过一方草皮，哪怕哄着一株藤蔓长到了他们够得着的地方，他们也照样被驱逐出去、被拦在外面，仿佛那是一个不为他们这类人预备的天堂。不久前，我在桑树街的一个院子里碰见两个小家伙，正用粉笔在栅栏上练他们人生的第一课「写字」。他们写下的字样是：「别踩草皮」（Keeb of te Grass）。这几个字他们背得滚瓜烂熟，因为方圆四分之一英里之内，我敢担保，找不出一块青草地。家，对他们只是一个空名。乐子呢？曾有一位先生在下城一所公立学校里，拿这个问题盘问过一班衣衫褴褛的学生，并记下了结果：四十八个男孩中，二十个从未见过步行不过五分钟远近的布鲁克林大桥（Brooklyn Bridge）；进过中央公园（Central Park）的，只有三个；尝过坐一回有轨马车（horsecar）的快乐的，有十五个。街道，连同它的炉灰桶（ash-barrel）与污泥浊水，还有那条裹挟泥污、臭气烘烘的河，就是他们的领地。他们所受的一切训练，都是从那里拾来的。而他们是学得快的学生。倘若污泥浊垢轻而易举地映进了他们的生活，又有什么可奇怪的呢？这样的少年，身子骨还没长成一半，便挺身向世界发出挑战：把他们被克扣得太久的应得之份交出来，不然——。我们的监狱，替这后半句话作了答。

===Original===
A little fellow who seemed clad in but a single rag was among the flotsam and jetsam stranded at Police Headquarters one day last summer. No one knew where he came from or where he belonged. The boy himself knew as little about it as anybody, and was the least anxious to have light shed on the subject after he had spent a night in the matron’s nursery. The discovery that beds were provided for boys to sleep in there, and that he could have “a whole egg” and three slices of bread for breakfast put him on the best of terms with the world in general, and he decided that Headquarters was “a bully place.” He sang “McGinty” all through, with Tenth Avenue variations, for the police, and then settled down to the serious business of giving an account of himself. The examination went on after this fashion:

===Chinese===
去年夏天有一天，在警察总局搁浅的漂零弃儿中间，有个小家伙，看上去浑身上下只挂着一绺破布。没有人知道他从哪里来，家属于何处。这孩子自己也和别人一样茫然，而且在女舍监（matron）的幼儿室里过了一夜之后，最不愿意有人把这件事弄个水落石出。在那里他发现居然备有床铺给男孩睡觉，早饭还吃得上「一整只鸡蛋」（a whole egg）外加三片面包——这一发现使他同整个世界言归于好，他断定总局是个「顶棒的地方」（a bully place）。他为警察们把《麦金蒂》（McGinty）从头唱到尾，还加上了第十大道（Tenth Avenue）的种种变调，然后郑重其事地着手交代自己的来历。盘问是这样进行的：
===Original===
“Where do you go to church, my boy?”

===Chinese===
“孩子，你上哪儿做礼拜？”

===Original===
“We don’t have no clothes to go to church.” And indeed his appearance, as he was, in the door of any New York church would have caused a sensation.

===Chinese===
“我们没有上教堂的衣服。”的确，就凭他这副模样，原样出现在纽约任何一座教堂的门口，都足以引起一场轰动。

===Original===
“Well, where do you go to school, then?”

===Chinese===
“那么，你上哪儿上学呢？”

===Original===
“I don’t go to school,” with a snort of contempt.

===Chinese===
“我不上学，”说着轻蔑地哼了一声。

===Original===
“Where do you buy your bread?”

===Chinese===
“你们的面包在哪儿买？”

===Original===
“We don’t buy no bread; we buy beer,” said the boy, and it was eventually the saloon that led the police as a landmark to his “home.” It was worthy of the boy. As he had said, his only bed was a heap of dirty straw on the floor, his daily diet a crust in the morning, nothing else.

===Chinese===
“我们不买面包；我们买啤酒，”男孩说。最后，正是那家酒馆像个路标似的，把警察一路引到了他的「家」。这个家也配得上这孩子。正如他说过的，他唯一的床，是地板上一堆脏草；一天的吃食，是早晨一块干面包皮，再无别的。
===Original===
Into the rooms of the Children’s Aid Society were led two little girls whose father had “busted up the house” and put them on the street after their mother died. Another, who was turned out by her stepmother “because she had five of her own and could not afford to keep her,” could not remember ever having been in church or Sunday-school, and only knew the name of Jesus through hearing people swear by it. She had no idea what they meant. These were specimens of the overflow from the tenements of our home-heathen that are growing up in New York’s streets today, while tenderhearted men and women are busying themselves with the socks and the hereafter of well-fed little Hottentots thousands of miles away. According to Canon Taylor, of York, one hundred and nine missionaries in the four fields of Persia, Palestine, Arabia, and Egypt spent one year and sixty thousand dollars in converting one little heathen girl. If there is nothing the matter with those missionaries, they might come to New York with a good deal better prospect of success.

===Chinese===
有两个小女孩被领进了儿童援助协会（Children's Aid Society）的屋子：她们的母亲死后，父亲「把这个家折腾散了」，把她们扔到了街上。还有一个，被继母撵了出来，理由是「她自己有五个孩子，养不起她」；她不记得自己进过教堂或主日学校（Sunday-school），连耶稣这个名字，也只是听人拿它赌咒起誓时才知道的。那名字指什么，她全无概念。这些，便是我们的本土异教徒（home-heathen）从廉租公寓里漫溢出来的样本——今天，他们正在纽约的街头长大；与此同时，心肠柔软的善男信女们，却忙着操心几千英里外那些吃得饱饱的小霍屯督人（Hottentots）的袜子和他们灵魂的归宿。据约克（York）的泰勒法政牧师（Canon Taylor）说，在波斯、巴勒斯坦、阿拉伯、埃及这四片园地里，一百零九名传教士耗去一整年工夫和六万美元，才使一个异教小女孩皈依。那些传教士若是本身没什么毛病，不妨到纽约来，成功的把握要大得多。

===Original===
By those who lay flattering unction to their souls in the knowledge that today New York has, at all events, no brood of the gutters of tender years that can be homeless long unheeded, let it be remembered well through what effort this judgment has been averted. In thirty-seven years the Children’s Aid Society, that came into existence as an emphatic protest against the tenement corruption of the young, has sheltered quite three hundred thousand outcast, homeless, and orphaned children in its lodging-houses, and has found homes in the West for seventy thousand that had none. Doubtless, as a mere stroke of finance, the five millions and a half thus spent were a wiser investment than to have let them grow up thieves and thugs. In the last fifteen years of this tireless battle for the safety of the State the intervention of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children has been invoked for 138,891 little ones: it has thrown its protection around more than twenty-five thousand helpless children, and has convicted nearly sixteen thousand wretches of child-beating and abuse. Add to this the standing army of fifteen thousand dependent children in New York’s asylums and institutions, and some idea is gained of the crop that is garnered day by day in the tenements, of the enormous force employed to check their inroads on our social life, and of the cause for apprehension that would exist did their efforts flag for ever so brief a time.

===Chinese===
有些人以这样的认识聊以自慰：今天的纽约，无论如何总算没有哪一群年幼的街头弃儿，会长久无家可归而无人过问——请这些人牢牢记住，这样的厄运是靠怎样的努力才得以避免的。三十七年来，作为对廉租公寓戕害青少年的一声斩钉截铁的抗议而问世的儿童援助协会，已在它开办的收容宿舍里庇护了近三十万被遗弃、无家可归与父母双亡的儿童，并为七万名无家可归者在西部找到了家。毫无疑问，单从理财的角度看，这样花掉的五百五十万美元，比放任他们长大成窃贼和暴徒，是更明智的一笔投资。在这场为国家安危而进行的不倦斗争的最近十五年里，防止虐待儿童协会（SPCC）应请出面的幼童多达 138,891 名：它为两万五千多名无助的儿童张起了保护伞，又将近一万六千名殴打、虐待儿童的恶徒定罪。再把纽约各收容院与救济机构里一万五千名受抚养儿童的这支常备大军算上，人们便可约略想见：廉租公寓里日复一日收割着怎样的一茬庄稼，为遏制他们侵入我们的社会生活动用着何等庞大的一支力量，而这些努力哪怕松懈短短一刻，又会生出怎样令人忧惧的因由。

===Original===
Nothing is now better understood than that the rescue of the children is the key to the problem of city poverty, as presented for our solution today: that character may be formed where to reform it would be a hopeless task. The concurrent testimony of all who have to undertake it at a later stage: that the young are naturally neither vicious nor hardened, simply weak and undeveloped, except by the bad influences of the street, makes this duty all the more urgent as well as hopeful. Helping hands are held out on every side. To private charity the municipality leaves the entire care of its proletariat of tender years, lulling its conscience to sleep with liberal appropriations of money to foot the bills. Indeed, it is held by those whose opinions are entitled to weight that it is far too liberal a paymaster for its own best interests and those of its wards. It deals with the evil in the seed to a limited extent in gathering in the outcast babies from the streets. To the ripe fruit the gates of its prisons, its reformatories, and its workhouses are opened wide the year round. What the showing would be at this end of the line were it not for the barriers wise charity has thrown across the broad highway to ruin﻿—is building day by day﻿—may be measured by such results as those quoted above in the span of a single life.

===Chinese===
如今再没有比这更明白的道理了：拯救儿童，是今天摆在我们面前亟待解决的城市贫困问题的钥匙；在日后改造无望之处，性格尚可塑造。所有不得不在更晚阶段接手这件任务的人，都众口一词地作证说：少年儿童就天性而言，既不邪恶，也不顽劣，只是软弱、未经陶冶——唯有街头的不良影响是例外。这证词使这项职责显得愈加紧迫，也愈加有望。援助之手正从四面八方伸过来。市政当局把它那些年幼无产者的照管，整个推给了私人慈善事业，一面以慷慨的拨款支付账单，哄自己的良心入睡。的确，在见解颇受重视的人士看来，无论为它自身的最大利益计，还是为它监护下的孩子们计，这位出钱的东家都出手太阔绰了。它把街头的弃婴收拢起来，算是有限地对付了一下尚在种子里的祸害；而对熟透的果子，它的监狱、教养院和济贫院的大门，则一年到头敞开着。倘若没有明智的慈善事业在通往毁灭的康庄大道上横筑起的道道屏障——这屏障还在一天天加筑——那么这条流水线的末端会是何等光景，从上文所引的那些成果——而它们只是在一个人的生命跨度之内取得的——便可测知分晓了。

## XVI Waifs of the City’s Slums / XVI 城市贫民窟的流浪儿

===Original===
First among these barriers is the Foundling Asylum. It stands at the very outset of the waste of life that goes on in a population of nearly two millions of people; powerless to prevent it, though it gather in the outcasts by night and by day. In a score of years an army of twenty-five thousand of these forlorn little waifs have cried out from the streets of New York in arraignment of a Christian civilization under the blessings of which the instinct of motherhood even was smothered by poverty and want. Only the poor abandon their children. The stories of richly-dressed foundlings that are dished up in the newspapers at intervals are pure fiction. Not one instance of even a well-dressed infant having been picked up in the streets is on record. They come in rags, a newspaper often the only wrap, semi-occasionally one in a clean slip with some evidence of loving care; a little slip of paper pinned on, perhaps, with some such message as this I once read, in a woman’s trembling hand: “Take care of Johnny, for God’s sake. I cannot.” But even that is the rarest of all happenings.

===Chinese===
在这重重屏障之中，位居第一的便是育婴堂（Foundling Asylum）。它立在近两百万人口中间那场生命浪费的最前端；尽管它日夜收容弃儿，却无力阻止这场浪费。二十年间，两万五千名这样孤苦无告的小小流浪儿从纽约街头发出哭喊，控诉着这样一个基督教文明——在它的福荫之下，连母爱的天性都被贫穷与匮乏活活闷死了。遗弃自己孩子的，只有穷人。报纸隔三差五端出来的那些衣着华贵的弃婴故事，纯属虚构。就连一个穿着体面的婴儿在街头被拾起的事例，也从来没有一桩记录在案。他们来时衣衫褴褛，裹身的往往只有一张报纸；偶尔也有一个穿着干净小衣、看得出受过爱抚照料的；身上或许还别着一张小纸条，以一个女人颤抖的笔迹写着类似这样一句我曾读过的话：“看在上帝份上，照管好约翰尼。我做不到。”但就连这样的事，也是一切当中最罕见的。

===Original===
The city divides with the Sisters of Charity the task of gathering them in. The real foundlings, the children of the gutter that are picked up by the police, are the city’s wards. In midwinter, when the poor shiver in their homes, and in the dog-days when the fierce heat and foul air of the tenements smother their babies by thousands, they are found, sometimes three and four in a night, in hallways, in areas and on the doorsteps of the rich, with whose comfort in luxurious homes the wretched mother somehow connects her own misery. Perhaps, as the drowning man clutches at a straw, she hopes that these happier hearts may have love to spare even for her little one. In this she is mistaken. Unauthorized babies especially are not popular in the abodes of the wealthy. It never happens outside of the storybooks that a baby so deserted finds home and friends at once. Its career, though rather more official, is less romantic, and generally brief. After a night spent at Police Headquarters it travels up to the Infants’ Hospital on Randall’s Island in the morning, fitted out with a number and a bottle, that seldom see much wear before they are laid aside for a fresh recruit. Few outcast babies survive their desertion long. Murder is the true name of the mother’s crime in eight cases out of ten. Of 508 babies received at the Randall’s Island Hospital last year 333 died, 65.55 percent. But of the 508 only 170 were picked up in the streets, and among these the mortality was much greater, probably nearer ninety percent, if the truth were told. The rest were born in the hospitals. The high mortality among the foundlings is not to be marvelled at. The wonder is, rather, that any survive. The stormier the night, the more certain is the police nursery to echo with the feeble cries of abandoned babes. Often they come half dead from exposure. One live baby came in a little pine coffin which a policeman found an inhuman wretch trying to bury in an uptown lot. But many do not live to be officially registered as a charge upon the county. Seventy-two dead babies were picked up in the streets last year. Some of them were doubtless put out by very poor parents to save funeral expenses. In hard times the number of dead and live foundlings always increases very noticeably. But whether travelling by way of the Morgue or the Infants’ Hospital, the little army of waifs meets, reunited soon, in the trench in the Potter’s Field where, if no medical student is in need of a subject, they are laid in squads of a dozen.

===Chinese===
收容弃婴的工作，由市政与慈善修女会（Sisters of Charity）分担。真正的弃婴，即由警察拾起的阴沟孩童，归市政照管。隆冬时节，穷人在自己家里瑟瑟发抖；三伏天里，廉租公寓的酷热与浊气成千地闷杀他们的婴孩——正是在这些时节，弃婴被人发现，有时一夜三四个，躺在门厅里、采光井里，或是富人家的门阶上；那可怜的母亲，不知怎么总把她自己的苦难，同富人在华宅里的安逸连在一起。也许，正如溺水者抓住一根稻草，她指望这些更幸福的人心或许多出一份爱来，哪怕分给她的孩子一点。在这一点上她想错了。没有名分的婴儿，在富贵人家尤其不受欢迎。这样被遗弃的婴儿一落地便立刻找到家和朋友的事，出了故事书便从不曾发生。它的人生历程虽说官派气更重些，却少些浪漫，而且一般都很短。在警察总局过上一夜之后，第二天早晨它便被送往兰德尔岛（Randall's Island）的婴儿医院（Infants' Hospital），领到一个编号和一只奶瓶——这两样东西很少用得多久，便被搁到一旁，让位给新来的小家伙。被遗弃的婴儿，少有在遗弃之后活得长久的。十桩案子里有八桩，母亲之罪的真正名字是谋杀。去年兰德尔岛医院收治的 508 名婴儿中，死了 333 名，合百分之65.55。但在这 508 名之中，从街头拾起的只有 170 名，而这些孩子的死亡率要高得多——若照实说，恐怕接近百分之九十。其余的都是医院里出生的。弃婴死亡率之高，不必惊叹；倒是竟有活下来的，才更可怪。夜越是风雨交加，警察育婴室便越准会回荡起弃婴微弱的啼哭。他们来时常常已被冻晒得半死。有一个活婴是装在一口小松木棺材里送来的——一名警察撞见一个丧尽天良的恶棍，正要把这口棺材埋进上城的一块空地。但许多孩子活不到被正式登记为县里负担的那一天。去年从街头拾起的死婴有七十二个。其中无疑有一些，是被赤贫的父母放到外面去的，为的是省下一笔丧葬费。年景艰难的时候，死婴和活弃婴的数目总是显著增加。但无论是取道陈尸所（Morgue）还是婴儿医院，这支流浪儿的小小队伍很快就重聚在乱葬岗（Potter's Field）的壕沟里；倘若没有医科学生正缺一具解剖材料，他们便十来个一组地被并排安放下去。

===Original===
Most of the foundlings come from the East Side, where they are left by young mothers without wedding-rings or other name than their own to bestow upon the baby, returning from the island hospital to face an unpitying world with the evidence of their shame. Not infrequently they wear the bed-tick regimentals of the Public Charities, and thus their origin is easily enough traced. Oftener no ray of light penetrates the gloom, and no effort is made to probe the mystery of sin and sorrow. This also is the policy pursued in the great Foundling Asylum of the Sisters of Charity in Sixty-eighth Street, known all over the world as Sister Irene’s Asylum. Years ago the crib that now stands just inside the street door, under the great main portal, was placed outside at night; but it filled up too rapidly. The babies took to coming in little squads instead of in single file, and in self-defence the sisters were forced to take the cradle in. Now the mother must bring her child inside and put it in the crib where she is seen by the sister on guard. No effort is made to question her, or discover the child’s antecedents, but she is asked to stay and nurse her own and another baby. If she refuses, she is allowed to depart unhindered. If willing, she enters at once into the great family of the good Sister who in twenty-one years has gathered as many thousand homeless babies into her fold. One was brought in when I was last in the asylum, in the middle of July, that received in its crib the number 20715. The death-rate is of course lowered a good deal where exposure of the child is prevented. Among the eleven hundred infants in the asylum it was something over nineteen percent last year; but among those actually received in the twelvemonth nearer twice that figure. Even the nineteen percent, remarkably low for a Foundling Asylum, was equal to the startling death-rate of Gotham Court in the cholera scourge.

===Chinese===
弃婴大多来自下东区（East Side）。在那里，他们被年轻母亲们留在街头——这些母亲指上没有婚戒，除了自己的名字，再没有旁的名字可以传给孩子；她们从岛上医院回来，带着自己耻辱的证据，去面对一个毫不怜悯的世界。她们当中不少人穿着公共慈善局（Public Charities）发的床袋布「号衣」，她们的来历因此不难追查。更多的时候，一线光也透不进那片幽暗，也无人费神去追探那罪与愁的谜。这也是慈善修女会设在第六十八街（Sixty-eighth Street）的大育婴堂奉行的方针——这所育婴堂以艾琳修女育婴堂（Sister Irene's Asylum）之名闻名于世。多年以前，如今立在大门内、正对街门的那只摇篮，夜里是放在门外的；可它填满得太快了。婴儿们不再鱼贯而来，改成了成小队成小队地来，修女们出于自卫，只得把摇篮搬了进来。如今母亲必须把孩子抱进屋来，放进摇篮，让值守的修女看见她。无人盘问她，也无人去追查孩子的来历，只是请她留下来，给自己的和另一个婴儿哺乳。她若拒绝，便听凭她不受阻拦地离去。她若愿意，便即刻进入那位好修女的大家庭——二十一年间，这位修女已把两万一千名无家可归的婴儿收进了她的羊栏。我上次到育婴堂时——那是七月中旬——恰有一个婴儿被送了进来，在摇篮里领到了第 20715 号。凡能防止婴儿遭曝弃的地方，死亡率自然大大降低。育婴堂里一千一百名婴儿当中，去年的死亡率略高于百分之十九；但在那一年的十二个月里实际收进的婴儿当中，则接近这个数字的两倍。即便是这百分之十九——对一所育婴堂而言低得惊人——也已赶得上哥谭院（Gotham Court）在霍乱浩劫中那骇人的死亡率。

===Original===
Four hundred and sixty mothers, who could not or would not keep their own babies, did voluntary penance for their sin in the asylum last year by nursing a strange waif besides their own until both should be strong enough to take their chances in life’s battle. An even larger number than the eleven hundred were “pay babies,” put out to be nursed by “mothers” outside the asylum. The money thus earned pays the rent of hundreds of poor families. It is no trifle, quite half of the quarter of a million dollars contributed annually by the city for the support of the asylum. The procession of these nurse-mothers, when they come to the asylum on the first Wednesday of each month to receive their pay and have the babies inspected by the sisters, is one of the sights of the city. The nurses, who are under strict supervision, grow to love their little charges and part from them with tears when, at the age of four or five, they are sent to Western homes to be adopted. The sisters carefully encourage the home-feeling in the child as their strongest ally in seeking its mental and moral elevation, and the toddlers depart happy to join their “papas and mammas” in the faraway, unknown home.

===Chinese===
去年，有四百六十位无力或不愿留养亲生婴儿的母亲，在育婴堂里为自己的罪自愿苦修：她们给自己的婴儿哺乳，也给一个素不相识的弃儿哺乳，直到两个孩子都足够强壮，可以去人生战场上各碰各的运气。比一千一百这个数目还要多的，是「寄养婴儿」（“pay babies”）——被送出堂外，交给堂外的「乳母」（“mothers”）去哺乳。这样挣来的钱，替几百户穷人家付了房租。这绝非小数：市里每年为维持这所育婴堂而拨的二十五万元中，足有一半便是这么付出的。每月第一个星期三，这些乳母到育婴堂来领工钱、让修女们检查婴儿，她们的行列堪称本城一景。乳母们受着严格监督，渐渐疼爱起自己照管的小家伙；待孩子四五岁上被送往西部人家收养时，她们挥泪而别。修女们着意培育孩子对家的感情，把它当作谋求孩子心智与道德上进的最强盟友；于是那些蹒跚学步的小家伙欢欢喜喜地动身，到那遥远而素昧平生的家里去投奔他们的「爸爸妈妈」了。

===Original===
An infinitely more fiendish, if to surface appearances less deliberate, plan of child-murder than desertion has flourished in New York for years under the title of baby-farming. The name, put into plain English, means starving babies to death. The law has fought this most heinous of crimes by compelling the registry of all baby-farms. As well might it require all persons intending murder to register their purpose with time and place of the deed under the penalty of exemplary fines. Murderers do not hang out a shingle. “Baby-farms,” said once Mr. Elbridge T. Gerry, the President of the Society charged with the execution of the law that was passed through his efforts, “are concerns by means of which persons, usually of disreputable character, eke out a living by taking two, or three, or four babies to board. They are the charges of outcasts, or illegitimate children. They feed them on sour milk, and give them paregoric to keep them quiet, until they die, when they get some young medical man without experience to sign a certificate to the Board of Health that the child died of inanition, and so the matter ends. The baby is dead, and there is no one to complain.” A handful of baby-farms have been registered and licensed by the Board of Health with the approval of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children in the last five years, but none of this kind. The devil keeps the only complete register to be found anywhere. Their trace is found oftenest by the coroner or the police; sometimes they may be discovered hiding in the advertising columns of certain newspapers, under the guise of the scarcely less heartless traffic in helpless children that is dignified with the pretence of adoption﻿—for cash. An idea of how this scheme works was obtained through the disclosures in a celebrated divorce case, a year or two ago. The society has among its records a very recent case18 of a baby a week old (Baby “Blue Eyes”) that was offered for sale﻿—adoption, the dealer called it﻿—in a newspaper. The agent bought it after some haggling for a dollar, and arrested the woman slave-trader; but the law was powerless to punish her for her crime. Twelve unfortunate women awaiting dishonored motherhood were found in her house.

===Chinese===
有一种比遗弃表面上看似不那么处心积虑、实则无比狠毒的杀婴图谋，多年来顶着「婴儿农场」（baby-farming）的名号在纽约生意兴隆。这个名字，说白了，就是把婴儿活活饿死。法律对抗这一穷凶极恶之罪的办法，是强令一切婴儿农场登记。这倒不如干脆要求所有蓄意杀人的人，在儆戒性罚款的威胁之下，把杀人意图连同作案的时间地点一并登记备案。杀人的是不挂招牌的。“婴儿农场，”受命执行这项经由他出力方才通过的法律的协会主席埃尔德里奇·T·格里（Elbridge T. Gerry）先生有一次说，“是这样一种营生：一些通常名声不堪的人，靠收带两三个或四个婴儿寄养来贴补生计。这些孩子是流浪女之流的私生儿女。他们拿酸牛奶喂孩子，给他们复方樟脑酊（paregoric）让他们安静，直到孩子死掉；这时便去找某个没有经验的年轻医生，向卫生委员会签一张证明，说孩子死于营养不良，事情就此了结。婴儿死了，没有人来告状。”过去五年里，经防止虐待儿童协会（SPCC）认可、由卫生委员会登记发照的婴儿农场有寥寥几家，但没有一家属于这一类。只有魔鬼那里，保存着世上仅有的完整登记簿。追踪它们的行迹，最常见的靠验尸官或警察；有时它们会藏身于某些报纸的广告栏里，顶着收养的幌子——一手现钱——干着那桩贩卖无助孩童、狠心程度几乎不相上下的买卖。这套把戏如何运作，一两年前一桩著名离婚案的披露让人窥见了一斑。协会档案里有一桩距今很近的案件（18）：一个出生才一星期的婴儿（「蓝眼睛」宝宝）被人登报出售——那贩子管它叫收养。协会探员讨价还价一番，花一块钱把婴儿买了下来，随后逮捕了那个做人口买卖的女人；可法律竟无力惩办她的罪行。在她家里，查出十二个不幸的女人，正等着不清不白的母亲身份落到自己头上。

===Original===
One gets a glimpse of the frightful depths to which human nature, perverted by avarice bred of ignorance and rasping poverty, can descend, in the mere suggestion of systematic insurance *for profit* of children’s lives. A woman was put on trial in this city last year for incredible cruelty in her treatment of a stepchild. The evidence aroused a strong suspicion that a pitifully small amount of insurance on the child’s life was one of the motives for the woman’s savagery. A little investigation brought out the fact that three companies that were in the business of insuring children’s lives, for sums varying from $17 up, had issued not less than a million such policies! The premiums ranged from five to twenty-five cents a week. What untold horrors this business may conceal was suggested by a formal agreement entered into by some of the companies, “for the purpose of preventing speculation in the insurance of children’s lives.” By the terms of this compact, “no higher premium than ten cents could be accepted on children under six years old.” Barbarism forsooth! Did ever heathen cruelty invent a more fiendish plot than the one written down between the lines of this legal paper?

===Chinese===
仅仅「系统性地为*牟利*（*for profit*）给儿童性命投保」这一个念头，就足以让人窥见人性——被无知养出的贪婪与锉骨的贫困所扭曲的人性——能堕入何等骇人的深渊。去年本城有一个女人受审，罪名是虐待继子残暴得令人难以置信。证据引起了一种强烈的怀疑：给孩子性命保下的那笔少得可怜的保险金，正是这女人行凶的动机之一。稍加调查便查明了一个事实：三家经营儿童人寿保险业务的公司——保额自 $17 起——竟发出了不少于一百万张这样的保单！保费从每星期五分到二十五分不等。这桩生意底下可能藏着多少说不尽的恐怖，从其中几家公司缔结的一份正式协定里已见端倪；协定自称其目的是“防止儿童人寿保险中的投机行为”。协定的条款写道：“六岁以下儿童，承保之保费不得高于十分钱。”这才是货真价实的野蛮！异教徒的残暴，可曾发明过比这份法律文件字里行间写下的更歹毒的图谋？

===Original===
It is with a sense of glad relief that one turns from this misery to the brighter page of the helping hands stretched forth on every side to save the young and the helpless. New York is, I firmly believe, the most charitable city in the world. Nowhere is there so eager a readiness to help, when it is known that help is worthily wanted; nowhere are such armies of devoted workers, nowhere such abundance of means ready to the hand of those who know the need and how rightly to supply it. Its poverty, its slums, and its suffering are the result of unprecedented growth with the consequent disorder and crowding, and the common penalty of metropolitan greatness. If the structure shows signs of being top-heavy, evidences are not wanting﻿—they are multiplying day by day﻿—that patient toilers are at work among the underpinnings. The Day Nurseries, the numberless Kindergartens and charitable schools in the poor quarters, the Fresh Air Funds, the thousand and one charities that in one way or another reach the homes and the lives of the poor with sweetening touch, are proof that if much is yet to be done, if the need only grows with the effort, hearts and hands will be found to do it in ever-increasing measure. Black as the cloud is it has a silver lining, bright with promise. New York is today a hundredfold cleaner, better, purer, city than it was even ten years ago.

===Chinese===
怀着一种欣然的宽慰，人们才从这片苦难转往较明亮的一页：四面八方伸出的援手，正在拯救年幼的与无助的。我坚信，纽约是世界上最乐善好施的城市。一旦得知所求之助确属应得，再没有哪里伸手相助有这般踊跃；再没有哪里有这样一支支忘我奉献的工作者大军，再没有哪里有这样充裕的资财，随时听凭那些既深知需求、又懂得如何正当供给的人取用。它的贫穷、它的贫民窟、它的苦难，是史无前例的扩张随之而来的紊乱与拥挤所致，是大都会之伟大共同付出的代价。倘若这座构筑显出头重脚轻的征象，那么证据并不缺乏——而且正与日俱增——表明有坚忍的劳动者正在底层的基座之间埋头工作。日间托儿所（Day Nurseries）、贫民区里数不清的幼儿园（Kindergarten）和慈善学校、新鲜空气基金（Fresh Air Fund），以及那千百桩以种种方式用甘甜之触抵达穷人的家庭与生活的善举，都证明：纵然尚有许多待做，纵然需求只随努力一同增长，去做的心与手总会以愈来愈大的规模应运而出。乌云纵黑，衬里却镶着银光，闪着应许的亮。今天的纽约，比哪怕十年前也干净了百倍、更好了百倍、更纯净了百倍。

===Original===
Two powerful agents that were among the pioneers in this work of moral and physical regeneration stand in Paradise Park today as milestones on the rocky, uphill road. The handful of noble women, who braved the foul depravity of the Old Brewery to rescue its child victims, rolled away the first and heaviest boulder, which legislatures and city councils had tackled in vain. The Five Points Mission and the Five Points House of Industry have accomplished what no machinery of government availed to do. Sixty thousand children have been rescued by them from the streets and had their little feet set in the better way. Their work still goes on, increasing and gathering in the waifs, instructing and feeding them, and helping their parents with advice and more substantial aid. Their charity knows not creed or nationality. The House of Industry is an enormous nursery-school with an average of more than four hundred day scholars and constant boarders﻿—“outsiders” and “insiders.” Its influence is felt for many blocks around in that crowded part of the city. It is one of the most touching sights in the world to see a score of babies, rescued from homes of brutality and desolation, where no other blessing than a drunken curse was ever heard, saying their prayers in the nursery at bedtime. Too often their white nightgowns hide tortured little bodies and limbs cruelly bruised by inhuman hands. In the shelter of this fold they are safe, and a happier little group one may seek long and far in vain.

===Chinese===
在这场道德与身体再造的工程中，有两股强大力量当年曾充当前驱，今天作为里程碑立在乐园公园（Paradise Park），立在那条多石的上坡路上。那一小群高贵的妇女，当年闯进老酿酒厂污浊堕落的渊薮去救出那里的孩童受害者，推开了第一块也是最重的巨石——立法机关和市议会屡撬不动的那一块。五点区布道会（Five Points Mission）和五点区工艺院（Five Points House of Industry）完成了任何政府机器都无能为力的事。经它们之手，六万名儿童已从街头获救，小脚被引上了正路。它们的工作仍在继续，规模日增，收容流浪儿，教导他们、喂养他们，并以劝导和更切实的资助帮助他们的父母。它们的慈善不问信仰与国籍。工艺院是一所巨大的幼儿学校，平均有四百多名走读生和常年寄宿生——「外宿的」与「内宿的」。它的影响遍及城中那片拥挤地段周围好多个街区。二十来个婴儿——从残暴荒凉之家中救出的孩子，在那样的家里，除了一声醉后的咒骂，从未听过别的祝福——在育婴室里于就寝时分念诵晚祷，这是世上最动人的景象之一。他们白色的睡袍之下，往往掩着备受折磨的小小身体，和被无人性的手残酷打伤的四肢。在这个羊圈的庇护里，他们是安全的；要寻比这更快乐的一小群，任你走遍远近，也是枉然。

## XVII The Street Arab / XVII 街头流浪儿

===Original===
Not all the barriers erected by society against its nether life, not the labor of unnumbered societies for the rescue and relief of its outcast waifs, can dam the stream of homelessness that issues from a source where the very name of home is a mockery. The Street Arab is as much of an institution in New York as Newspaper Row, to which he gravitates naturally, following his Bohemian instinct. Crowded out of the tenements to shift for himself, and quite ready to do it, he meets there the host of adventurous runaways from every State in the Union and from across the sea, whom New York attracts with a queer fascination, as it attracts the older emigrants from all parts of the world. A census of the population in the Newsboys’ Lodging House on any night will show such an odd mixture of small humanity as could hardly be got together in any other spot. It is a mistake to think that they are helpless little creatures, to be pitied and cried over because they are alone in the world. The unmerciful “guying” the good man would receive, who went to them with such a programme, would soon convince him that that sort of pity was wasted, and would very likely give him the idea that they were a set of hardened little scoundrels, quite beyond the reach of missionary effort.

===Chinese===
社会为防范其底层生活而筑起的一切屏障，无数团体为营救、救济被弃流浪儿而付出的一切辛劳，都堵不住那股无家可归的人流——它从一个连「家」这个名字本身都成了嘲弄的源头汩汩涌出。街头流浪儿（street arab）在纽约同报馆街（Newspaper Row）一样，是一种建制；他循着那份波希米亚式的本能，自然而然地被吸引到那里去。他被廉租公寓挤了出来，要自谋生计，而他对此也全然乐意。在那里，他遇见成群结队的冒险出走者——来自合众国各州，也来自大洋彼岸；纽约以一种古怪的魅力吸引着他们，正如它吸引着来自世界各地的年长移民一样。随便哪个夜晚，到报童寄宿所（Newsboys' Lodging House）去清点一遍人口，都会见到这样一锅古怪的小小人类的大杂烩，换了别的地点几乎再难凑齐。以为他们是无依无靠的小东西、因为举目无亲就该受人怜惜、惹人垂泪，那就错了。哪位好心人若带着这样一套打算上门，挨的那顿毫不留情的「挖苦」（guying）很快就会叫他相信：那类怜悯纯属白费；而且十之八九还会叫他生出另一个念头——这帮小子是一伙顽劣成性的小恶棍，传教的功夫根本够不着他们。

===Original===
But that would only be his second mistake. The Street Arab has all the faults and all the virtues of the lawless life he leads. Vagabond that he is, acknowledging no authority and owing no allegiance to anybody or anything, with his grimy fist raised against society whenever it tries to coerce him, he is as bright and sharp as the weasel, which, among all the predatory beasts, he most resembles. His sturdy independence, love of freedom and absolute self-reliance, together with his rude sense of justice that enables him to govern his little community, not always in accordance with municipal law or city ordinances, but often a good deal closer to the saving line of “doing to others as one would be done by”﻿—these are strong handles by which those who know how can catch the boy and make him useful. Successful bankers, clergymen, and lawyers all over the country, statesmen in some instances of national repute, bear evidence in their lives to the potency of such missionary efforts. There is scarcely a learned profession, or branch of honorable business, that has not in the last twenty years borrowed some of its brightest light from the poverty and gloom of New York’s streets.

===Chinese===
不过，那只是他的第二个错误。街头流浪儿兼备他所过的那种无法无天的生活的全部恶习与全部美德。他是个流浪儿，不承认任何权威，不对任何人、任何东西负半点效忠之忱；每当社会试图胁迫他，他就攥起乌黑的小拳头与之相抗。他像黄鼠狼一样机敏伶俐——在所有掠食兽中，他与黄鼠狼最为相像。他那份顽强的独立、对自由的热爱和绝对的自恃，连同他那份粗拙的正义感——正是这份正义感使他管得住自己的小小社群，虽说不总依着市政法令或市府条例，却往往大大贴近「你愿意人怎样待你，也要怎样待人」那条救人的底线——这些正是结实的把手，懂行的人可以借以抓住这孩子，使他成器。全国各地功成名就的银行家、牧师和律师，间或还有名闻全国的政坛人物，都以自身的人生为这类传教努力的效力作证。近二十年来，几乎没有什么学问职业或体面行当，不曾从纽约街头的贫穷与幽暗中借得几分最亮的光彩。

===Original===
Anyone, whom business or curiosity has taken through Park Row or across Printing House Square in the midnight hour, when the air is filled with the roar of great presses spinning with printers’ ink on endless rolls of white paper the history of the world in the twenty-four hours that have just passed away, has seen little groups of these boys hanging about the newspaper offices; in winter, when snow is on the streets, fighting for warm spots around the grated vent-holes that let out the heat and steam from the underground press-rooms with their noise and clatter, and in summer playing craps and 7‒11 on the curb for their hard-earned pennies, with all the absorbing concern of hardened gamblers. This is their beat. Here the agent of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children finds those he thinks too young for “business,” but does not always capture them. Like rabbits in their burrows, the little ragamuffins sleep with at least one eye open, and every sense alert to the approach of danger: of their enemy, the policeman, whose chief business in life is to move them on, and of the agent bent on robbing them of their cherished freedom. At the first warning shout they scatter and are off. To pursue them would be like chasing the fleet-footed mountain goat in his rocky fastnesses. There is not an open door, a hidden turn or runway which they do not know, with lots of secret passages and shortcuts no one else ever found. To steal a march on them is the only way. There is a coal chute from the sidewalk to the boiler-room in the sub-cellar of the Post Office which the Society’s officer found the boys had made into a sort of toboggan slide to a snug berth in wintry weather. They used to slyly raise the cover in the street, slide down in single file, and snuggle up to the warm boiler out of harm’s way, as they thought. It proved a trap, however. The agent slid down himself one cold night﻿—there was no other way of getting there﻿—and, landing right in the midst of the sleeping colony, had it at his mercy. After repeated raids upon their headquarters, the boys forsook it last summer, and were next found herding under the shore-end of one of the East River banana docks, where they had fitted up a regular clubroom that was shared by thirty or forty homeless boys and about a million rats.

===Chinese===
无论何人，只要因公务或好奇心在午夜时分走过公园路（Park Row）或穿过印刷所广场（Printing House Square），都会见过一小伙一小伙这样的男孩在报馆周围转悠。午夜时分，空气里充满大印刷机的轰响，那些机器正用印刷油墨，在无休无止的白纸卷上飞旋出刚刚过去的二十四小时里整个世界的历史。冬天，男孩们围着地下印报间的铁栅通气口争夺暖位——热气和蒸汽夹着机器的喧嚣嘈杂从那些口子里泄出；夏天，他们就在路缘石上玩「双骰」（craps）和「七——十一」（7‒11），拿辛苦挣来的分币作注，那份全神贯注，俨然是赌场老手。这里是他们的巡道。防止虐待儿童协会（SPCC）的稽查员就在这里发现那些他认定年纪太小、干不得「买卖」的孩子，却并不总能捉住他们。这些衣衫褴褛的小家伙像兔穴里的兔子，睡觉也至少睁着一只眼，每种感官都警觉着危险的临近：危险来自他们的仇敌警察——警察此生的头等大事就是撵他们挪窝——也来自那个一心要夺走他们珍视的自由的稽查员。警报的喊声一起，他们便四散而逃。追他们，就像在岩石堡垒里追赶捷足的山羊。没有一扇敞开的门、一处隐蔽的拐角或一条逃路是他们不知道的，还有许许多多谁也从未发现的暗道和近路。出其不意地偷袭是唯一的办法。邮局（Post Office）的人行道下有一条通到地下锅炉房的溜煤槽，协会的稽查员发现，男孩们竟把它改成了雪橇滑道，寒冬里顺着它可以滑进一个安稳的铺位。他们惯会偷偷掀起街面上的盖子，鱼贯滑下，偎到暖烘烘的锅炉旁，自以为安然无恙。不料这地方竟成了陷阱。一个寒冷的夜晚，那位稽查员亲自滑了下去——要到那儿没有别的路——正好落在那群睡着的孩子当中，把他们捏在了手心里。老巢接连遭了几番突袭之后，男孩们去年夏天舍弃了它，后来被发现成群聚在东河（East River）一座香蕉码头的岸端底下；他们在那里布置起一间正经的会所，由三四十个无家可归的男孩和大约一百万只老鼠合用。

===Original===
Newspaper Row is merely their headquarters. They are to be found all over the city, these Street Arabs, where the neighborhood offers a chance of picking up a living in the daytime and of “turning in” at night with a promise of security from surprise. In warm weather a truck in the street, a convenient outhouse, or a dugout in a hay-barge at the wharf make good bunks. Two were found making their nest once in the end of a big iron pipe up by the Harlem Bridge, and an old boiler at the East River served as an elegant flat for another couple, who kept house there with a thief the police had long sought, little suspecting that he was hiding under their very noses for months together. When the Children’s Aid Society first opened its lodging-houses, and with some difficulty persuaded the boys that their charity was no “pious dodge” to trap them into a treasonable “Sunday-school racket,” its managers overheard a laughable discussion among the boys in their unwontedly comfortable beds﻿—perhaps the first some of them had ever slept in﻿—as to the relative merits of the different styles of their everyday berths. Preferences were divided between the steam-grating and a sandbox; but the weight of the evidence was decided to be in favor of the sandbox, because, as its advocate put it, “you could curl all up in it.” The new “find” was voted a good way ahead of any previous experience, however. “My eyes, ain’t it nice!” said one of the lads, tucked in under his blanket up to the chin, and the roomful of boys echoed the sentiment. The compact silently made that night between the Street Arabs and their hosts has never been broken. They have been fast friends ever since.

===Chinese===
报馆街只是他们的大本营。这些街头流浪儿遍布全城：凡是白天有糊口的机会、夜里又能「钻被窝」并且保得不遭突袭的地段，就有他们。天气暖和时，街上一辆货车、一间现成的棚屋，或码头干草驳船里的一个洞窝，都是不坏的铺位。有人曾在哈莱姆桥（Harlem Bridge）旁一根大铁管的管头里发现两个男孩筑巢安身；东河边的一只旧锅炉则给另外两个男孩当了雅致的「公寓」（flat），他们同一名警察久缉不获的窃贼在那里一同持家过了好几个月，全然想不到他就藏在他们的鼻子底下。儿童援助协会（Children's Aid Society）初办寄宿所时，颇费了些力气才让男孩们相信，他们的善举不是诱他们落进大逆不道的「主日学校把戏」（“Sunday-school racket”）的「假仁假义的圈套」（“pious dodge”）。事后，管事们偷听到男孩们躺在那不寻常的舒适床铺上——那也许是一些人有生以来睡过的头一张床——争论日常各类铺位孰优孰劣，煞是有趣。蒸汽铁栅口和沙箱各有拥趸，但权衡证词，天平断定倒向沙箱，因为它的辩护人如是说：「你能把全身蜷成一团。」不过，大家一致认定，这个新「发现」远胜以往任何经验。「我的天，这可真不赖！」一个小伙子被子一直掖到下巴底下，这样感叹道，满屋男孩齐声附和。那天夜里，街头流浪儿与东道之间默默订下的契约，从此不曾破坏。他们一直是莫逆之交。

===Original===
Whence this army of homeless boys? is a question often asked. The answer is supplied by the procession of mothers that go out and in at Police Headquarters the year round, inquiring for missing boys, often not until they have been gone for weeks and months, and then sometimes rather as a matter of decent form than from any real interest in the lad’s fate. The stereotyped promise of the clerks who fail to find his name on the books among the arrests, that he “will come back when he gets hungry,” does not always come true. More likely he went away because he was hungry.
