Some are orphans, actually or in effect, thrown upon the world when their parents were “sent up” to the island or to Sing Sing, and somehow overlooked by the “Society,” which thenceforth became the enemy to be shunned until growth and dirt and the hardships of the street, that make old early, offer some hope of successfully floating the lie that they are “sixteen.” A drunken father explains the matter in other cases, as in that of John and Willie, aged ten and eight, picked up by the police. They “didn’t live nowhere,” never went to school, could neither read nor write. Their twelve-year-old sister kept house for the father, who turned the boys out to beg, or steal, or starve. Grinding poverty and hard work beyond the years of the lad; blows and curses for breakfast, dinner, and supper; all these are recruiting agents for the homeless army. Sickness in the house, too many mouths to feed:

===Chinese===
这支无家可归的男孩大军从何而来？这是常有人问的问题。答案由一年到头在警察总局（Police Headquarters）出出进进的母亲们的长队给出：她们来打听失踪的男孩，往往要等孩子走了几个星期、几个月才来，而且有时与其说是真心挂念孩子的命运，不如说是走个体面的过场。文书们在他的名字没有出现在被捕簿册上时，总会千篇一律地许诺说，他「饿了自会回来」；这话并不总是应验。更可能的是，他正是因为挨饿才走的。有的是孤儿——当真的孤儿，或与孤儿无异——父母被「送上去」关进了岛上的监狱（布莱克韦尔岛）或新新监狱（Sing Sing），把他们抛给了这个世界，又被「协会」不知怎么漏掉了；从此那协会便成了要躲开的仇敌，直到个头长起来、泥垢厚起来、吃够了街头那些使人早衰的苦头之后，才多少有了指望，能把「十六岁」这个谎话圆过去。在另一些例子里，一个酗酒的父亲便解释了一切，比如警察带回的十岁的约翰（John）和八岁的威利（Willie）。他们「哪儿也不住」，从没上过学，既不会读也不会写。他们十二岁的姐姐替父亲操持家务，父亲则把两个男孩撵出门去讨饭、偷窃或者挨饿。压得人喘不过气的贫穷，超出孩子年纪的重活；早中晚三餐换来的打骂：这一切都是这支无家可归大军的募兵员。屋里害着病，吃饭的嘴太多：

===Original===
“We wuz six,” said an urchin of twelve or thirteen I came across in the Newsboys’ Lodging House, “and we ain’t got no father. Some on us had to go.” And so he went, to make a living by blacking boots. The going is easy enough. There is very little to hold the boy who has never known anything but a home in a tenement. Very soon the wild life in the streets holds him fast, and thenceforward by his own effort there is no escape. Left alone to himself, he soon enough finds a place in the police books, and there would be no other answer to the second question: “what becomes of the boy?” than that given by the criminal courts every day in the week.

===Chinese===
「俺们家本来六个，」我在报童寄宿所里碰到的一个十二三岁的顽童说，「俺们又没有爹。总得有人走。」于是他就走了，靠擦鞋谋生。走出去是再容易不过的事。对于一个除了廉租公寓里的「家」什么都没见识过的孩子，能拴住他的东西实在少得很。用不了多久，街头那种野性的生活便把他攥牢了，从此凭他自己的力气再也脱不了身。任他自生自灭，他很快便会在警察的簿册上挣得一席之地；于是对第二个问题——「这孩子后来怎样了？」——便不会有别的答案，只剩刑事法庭一周七天每天给出的那一种。

===Original===
But he is not left alone. Society in our day has no such suicidal intention. Right here, at the parting of the ways, it has thrown up the strongest of all its defences for itself and for the boy. What the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children is to the baby-waif, the Children’s Aid Society is to the homeless boy at this real turning-point in his career. The good it has done cannot easily be overestimated. Its lodging-houses, its schools and its homes block every avenue of escape with their offer of shelter upon terms which the boy soon accepts, as on the whole cheap and fair. In the great Duane Street lodging-house for newsboys, they are succinctly stated in a “notice” over the door that reads thus: “Boys who swear and chew tobacco cannot sleep here.” There is another unwritten condition, viz.: that the boy shall be really without a home; but upon this the managers wisely do not insist too obstinately, accepting without too close inquiry his account of himself where that seems advisable, well knowing that many a home that sends forth such lads far less deserves the name than the one they are able to give them.

===Chinese===
但他并没有被撒手不管。我们这个时代的社会没有那种自戕的打算。恰恰在这岔路口上，社会为它自己、也为这孩子，筑起了全部防御工事中最坚固的一道。防止虐待儿童协会之于被弃的幼童，恰如儿童援助协会之于走到人生这真正转折点上的无家可归的男孩。它所行的善，怎么高估都不为过。它的寄宿所、学校和育幼之家以提出的收容条件堵住了每一条退路，而那些条件男孩很快就接受了，因为总的说来既便宜又公道。在杜安街（Duane Street）那所大型报童寄宿所里，这些条件被简明扼要地写进门上一张「告示」：「骂人和嚼烟草的男孩不得在此睡觉。」另有一条不成文的条件，即：这孩子必须是真正无家可归；但在这一条上，管事们明智地不至过分死抠，凡遇似乎相宜之处，便不加细究地采信他的自述——他们深知，许多把这类孩子送出门的「家」，远不如他们所能给孩子的那一个配得上「家」这个名字。

===Original===
With these simple preliminaries the outcast boy may enter. Rags do not count; to ignorance the door is only opened wider. Dirt does not survive long, once within the walls of the lodging-house. It is the settled belief of the men who conduct them that soap and water are as powerful moral agents in their particular field as preaching, and they have experience to back them. The boy may come and go as he pleases, so long as he behaves himself. No restraint of any sort is put on his independence. He is as free as any other guest at a hotel, and, like him, he is expected to pay for what he gets. How wisely the men planned who laid the foundation of this great rescue work and yet carry it on, is shown by no single feature of it better than by this. No pauper was ever bred within these houses. Nothing would have been easier with such material, or more fatal. But charity of the kind that pauperizes is furthest from their scheme. Self-help is its very keynote, and it strikes a response in the boy’s sturdiest trait that raises him at once to a level with the effort made in his behalf. Recognized as an independent trader, capable of and bound to take care of himself, he is in a position to ask trust if trade has gone against him and he cannot pay cash for his “grub” and his bed, and to get it without question. He can even have the loan of the small capital required to start him in business with a bootblack’s kit, or an armful of papers, if he is known or vouched for; but every cent is charged to him as carefully as though the transaction involved as many hundreds of dollars, and he is expected to pay back the money as soon as he has made enough to keep him going without it. He very rarely betrays the trust reposed in him. Quite on the contrary, around this sound core of self-help, thus encouraged, habits of thrift and ambitious industry are seen to grow up in a majority of instances. The boy is “growing” a character, and he goes out to the man’s work in life with that which for him is better than if he had found a fortune.

===Chinese===
办完这几道简单的进门手续，被弃的男孩便可入内。破衣烂衫不作数；对无知的人，门反而开得更大。一进寄宿所的院墙，肮脏便活不长久。主持这些所务的人有一个定见：在他们这个特殊行当里，肥皂和清水是同布道一样有力的道德力量，而且他们有经验作后盾。只要行为规矩，男孩来去自便。他的独立不受任何形式的拘束。他同旅馆里任何别的客人一样自由，而且同客人一样，享用了什么就要付钱。奠定这项伟大营救事业的基础并把它主持到今天的那班人筹划得何等明智，没有哪一点比这一条体现得更清楚。这些房子里从来没有养出过一个贫民。拿这样的材料来说，再没有比这更容易的事，也没有比这更致命的事了。但那种养懒人的慈善，同他们的方案相距最远。自助正是它的基调；这一调子敲在男孩最顽强的品性上，激起回响，立刻把他提升到与为他付出的努力相称的水平。他被当作一个独立的生意人对待——有能力、也有本分照料自己——于是，一旦买卖折了本、付不起「口粮」（grub）和床铺的现钱，他就有立场请求赊账，而且能毫无疑问地赊到。如果他为众人所知、或者有人作保，他甚至可以借到一小笔开业资本，置办一套擦鞋家伙什或一抱报纸；但每一个分币都一丝不苟地记在他的账上，仿佛这笔交易牵涉的是同样数目的几百美元；而且一俟挣到的钱足以无须借款维持生计，他就得把钱还清。他极少辜负托付给他的信任。恰恰相反，在这块受此激励的自助的坚实内核周围，人们看到节俭和上进的勤劳习惯在多数情形下生长起来。这男孩正在「长」出一副品格，带着它走出去担当人生中男子汉的工作；对他来说，这比捡到一笔财产还要好。

===Original===
Six cents for his bed, six for his breakfast of bread and coffee, and six for his supper of pork and beans, as much as he can eat, are the rates of the boys’ “hotel” for those who bunk together in the great dormitories that sometimes hold more than a hundred berths, two tiers high, made of iron, clean and neat. For the “upper ten,” the young financiers who early take the lead among their fellows, hire them to work for wages and add a share of their profits to their own, and for the lads who are learning a trade and getting paid by the week, there are ten-cent beds with a locker and with curtains hung about. Night schools and Sunday night meetings are held in the building and are always well attended, in winter especially, when the lodging-houses are crowded. In summer the towpath and the country attract their share of the bigger boys. The “Sunday-school racket” has ceased to have terror for them. They follow the proceedings with the liveliest interest, quick to detect cant of any sort, should any stray in. No one has any just conception of what congregational singing is until he has witnessed a roomful of these boys roll up their sleeves and start in on “I am a lily of the valley.” The swinging trapeze in the gymnasium on the top floor is scarcely more popular with the boys than this tremendously vocal worship. The Street Arab puts his whole little soul into what interests him for the moment, whether it be pulverizing a rival who has done a mean trick to a smaller boy, or attending at the “gospel shop” on Sundays. This characteristic made necessary some extra supervision when recently the lads in the Duane Street Lodging House “chipped in” and bought a set of boxing gloves. The trapeze suffered a temporary eclipse until this new toy had been tested to the extent of several miniature black eyes upon which soap had no effect, and sundry little scores had been settled that evened things up, as it were, for a fresh start.

===Chinese===
床铺六分钱，早饭面包加咖啡六分钱，晚饭猪肉烧豆子、管饱管够六分钱——这就是男孩们那家「旅馆」对合睡大宿舍者的价目；大宿舍有时摆着一百多个铺位，铁制，上下两层，干净齐整。至于「上流十子」（upper ten）——那班早早领先于同伴、雇人打工挣工钱、再把一份利润归入自己名下的少年金融家们——以及正在学手艺、按周领薪的少年们，则有十分钱的床位，带一只存物柜，四围还挂起幔子。楼里开设夜校和周日晚间聚会，出席者总是踊跃，冬天尤其如此，那时寄宿所里人满为患。夏天，纤道和乡野又把大些的男孩吸引去一批。「主日学校把戏」在他们眼里已不再可怕。他们怀着最活泼的兴趣跟进各项议程，一旦有什么伪善之言混了进来，立刻就会被识破。一个人若不曾亲眼见过满屋这样的男孩挽起袖子起头高唱《我是谷中百合花》（I am a lily of the valley），就完全谈不上对会众唱诗有何中肯的概念。顶层健身房里那只荡来荡去的吊杠，在男孩中间得宠的程度，也未必赛得过这场嗓音鼎沸的礼拜。街头流浪儿一旦对什么来了兴致，就把整个小小的灵魂投进去——不管是痛揍一个对更小的男孩使过下作手段的对头，还是礼拜天到「福音铺子」（“gospel shop”）去听道。正是这一品性，使杜安街寄宿所的少年们前不久「凑份子」买了一副拳击手套之后，额外的看管成了必要。吊杠一时失宠，直到这件新玩具被试到了这样的程度——试出好几只肥皂治不好的小小乌青眼，几笔小旧账也算了清，可以说是扯平了旧账，好重新开张。

===Original===
I tried one night, not with the best of success I confess, to photograph the boys in their washroom, while they were cleaning up for supper. They were quite turbulent, to the disgust of one of their number who assumed, unasked, the office of general manager of the show, and expressed his mortification to me in very polite language. “If they would only behave, sir!” he complained, “you could make a good picture.”

===Chinese===
一天晚上，我试着给盥洗室里的男孩们拍照——他们正洗收拾干净准备吃晚饭——我承认，成绩不算理想。他们闹腾得厉害，惹得他们当中一位大倒胃口；这位没人请、自己揽下「演出总管」差事的男孩，用非常有礼貌的语言向我表达了他的懊恼。「要是他们肯守点规矩，先生！」他抱怨道，「您本可以拍到一幅好照片的。」

===Original===
“Yes,” I said, “but it isn’t in them, I suppose.”

===Chinese===
「是啊，」我说，「不过我想，规矩这东西不在他们身上。」

===Original===
“No, b’gosh!” said he, lapsing suddenly from grace under the provocation, “them kids ain’t got no sense, nohow!”

===Chinese===
「可不，老天爷作证！」他说——受了这个刺激，霎时从斯文里跌了出来——「这帮小子压根儿就没脑子，怎么着都没有！」

===Original===
The Society maintains five of these boys’ lodging houses, and one for girls, in the city. The Duane Street Lodging House alone has sheltered since its foundation in 1855 nearly a quarter of a million different boys, at a total expense of a good deal less than half a million dollars. Of this amount, up to the beginning of the present year, the boys and the earnings of the house had contributed no less than $172,776.38. In all of the lodging-houses together, 12,153 boys and girls were sheltered and taught last year. The boys saved up no inconsiderable amount of money in the savings banks provided for them in the houses, a simple system of lockboxes that are emptied for their benefit once a month. Besides these, the Society has established and operates in the tenement districts twenty-one industrial schools, coordinate with the public schools in authority, for the children of the poor who cannot find room in the city’s schoolhouses, or are too ragged to go there; two free reading-rooms, a dressmaking and typewriting school and a laundry for the instruction of girls; a sick-children’s mission in the city and two on the seashore, where poor mothers may take their babies; a cottage by the sea for crippled girls, and a brush factory for crippled boys in Forty-fourth Street. The Italian school in Leonard Street, alone, had an average attendance of over six hundred pupils last year. The daily average attendance at all of them was 4,105, while 11,331 children were registered and taught. When the fact that there were among these 1,132 children of drunken parents, and 416 that had been found begging in the street, is contrasted with the showing of $1,337.21 deposited in the school savings banks by 1,745 pupils, something like an adequate idea is gained of the scope of the Society’s work in the city.

===Chinese===
协会在全城维持着五所这样的男孩寄宿所，另有一所收女孩。单是杜安街寄宿所，自 1855 年创办以来，收留过的不同男孩就将近二十五万，总开销却远远不到五十万美元。在这笔款项中，截至今年年初，男孩们和寄宿所自身的进项贡献了不下 172,776.38 美元。各寄宿所合计，去年共收留并施教男孩女孩 12,153 名。男孩们在所内为他们设的储蓄银行里攒下了数目可观的一笔钱——那是一套简单的上锁存匣制度，每月为他们清点兑付一次。除此以外，协会还在廉租公寓地带开办并管理着二十一所工业学校（industrial schools），其权责与公立学校相当，收容的是在市立校舍里挤不到座位、或穿得太破上不了学的贫家孩子；两间免费阅览室；一所裁缝与打字学校和一间洗衣作坊，用以教导女孩；一处设在城里的病儿救济所和两处设在海滨、可供贫苦母亲携婴儿前往的病儿之所；一幢海滨小屋收留跛足女孩，第四十四街（Forty-fourth Street）的一间制刷工场收留跛足男孩。单是伦纳德街（Leonard Street）的意大利人学校，去年平均到校学生就超过六百名。所有这些学校每日平均到校人数为 4,105 人，登记在册并受教的孩子共 11,331 名。这些孩子中有 1,132 名出自酗酒父母之家，416 名曾被发现在街头行乞；把这个事实同另一项显示对照——1,745 名学生向学校储蓄银行存入了 1,337.21 美元——协会在这座城市里工作的规模如何，便大致有了一个恰如其分的概念。

===Original===
A large share of it, in a sense the largest, certainly that productive of the happiest results, lies outside of the city, however. From the lodging-houses and the schools are drawn the battalions of young emigrants that go every year to homes in the Far West, to grow up self-supporting men and women safe from the temptations and the vice of the city. Their number runs far up in the thousands. The Society never loses sight of them. The records show that the great mass, with this start given them, become useful citizens, an honor to the communities in which their lot is cast. Not a few achieve place and prominence in their new surroundings. Rarely bad reports come of them. Occasionally one comes back, lured by homesickness even for the slums; but the briefest stay generally cures the disease for good. I helped once to see a party off for Michigan, the last sent out by that great friend of the homeless children, Mrs. Astor, before she died. In the party was a boy who had been an “Insider” at the Five Points House of Industry, and brought along as his only baggage a padlocked and iron-bound box that contained all his wealth, two little white mice of the friendliest disposition. They were going with him out to live on the fat of the land in the fertile West, where they would never be wanting for a crust. Alas! for the best-laid plans of mice and men. The Western diet did not agree with either. I saw their owner some months later in the old home at the Five Points. He had come back, walking part of the way, and was now pleading to be sent out once more. He had at last had enough of the city. His face fell when I asked him about the mice. It was a sad story, indeed. “They had so much corn to eat,” he said, “and they couldn’t stand it. They burned all up inside, and then they busted.”

===Chinese===
然而，这项工作的一大块——从某种意义上说是最大的一块，而且确乎是结出最幸福果实的一块——却在城外。一营一营的少年移民，每年从各寄宿所和学校里征募出来，送往远西部（the Far West）的家庭，在那里长成自食其力的男女，远离城市的种种诱惑与罪恶。他们的数目远达数千。协会从不失去他们的音讯。记录表明，这大批的孩子得了这个起点之后，大多成了有用的公民，为他们日后安身其中的社区增光。不少人在新的环境里赢得了地位和声名。关于他们的坏消息难得传来。偶尔有一个被乡愁引了回来——哪怕是对贫民窟的乡愁；但通常只消小住片刻，这毛病便根治了。有一回，我帮着送别一批前往密歇根（Michigan）的孩子，那是无家可归儿童的伟大朋友阿斯特夫人（Mrs. Astor）生前送出的最后一批。队伍里有一个男孩，原是五点区工艺院（Five Points House of Industry）里的「内宿的」（Insider）；随身的全部行李只有一只上了挂锁、包着铁皮的箱子，里面装着他全部的家当——两只性子顶亲昵的小白鼠。它们将随他到肥沃的西部去享用膏腴之地，在那里永远不愁一口吃的。唉！老鼠和人的如意算盘，终究都靠不住。西部的伙食，它们俩谁也消受不起。几个月后，我在五点区的老家见到了它们的主人。他回来了，一段路还是步行走回来的，如今正恳求再把他送出去。他总算受够了这座城市。我向他问起那两只老鼠时，他的脸沉了下来。那确乎是个悲惨的故事。「玉米多得吃不完，」他说，「它们受不住。肚里全都烧起来了，然后就胀破了。」

===Original===
Mrs. Astor set an example during her noble and useful life in gathering every year a company of homeless boys from the streets and sending them to good homes, with decent clothes on their backs﻿—she had sent out no less than thirteen hundred when she died, and left funds to carry on her work﻿—that has been followed by many who, like her, had the means and the heart for such a labor of love. Most of the lodging-houses and school-buildings of the society were built by some one rich man or woman who paid all the bills, and often objected to have even the name of the giver made known to the world. It is one of the pleasant experiences of life that give one hope and courage in the midst of all this misery to find names, that stand to the unthinking mass only for money-getting and grasping, associated with such unheralded benefactions that carry their blessings down to generations yet unborn. It is not so long since I found the carriage of a woman, whose name is synonymous with millions, standing in front of the boys’ lodging-house in Thirty-fifth Street. Its owner was at that moment busy with a surgeon making a census of the crippled lads in the brush-shop, the most miserable of all the Society’s charges, as a preliminary to fitting them out with artificial limbs.

===Chinese===
阿斯特夫人在她高贵而有为的一生中立下了一个榜样，后来者随之一一效法——他们都同她一样，既有财力、又有心肠来做这样一件爱的劳作。她每年从街头收聚一批无家可归的男孩，给他们背上换上体面的衣裳，送到良善人家；去世时，她已送出不少于一千三百名，还留下基金把这项事业延续下去。协会的寄宿所和校舍，大多由某一位富人或贵妇独力兴建、付清全部账单，而且往往连捐赠者的姓名也不愿让世人知晓。在这一切苦难之中，发现有些名字——在不假思索的芸芸大众眼里只意味着敛财与贪婪的名字——竟同这样一些不事声张的善举连在一起，福泽绵延及于尚未出世的世世代代，这是人生中令人快慰的经历之一，给人以希望和勇气。就在不久以前，我还看见一位妇人的马车停在三十五街（Thirty-fifth Street）男孩寄宿所门前，她的名字简直就是「百万」的同义词。那一刻，车的主人正同一位外科医生忙着给制刷工场里的跛足少年点数造册——那是协会照管的所有孩子中最不幸的一批——作为给他们装配假肢的先期准备。

===Original===
Farther uptown than any reared by the Children’s Aid Society, in Sixty-seventh Street, stands a lodging-house intended for boys of a somewhat larger growth than most of those whom the Society shelters. Unlike the others, too, it was built by the actual labor of the young men it was designed to benefit. In the day when more of the boys from our streets shall find their way to it and to the New York Trade Schools, of which it is a kind of home annex, we shall be in a fair way of solving in the most natural of all ways the question what to do with this boy, in spite of the ignorant opposition of the men whose tyrannical policy is now to blame for the showing that, out of twenty-three millions of dollars paid annually to mechanics in the building trades in this city, less than six millions go to the workman born in New York, while his boy roams the streets with every chance of growing up a vagabond and next to none of becoming an honest artisan. Colonel Auchmuty is a practical philanthropist to whom the growing youth of New York will one day owe a debt of gratitude not easily paid. The progress of the system of trade schools established by him, at which a young man may acquire the theory as well as the practice of a trade in a few months at a merely nominal outlay, has not been nearly as rapid as was to be desired, though the fact that other cities are copying the model, with their master mechanics as the prime movers in the enterprise, testifies to its excellence. But it has at last taken a real start, and with union men and even the officers of unions now sending their sons to the trade schools to be taught,19 one may perhaps be permitted to hope that an era of better sense is dawning that shall witness a rescue work upon lines which, when the leaven has fairly had time to work, will put an end to the existence of the New York Street Arab, of the native breed at least.

===Chinese===
在第六十七街（Sixty-seventh Street），矗立着一所寄宿所，比儿童援助协会所建的任何一所都更靠上城，收容的是比协会寻常照管的男孩年岁略长一些的少年。它同别所还有一点不同：它是由那些它打算造福的年轻人亲手劳作建成的。等到我们街头的男孩有更多的找到通往它和纽约工艺学校（New York Trade Schools）的路——它之于工艺学校，犹如一处附设的家舍——的那一天，我们便大有希望以一切办法中最自然的一种，来解决「拿这孩子怎么办」的问题；尽管有些人愚昧作梗，难辞其咎，正是他们那套专横的政策造成了眼下这样的局面：本市建筑行业每年付给工匠的两千三百万美元中，落到生于纽约的工人手里的不足六百万，而他的儿子却在街头游荡，长成流浪汉的机会样样俱全，长成诚实工匠的机会却近乎于无。奥克穆蒂上校（Colonel Auchmuty）是一位脚踏实地的慈善家，纽约正在成长的青年一代终有一天要欠他一笔不易偿还的感激之债。由他创立的工艺学校制度——年轻人只消一笔象征性的花费，便可在几个月内学到一门手艺的理论与实践——进展得远不如人意地迅速；不过，其他城市正在仿效这个样板，而且以各自的师傅工匠为这项事业的主要推动者，这一事实足证其优越。但它毕竟已真正起步；如今连工会会员、甚至工会的干事们也在把儿子送进工艺学校受教（19），或许可以容人指望：一个更有见识的时代正在破晓，它将见证一场循此路线的营救工作——等到酵母有足够的时间充分发起来，这场工作终将结束纽约街头流浪儿的存在，至少是本地品种的那一种。

## XVIII The Reign of Rum / XVIII 朗姆酒当道

===Original===
Where God builds a church the devil builds next door﻿—a saloon, is an old saying that has lost its point in New York. Either the devil was on the ground first, or he has been doing a good deal more in the way of building. I tried once to find out how the account stood, and counted to 111 Protestant churches, chapels, and places of worship of every kind below Fourteenth Street, 4,065 saloons. The worst half of the tenement population lives down there, and it has to this day the worst half of the saloons. Uptown the account stands a little better, but there are easily ten saloons to every church today. I am afraid, too, that the congregations are larger by a good deal; certainly the attendance is steadier and the contributions more liberal the week round, Sunday included. Turn and twist it as we may, over against every bulwark for decency and morality which society erects, the saloon projects its colossal shadow, omen of evil wherever it falls into the lives of the poor.

===Chinese===
「上帝在哪里盖起教堂，魔鬼就在隔壁盖起一家酒馆」——这句老话在纽约已经失了锋芒。要么是魔鬼先到了场，要么是他盖起的东西实在多得多。有一回我想弄清这笔账究竟如何，数了数第十四街（Fourteenth Street）以南的新教教堂、礼拜堂和各式聚会所，共 111 处；酒馆呢，4,065 家。廉租公寓（tenement）人口中最糟的那一半就住在那里，而且直到今天，那里也还占着酒馆中最糟的一半。上城的账面略好一点，可如今每有一座教堂，轻轻松松就对得上十家酒馆。我还担心，那些「会众」的规模更要大得多；至少出勤更稳定，奉献也更慷慨，一个礼拜天天如此，星期天也不例外。任凭我们怎样左遮右拦，社会为体统与道德筑起的每一道壁垒对面，都横着酒馆投下的庞大阴影——这阴影落进穷人的生活，落到哪里，哪里就是凶兆。


===Original===
Nowhere is its mark so broad or so black. To their misery it sticketh closer than a brother, persuading them that within its doors only is refuge, relief. It has the best of the argument, too, for it is true, worse pity, that in many a tenement-house block the saloon is the one bright and cheery and humanly decent spot to be found. It is a sorry admission to make, that to bring the rest of the neighborhood up to the level of the saloon would be one way of squelching it; but it is so. Wherever the tenements thicken, it multiplies. Upon the direst poverty of their crowds it grows fat and prosperous, levying upon it a tax heavier than all the rest of its grievous burdens combined. It is not yet two years since the Excise Board made the rule that no three corners of any street-crossing, not already so occupied, should thenceforward be licensed for rum-selling. And the tardy prohibition was intended for the tenement districts. Nowhere else is there need of it. One may walk many miles through the homes of the poor searching vainly for an open reading-room, a cheerful coffeehouse, a decent club that is not a cloak for the traffic in rum. The dramshop yawns at every step, the poor man’s club, his forum and his haven of rest when weary and disgusted with the crowding, the quarrelling, and the wretchedness at home. With the poison dealt out there he takes his politics, in quality not far apart. As the source, so the stream. The rumshop turns the political crank in New York. The natural yield is rum politics. Of what that means, successive Boards of Aldermen, composed in a measure, if not of a majority, of dive-keepers, have given New York a taste. The disgrace of the infamous “Boodle Board” will be remembered until some corruption even fouler crops out and throws it into the shade.

===Chinese===
酒馆的印记，没有哪里比这里更宽、更黑。对穷人的苦难，它比亲兄弟贴得还紧，劝他们相信：只有它的门里，才有避难，才有解脱。在这场争辩中它还占着上风，因为——说来更是可悲——在许多廉租公寓街区，酒馆确实是能找到的惟一亮堂、快活、尚有人味的体面去处。把街坊其余的地方都提到酒馆的水准，就算是一种压垮它的法子——这话认下来实在难堪，可事实如此。廉租公寓稠密到哪里，它就成倍地增多到哪里。它靠人群中最凄惨的贫穷，养得自己肥壮兴旺，向这贫穷征一笔税，比压在它身上的其余一切沉重负担加在一起还要重。酒类许可委员会（Excise Board）立下规矩——至今还不满两年：任何街口，凡尚未如此占用的街角，此后一概不得再发朗姆酒执照，不许任何一个路口凑足三个卖酒的街角。这条姗姗来迟的禁令，正是为廉租公寓街区而设。别处用不着它。你可以在穷人的家园之间走上许多英里，徒劳地寻一处开放的阅览室、一家明朗的咖啡馆、一个不当朗姆酒买卖幌子的像样俱乐部。而零沽酒肆（dramshop）却在每一步上打着呵欠张着口——这是穷人的俱乐部，他的论坛，他歇脚的港湾：当他对家里的拥挤、争吵和凄惨感到疲惫厌倦的时候，便来此处安身。在那里分发出来的毒汁，他连同自己的政治一并领受，两样东西品质相差不远。源头如此，河水亦然。在纽约，摇政治曲柄的是朗姆酒馆。天然的出产，便是朗姆酒政治。这究竟是什么滋味，历届市议会——就算够不上多数、也有相当一部分由酒窟老板组成——已经让纽约尝过了。那声名狼藉的「贿金委员会」（“Boodle Board”）的耻辱，人们会长久记得，直到某种更肮脏的腐败冒出头来，把它也盖进影子里去。


===Original===
What relation the saloon bears to the crowds, let me illustrate by a comparison. Below Fourteenth Street were, when the Health Department took its first accurate census of the tenements a year and a half ago, 13,220 of the 32,390 buildings classed as such in the whole city. Of the eleven hundred thousand tenants, not quite half a million, embracing a host of more than sixty-three thousand children under five years of age, lived below that line. Below it, also, were 234 of the cheap lodging-houses accounted for by the police last year, with a total of four millions and a half of lodgers for the twelvemonth, 59 of the city’s 110 pawnshops, and 4,065 of its 7,884 saloons. The four most densely peopled precincts, the Fourth, Sixth, Tenth, and Eleventh, supported together in round numbers twelve hundred saloons, and their returns showed twenty-seven percent of the whole number of arrests for the year. The Eleventh Precinct, that has the greatest and the poorest crowds of all﻿—it is the Tenth Ward﻿—and harbored one-third of the army of homeless lodgers and fourteen percent of all the prisoners of the year, kept 485 saloons going in 1889. It is not on record that one of them all failed for want of support. A number of them, on the contrary, had brought their owners wealth and prominence. From their bars these eminent citizens stepped proudly into the councils of the city and the State. The very floor of one of the barrooms, in a neighborhood that lately resounded with the cry for bread of starving workmen, is paved with silver dollars!

===Chinese===
酒馆同这些人潮是什么关系，且让我用一个比较来说明。一年半以前，卫生局（Health Department）第一次对廉租公寓作了精确普查：全市归入此类的房屋共 32,390 座，其中 13,220 座在第十四街以南。一百一十万廉租公寓房客中，将近五十万人住在那条线以南，其中裹着六万三千多名五岁以下的儿童。那条线以南，还有警方去年清点在册的 234 家廉价寄宿所（cheap lodging-house），全年寄宿总计四百五十万人次；有全市 110 家当铺中的 59 家；还有全市 7,884 家酒馆中的 4,065 家。人口最稠密的四个警区——第四、第六、第十和第十一警区——加在一起，取整数约供养着一千二百家酒馆，而全年逮捕总次数的百分之二十七，都记在它们的账上。第十一警区拥有全城人数最多、也最穷的人群——它就是第十选区（the Tenth Ward）——收容了无家可归的寄宿大军的三分之一，还有全年人犯的百分之十四；1889 年，那里养活着 485 家酒馆。有案可查的是，其中没有一家因缺少捧场的而倒闭。相反，有好几家还替它们的老板挣来了财富和名望。这些显赫的公民正是从自家的酒吧台后面，昂然跨进了本市和本州的议事场所。就在不久前还回荡着饥饿工人讨面包呼声的一个街区里，有一家酒吧间的地面，竟是用银元铺成的！


===Original===
East Side poverty is not alone in thus rewarding the tyrants that sweeten its cup of bitterness with their treacherous poison. The Fourth Ward points with pride to the honorable record of the conductors of its “Tub of Blood,” and a dozen barrooms with less startling titles; the West Side to the wealth and “social” standing of the owners of such resorts as the “Witches’ Broth” and the “Plug Hat” in the region of Hell’s Kitchen three-cent whiskey, names ominous of the concoctions brewed there and of their fatally generous measure. Another ward, that boasts some of the best residences and the bluest blood on Manhattan Island, honors with political leadership in the ruling party the proprietor of one of the most disreputable Black-and-Tan dives and dancing-hells to be found anywhere. Criminals and policemen alike do him homage. The list might be strung out to make texts for sermons with a stronger home flavor than many that are preached in our pulpits on Sunday. But I have not set out to write the political history of New York. Besides, the list would not be complete. Secret dives are skulking in the slums and out of them, that are not labelled respectable by a Board of Excise and support no “family entrance.” Their business, like that of the stale-beer dives, is done through a side-door the week through. No one knows the number of unlicensed saloons in the city. Those who have made the matter a study estimate it at a thousand, more or less. The police make occasional schedules of a few and report them to headquarters. Perhaps there is a farce in the police court, and there the matter ends. Rum and “influence” are synonymous terms. The interests of the one rarely suffer for the want of attention from the other.

===Chinese===
用奸诈的毒汁给苦杯加甜、从而如此犒赏自家暴君的，不止下东区（East Side）一处的贫穷。第四选区（Fourth Ward）自豪地指给人们看它那家「血桶」（“Tub of Blood”）——还有一打名头不那么骇人的酒吧间——的经营者们何等体面的记录；西区（West Side）则指给人们看「女巫汤」（“Witches’ Broth”）和「大礼帽」（“Plug Hat”）这一类场所主人的财富与「社交」地位——它们盘踞在地狱厨房（Hell’s Kitchen）那三分钱威士忌的地界上，两个名字不祥地预示着里头熬的是什么配方，也预示着那分量慷慨得足以致命。还有一个选区，夸耀着曼哈顿岛上数得着的几处最好宅邸和最纯正的贵族血统，却把执政党里的政治领袖地位，敬献给一位老板——他开的那家黑白混杂酒窟兼跳舞地狱，无论在哪里都数得上最不体面的。罪犯和警察一齐向他致敬。这份名单本来还可以一直开下去，开成一篇篇布道经文，比我们许多讲坛上星期天所讲的更带本土的辣味。但我并不是存心要来写纽约的政治史。何况，这名单也开不全。秘密酒窟正鬼鬼祟祟地藏身于贫民窟内外，酒类许可委员会不曾给它们贴上体面的标签，它们也不设「家庭入口」（“family entrance”）。它们的生意，同坏啤酒窟（stale-beer dive）一样，整个礼拜都从一扇边门里做成。谁也不知道全市无照酒馆的数目。认真研究过这个问题的人估计，总在一千家上下。警察偶尔开出几张单子，把其中几家报给总局。治安法庭上兴许演一出闹剧，事情便到此为止。朗姆酒和「势力」是同义语。这一位的利益，很少会因为那一位照应不上而吃了亏。


===Original===
With the exception of these free lances that treat the law openly with contempt, the saloons all hang out a sign announcing in fat type that no beer or liquor is sold to children. In the downtown “morgues” that make the lowest degradation of tramp-humanity pan out a paying interest, as in the “reputable resorts” uptown where Inspector Byrnes’s men spot their worthier quarry elbowing citizens whom the idea of associating with a burglar would give a shock they would not get over for a week, this sign is seen conspicuously displayed. Though apparently it means submission to a beneficent law, in reality the sign is a heartless, cruel joke. I doubt if one child in a thousand, who brings his growler to be filled at the average New York bar, is sent away empty-handed, if able to pay for what he wants. I once followed a little boy, who shivered in bare feet on a cold November night so that he seemed in danger of smashing his pitcher on the icy pavement, into a Mulberry Street saloon where just such a sign hung on the wall, and forbade the barkeeper to serve the boy. The man was as astonished at my interference as if I had told him to shut up his shop and go home, which in fact I might have done with as good a right, for it was after 1 a.m., the legal closing hour. He was mighty indignant too, and told me roughly to go away and mind my business, while he filled the pitcher. The law prohibiting the selling of beer to minors is about as much respected in the tenement-house districts as the ordinance against swearing. Newspaper readers will recall the story, told little more than a year ago, of a boy who after carrying beer a whole day for a shopful of men over on the East Side, where his father worked, crept into the cellar to sleep off the effects of his own share in the rioting. It was Saturday evening. Sunday his parents sought him high and low; but it was not until Monday morning, when the shop was opened, that he was found, killed and half-eaten by the rats that overran the place.

===Chinese===
除了这些公然蔑视法律的散兵游勇，各家酒馆无一不挂出一块招牌，用粗大的字体宣布：不向儿童出售啤酒或烈酒。在下城那些「陈尸所」（“morgues”）里——流浪汉人性最低一层的堕落，到了那里也能生出可观的利息——同在上城那些「体面场所」（“reputable resorts”）里——伯恩斯探长手下的警探正是在那里物色更有分量的猎物，看那些猎物同一些公民摩肩接肘，而那些公民一想到自己竟同夜盗之流厮混，所受的惊吓一个星期都缓不过来——这块招牌都悬挂得格外醒目。它看似表示对一部善法的顺从，实际上却是一个无情而残忍的玩笑。一个提着「打酒桶」（growler）到纽约寻常吧台前打酒的孩子，只要付得起他要的酒钱，一千个里也未必有一个被空手打发走，这一点我很怀疑。一年十一月的一个寒夜，一个小男孩光着脚冻得直抖，仿佛眼看就要把酒壶摔碎在结了冰的人行道上；我跟着他走进桑树街（Mulberry Street）的一家酒馆，那面墙上正挂着这么一块招牌，我便禁止掌柜的给这孩子打酒。他见我出面干预，惊讶得就像我劝他关店回家一样——而说实话，我倒真有同样正当的权利这么劝，因为那时已是凌晨一点过后，法定的打烊钟点。他还火冒三丈，一面给酒壶灌满，一面粗暴地叫我走开、管好自己的事。禁止向未成年人售啤酒的法律，在廉租公寓街区所受的尊重，大约同那条禁止骂人的条例不相上下。读报的人想必还记得一年多前传开的那件事：东区那头一家工场里——男孩的父亲就在那里做工——他替满工场的男人跑了一整天腿买啤酒，末了溜进地窖，去睡掉自己那份狂欢的酒劲。那是星期六晚上。星期天他的父母找遍了他的踪影；可直到星期一早晨工场开门，他才被人发现——已经被咬死了，身子叫满处横行的老鼠啃掉了半边。


===Original===
All the evil the saloon does in breeding poverty and in corrupting politics; all the suffering it brings into the lives of its thousands of innocent victims, the wives and children of drunkards it sends forth to curse the community; its fostering of crime and its shielding of criminals﻿—it is all as nothing to this, its worst offence. In its affinity for the thief there is at least this compensation that, as it makes, it also unmakes him. It starts him on his career only to trip him up and betray him into the hands of the law, when the rum he exchanged for his honesty has stolen his brains as well. For the corruption of the child there is no restitution. None is possible. It saps the very vitals of society; undermines its strongest defences, and delivers them over to the enemy. Fostered and filled by the saloon, the “growler” looms up in the New York street boy’s life, baffling the most persistent efforts to reclaim him. There is no escape from it; no hope for the boy, once its blighting grip is upon him. Thenceforward the logic of the slums, that the world which gave him poverty and ignorance for his portion “owes him a living,” is his creed, and the career of the “tough” lies open before him, a beaten track to be blindly followed to a bad end in the wake of the growler.

===Chinese===
酒馆在孳生贫困、败坏政治上作下的种种恶；它带进成千上万无辜受害者生活里的种种苦——那些被它放出家门去祸害社会的酒鬼的妻子儿女；它对犯罪的豢养、对罪犯的包庇——比起这一桩、它最重的罪过来，全都算不得什么。它同窃贼的亲和里至少还有这么一点补偿：它既然造就了他，也就同样毁掉他。它把他送上作恶的生涯，又趁他用诚实换来的朗姆酒把他的脑子也一并偷走之后，绊他一个跟头，把他出卖到法律手里。可是对孩子的腐蚀，没有赔偿可言，任何赔偿都不可能。它蛀蚀的是社会的命脉本身；它挖毁社会最坚固的防线，把它们拱手交给敌人。受酒馆豢养、在酒馆灌满的「打酒桶」，在纽约街头男孩的生活里赫然耸起，令一切想把他挽救回来的最坚韧的努力也束手无策。它一旦把那只摧残人的爪子攥上这男孩，便再无逃脱之路、再无得救之望。从那时起，贫民窟的那套逻辑——那个把贫穷和无知派给他当份的世界「欠他一口饭吃」——便成了他的信条；恶棍的生涯在他面前豁然铺开：一条踩熟的道儿，让他盲目地跟在打酒桶后面一路走到坏的结局。

## XIX The Harvest of Tares / XIX 稗子的收获

===Original===
The “growler” stood at the cradle of the tough. It bosses him through his boyhood apprenticeship in the “gang,” and leaves him, for a time only, at the door of the jail that receives him to finish his training and turn him loose upon the world a thief, to collect by stealth or by force the living his philosophy tells him that it owes him, and will not voluntarily surrender without an equivalent in the work which he hates. From the moment he, almost a baby, for the first time carries the growler for beer, he is never out of its reach, and the two soon form a partnership that lasts through life. It has at least the merit, such as it is, of being loyal. The saloon is the only thing that takes kindly to the lad. Honest play is interdicted in the streets. The policeman arrests the ball-tossers, and there is no room in the backyard. In one of these, between two enormous tenements that swarmed with children, I read this ominous notice: “All boys caught in this yard will be delt with accorden to law.”

===Chinese===
「打酒桶」（growler）就站在恶棍的摇篮旁。在他童年于「帮派」中当学徒的整个年月里，都是它在支使着他；之后它也只离开他一时——把他送到监狱门口为止。那监狱收下他，替他完成学业，再把他放到世上做一个窃贼，让他去用偷或抢讨取生活之资——他的哲学告诉他，这世界欠他一口饭吃，而且若不拿出他所痛恨的劳动作抵偿，这世界绝不肯自愿交出来。从他几乎还是个吃奶娃娃、头一回提着打酒桶去打啤酒的那一刻起，他就再没有离开过它的势力范围，两者很快结成一桩延续终身的合伙关系。这关系至少有这么一条可取之处——姑且算它可取——忠实不渝。酒馆是唯一肯亲近这小子的东西。街头不容正当的游戏。警察要抓抛球玩的孩子，后院里又没有空地。在这样的一处后院——夹在两座塞满了孩子的高大廉租公寓之间——我读到过这么一条不祥的告示：「凡在本院拿获的男孩，均将依律惩办。」

===Original===
Along the waterfronts, in the holes of the dock-rats, and on the avenues, the young tough finds plenty of kindred spirits. Every corner has its gang, not always on the best of terms with the rivals in the next block, but all with a common programme: defiance of law and order, and with a common ambition: to get “pinched,” i.e., arrested, so as to pose as heroes before their fellows. A successful raid on the grocer’s till is a good mark, “doing up” a policeman cause for promotion. The gang is an institution in New York. The police deny its existence while nursing the bruises received in nightly battles with it that tax their utmost resources. The newspapers chronicle its doings daily, with a sensational minuteness of detail that does its share toward keeping up its evil traditions and inflaming the ambition of its members to be as bad as the worst. The gang is the ripe fruit of tenement-house growth. It was born there, endowed with a heritage of instinctive hostility to restraint by a generation that sacrificed home to freedom, or left its country for its country’s good. The tenement received and nursed the seed. The intensity of the American temper stood sponsor to the murderer in what would have been the common “bruiser” of a more phlegmatic clime. New York’s tough represents the essence of reaction against the old and the new oppression, nursed in the rank soil of its slums. Its gangs are made up of the American-born sons of English, Irish, and German parents. They reflect exactly the conditions of the tenements from which they sprang. Murder is as congenial to Cherry Street or to Battle Row, as quiet and order to Murray Hill. The “assimilation” of Europe’s oppressed hordes, upon which our Fourth of July orators are fond of dwelling, is perfect. The product is our own.

===Chinese===
沿着滨水地带，在码头鼠（dock-rats）的窟窿里，在一条条大道上，年轻的恶棍找得到大把气味相投的伙伴。每个街角都有它的帮派，同邻街的对手未必相处得和睦，但全都有同一个纲领：蔑视法律与秩序；也都有同一个抱负：挨一次「掐」，也就是被逮捕，好在同伙面前充一回英雄。成功地偷了杂货店的钱匣是一枚好奖章，「收拾」一名警察则是擢升的资本。帮派在纽约是一种建制。警察一面否认它的存在，一面揉着夜夜同它开战换来的浑身瘀伤——那些战斗把他们最大的本事都使尽了。报纸天天记载它的行止，那种耸人听闻的巨细靡遗，也为维系它的邪恶传统、煽动它的成员争取坏到极处的雄心尽了一份力。帮派是廉租公寓滋长出来的熟透的果实。它就诞生在那里，由牺牲了家庭去换取自由、或为国家的利益离开了祖国的一代人，赋予它一份对一切管束本能仇视的遗产。廉租公寓收下了这粒种子，加以哺育。美国脾气的烈度给杀人凶徒当了教父——若在一个更沉滞的国度，他本不过是寻常的「打手」。纽约的恶棍体现着在贫民窟的沃土中养大的、对旧压迫与新压迫之反动的精髓。这些帮派由英国人、爱尔兰人和德国移民生在美国的儿子们组成。他们恰恰映照出其所由出的那些廉租公寓的境况。杀人之于樱桃街或「厮杀排」，正如安宁与秩序之于默里山，都是与生俱来的习性。我们的七月四日演说家们津津乐道的、对欧洲受压迫人群的「同化」，可谓尽善尽美。产品是我们自己的。

===Original===
Such is the genesis of New York’s gangs. Their history is not so easily written. It would embrace the largest share of our city’s criminal history for two generations back, every page of it dyed red with blood. The guillotine Paris set up a century ago to avenge its wrongs was not more relentless, or less discriminating, than this Nemesis of New York. The difference is of intent. Murder with that was the serious purpose; with ours it is the careless incident, the wanton brutality of the moment. Bravado and robbery are the real purposes of the gangs; the former prompts the attack upon the policeman, the latter that upon the citizen. Within a single week last spring, the newspapers recorded six murderous assaults on unoffending people, committed by young highwaymen in the public streets. How many more were suppressed by the police, who always do their utmost to hush up such outrages “in the interests of justice,” I shall not say. There has been no lack of such occurrences since, as the records of the criminal courts show. In fact, the past summer has seen, after a period of comparative quiescence of the gangs, a reawakening to renewed turbulence of the East Side tribes, and over and over again the reserve forces of a precinct have been called out to club them into submission. It is a peculiarity of the gangs that they usually break out in spots, as it were. When the West Side is in a state of eruption, the East Side gangs “lie low,” and when the toughs along the North River are nursing broken heads at home, or their revenge in Sing Sing, fresh trouble breaks out in the tenements east of Third Avenue. This result is brought about by the very efforts made by the police to put down the gangs. In spite of local feuds, there is between them a species of ruffianly Freemasonry that readily admits to full fellowship a hunted rival in the face of the common enemy. The gangs belt the city like a huge chain from the Battery to Harlem﻿—the collective name of the “chain gang” has been given to their scattered groups in the belief that a much closer connection exists between them than commonly supposed﻿—and the ruffian for whom the East Side has became too hot, has only to step across town and change his name, a matter usually much easier for him than to change his shirt, to find a sanctuary in which to plot fresh outrages. The more notorious he is, the warmer the welcome, and if he has “done” his man he is by common consent accorded the leadership in his new field.

===Chinese===
纽约诸帮派的发祥便是如此。它们的历史却不那么容易写。写起来将囊括我们这座城市两代人以来的刑事史中最大的一部分，页页浸透着血。一个世纪前巴黎为昭雪自己的冤屈而竖起的断头台，比起纽约的这位涅墨西斯（Nemesis）来，既不更冷酷，也不更不辨善恶。差别在于用意。在巴黎，杀人是郑重其事的目的；在我们这里，它是漫不经心的插曲，是一时兴起的恣意暴行。虚张声势与劫掠才是帮派的真正目的；前者驱使他们袭击警察，后者驱使他们袭击市民。去年春天，单单一个星期之内，报纸就记载了六起少年路匪在通衢大街上对无辜路人行凶杀人的案子。至于警察又压下了多少——为了「司法的利益」，他们总是竭力捂住这类暴行——我就不说了。自那以后这类事从未断过，刑事法院的案卷可以为证。事实上，刚刚过去的这个夏天，在帮派们相对消停了一段之后，下东区的各个部落重又醒来，再度兴风作浪，一个分局的预备队被一次又一次地叫出来，用警棍把他们打服。帮派有一个特点：它们往往像出疹子一样一处一处地发作。西区正闹得如火如荼时，下东区的帮派便「蛰伏」起来；北河沿岸的恶棍们正在家里养着头上的破口，或在辛辛监狱（Sing Sing）里伺机报仇时，第三大道以东的廉租公寓里又闹出了新的乱子。这一结果恰恰是警察镇压帮派的努力本身造成的。尽管有地方上的恩怨，帮派之间却存在着一种恶棍之间的共济会（Freemasonry）式交情，面对共同的敌人，一个遭追捕的对手随时会被接纳为完全的会友。这些帮派从炮台到哈莱姆像一条巨大的锁链般环城一周——人们给这些散落的小群体起了个总名，叫「锁链帮」（“chain gang”），因为相信它们之间的联络比一般想象的紧密得多——下东区混不下去的恶棍，只消穿城走一遭、改个名字——这事对他来说通常远比换一件衬衫容易——就能找到一处圣所，从容谋划新的暴行。他名声越臭，受到的欢迎越热烈；他要是「做过」人命，大家还会一致推他在新的地盘上领头。

===Original===
From all this it might be inferred that the New York tough is a very fierce individual, of indomitable courage and naturally as bloodthirsty as a tiger. On the contrary he is an arrant coward. His instincts of ferocity are those of the wolf rather than the tiger. It is only when he hunts with the pack that he is dangerous. Then his inordinate vanity makes him forget all fear or caution in the desire to distinguish himself before his fellows, a result of his swallowing all the flash literature and penny-dreadfuls he can beg, borrow, or steal﻿—and there is never any lack of them﻿—and of the strongly dramatic element in his nature that is nursed by such a diet into rank and morbid growth. He is a queer bundle of contradictions at all times. Drunk and foul-mouthed, ready to cut the throat of a defenceless stranger at the toss of a cent, fresh from beating his decent mother black and blue to get money for rum,20 he will resent as an intolerable insult the imputation that he is “no gentleman.” Fighting his battles with the coward’s weapons, the brass-knuckles and the deadly sandbag, or with brickbats from the housetops, he is still in all seriousness a lover of fair play, and as likely as not, when his gang has downed a policeman in a battle that has cost a dozen broken heads, to be found next saving a drowning child or woman at the peril of his own life. It depends on the angle at which he is seen, whether he is a cowardly ruffian, or a possible hero with different training and under different social conditions. Ready wit he has at all times, and there is less meanness in his makeup than in that of the bully of the London slums; but an intense love of show and applause, that carries him to any length of bravado, which his twin-brother across the sea entirely lacks. I have a very vivid recollection of seeing one of his tribe, a robber and murderer before he was nineteen, go to the gallows unmoved, all fear of the rope overcome, as it seemed, by the secret, exultant pride of being the centre of a first-class show, shortly to be followed by that acme of tenement-life bliss, a big funeral. He had his reward. His name is to this day a talisman among West Side ruffians, and is proudly borne by the gang of which, up till the night when he “knocked out his man,” he was an obscure though aspiring member.

===Chinese===
由这一切或许可以推想，纽约的恶棍是个十分凶悍的角色，勇气不可折服，天生就像老虎一样嗜血。恰恰相反，他是个十足的懦夫。他的凶残本能属于狼，而非虎。只有随狼群出猎时，他才是危险的。那时，他无度的虚荣心使他把一切恐惧和谨慎都抛在脑后，只求在同伙面前露一手——这是他吞下了所能讨到、借到、偷到的一切描写盗匪的低俗读物和便士惊险小说（penny dreadfuls）的结果——这类读物从来不愁没有——也是他天性中强烈的戏子气质被这般食粮滋养得疯长而至于病态的结果。他任何时候都是一捆奇怪的矛盾。喝得烂醉、满口污言，抛一枚铜板便可决定去割一个手无寸铁的陌生人的喉咙，前脚刚为讨朗姆酒钱把自己贤良的母亲打得遍体青紫（20），后脚就会把旁人说他「不是绅士」的指摘当作不可容忍的奇耻大辱而勃然大怒。他打仗用的是懦夫的兵器——黄铜指虎和致命的沙袋，或者从屋顶上扔砖头——却又一本正经地自命崇尚公平竞技，而且十有八九，他的帮派刚在一场砸破十几个脑袋的恶战中放倒了一名警察，转眼你就会发现他正冒着性命之险，去救一个溺水的孩子或女人。他是无赖懦夫，还是换一种训练、换一种社会境况便可能成为的英雄，全看你从哪个角度看他。急智他从来不缺，他身上的卑劣也少于伦敦贫民窟的恶霸；但他对排场与喝彩的酷爱却能把他的虚张声势推到任何地步——这是他海外的孪生兄弟全然没有的。我至今历历在目地记得，亲眼看着他的部族中的一个——未满十九岁已是劫匪和杀人犯——不动声色地走上绞架，对绞索的恐惧似乎全被一种隐秘而得意的自豪压倒了：他是一场头等大戏的中心，紧接着还有廉租公寓人生至福的极致——一场风光大出殡。他的报偿是到手了。直到今天，他的名号在西区的恶棍中间仍是一句灵验的咒，被他所属的那个帮派自豪地顶着——而在他「撂倒自己的人」的那一夜之前，他在帮里不过是个无名小卒，尽管野心勃勃。

===Original===
The crime that made McGloin famous was the cowardly murder of an unarmed saloonkeeper who came upon the gang while it was sacking his barroom at the dead of night. McGloin might easily have fled, but disdained to “run for a Dutchman.” His act was a fair measure of the standard of heroism set up by his class in its conflicts with society. The finish is worthy of the start. The first long step in crime taken by the half-grown boy, fired with ambition to earn a standing in his gang, is usually to rob a “lush,” i.e., a drunken man who has strayed his way, likely enough is lying asleep in a hallway. He has served an apprenticeship on copper-bottom wash-boilers and like articles found lying around loose, and capable of being converted into cash enough to give the growler a trip or two; but his first venture at robbery moves him up into full fellowship at once. He is no longer a “kid,” though his years may be few, but a tough with the rest. He may even in time﻿—he is reasonably certain of it﻿—get his name in the papers as a murderous scoundrel, and have his cup of glory filled to the brim. I came once upon a gang of such young rascals passing the growler after a successful raid of some sort, down at the West Thirty-seventh Street dock, and, having my camera along, offered to “take” them. They were not old and wary enough to be shy of the photographer, whose acquaintance they usually first make in handcuffs and the grip of a policeman; or their vanity overcame their caution. It is entirely in keeping with the tough’s character that he should love of all things to pose before a photographer, and the ambition is usually the stronger the more repulsive the tough. These were of that sort, and accepted the offer with great readiness, dragging into their group a disreputable-looking sheep that roamed about with them (the slaughterhouses were close at hand) as one of the band. The homeliest ruffian of the lot, who insisted on being taken with the growler to his “mug,” took the opportunity to pour what was left in it down his throat and this caused a brief unpleasantness, but otherwise the performance was a success. While I was getting the camera ready, I threw out a vague suggestion of cigarette-pictures, and it took root at once. Nothing would do then but that I must take the boldest spirits of the company “in character.” One of them tumbled over against a shed, as if asleep, while two of the others bent over him, searching his pockets with a deftness that was highly suggestive. This, they explained for my benefit, was to show how they “did the trick.” The rest of the band were so impressed with the importance of this exhibition that they insisted on crowding into the picture by climbing upon the shed, sitting on the roof with their feet dangling over the edge, and disposing themselves in every imaginable manner within view, as they thought. Lest any reader be led into the error of supposing them to have been harmless young fellows enjoying themselves in peace, let me say that within half an hour after our meeting, when I called at the police station three blocks away, I found there two of my friends of the “Montgomery Guards” under arrest for robbing a Jewish pedlar who had passed that way after I left them, and trying to saw his head off, as they put it, “just for fun. The sheeny cum along an’ the saw was there, an’ we socked it to him.” The prisoners were described to me by the police as Dennis, “the Bum,” and “Mud” Foley.

===Chinese===
使麦格洛因（McGloin）名扬一时的那桩罪行，是对一名手无寸铁的酒馆老板的怯懦谋杀——深夜最静的时辰，帮派正在洗劫他的店堂，被他撞了个正着。麦格洛因本可以轻易逃走，却不屑「为一个荷兰佬拔腿逃命」。他的行径，恰好丈量出他这一阶层在同社会较量时所立起的那种英雄标准。这结局与开头正相匹配。半大的孩子为在帮派里挣得一席之地而雄心万丈，由此在犯罪之路上迈出的第一大步，通常是抢劫一个「醉鬼」，也就是失足撞到他们手里的醉汉，多半正睡倒在某处门厅过道里。他已在铜底洗衣锅炉之类随处散置的物件上当过学徒——那些东西换成现钱，够打发打酒桶跑上一两趟了；但他头一回出手行抢，便一下子把他提升为帮中的正式会友。尽管年纪可能还小，他不再是「小毛孩」了，而是同其余人一样的恶棍。假以时日，他甚至——他自己对此也颇有把握——会作为杀人恶棍登上报纸，把他的光荣之杯斟得满满当当。有一回，我在西三十七街的码头上撞见这样一帮小无赖，打完某类得手的突袭，正传着打酒桶；我随身带着相机，便提出给他们「照」一张。他们还不够老练警觉，见了照相师不知躲闪——他们通常头一回结识这位先生，是在手铐里、在警察的铁腕钳制之下；再不然，就是虚荣压倒了戒心。恶棍最爱不过的事便是在照相师面前摆姿势，这与他的品性完全相合，而且通常是人越面目可憎，这份雄心越旺。这几个正是那一路人，一口应承下来，还把一头同他们厮混的、一脸寒酸的绵羊（屠宰场就在近旁）拖进队伍，充作帮中一员。那伙人里长相最寒碜的一个无赖，坚持要捧着打酒桶贴住他的「尊容」入镜，趁机把桶里剩下的酒倒进了喉咙，惹起一场短短的不愉快，除此之外，这场演出算是成功的。我架相机的时候，随口抛出一句香烟画片（cigarette-pictures）之类的建议，这话立刻生了根。这下非让我给这一伙里胆气最大的几位照「角色照」不可了。一个倒在一间棚屋边装睡，另外两个俯身搜他的口袋，手法之利落令人浮想联翩。这是演给我看的，他们解释说，好教我明白他们是怎样「做这一票的」。其余的人被这场演示的重大意义深深打动，非要挤进画面不可：爬上棚屋，坐在屋顶上晃荡着腿探出边缘，还各自以为想得出的每种姿势都摆进了镜头。为了不让哪位读者误以为他们不过是一伙安然自乐的无害少年，我得说明：我们会面之后不出半个钟头，我走进三个街区外的警察局，发现我那两位「蒙哥马利卫队」（Montgomery Guards）的朋友已经在押——他们在我离开之后抢劫了一个打那里经过的犹太货郎，还试图把他的脑袋锯下来，用他们自己的话说，「就为好玩。那犹太佬晃过来，锯子正巧在手边，我们就拿它招呼了他。」警察向我这样描述这两个在押的人：一个是丹尼斯，诨名「瘪三」，另一个是「泥蛋」福利（Foley）。

===Original===
It is not always that their little diversions end as harmlessly as did this, even from the standpoint of the Jew, who was pretty badly hurt. Not far from the preserves of the Montgomery Guards, in Poverty Gap, directly opposite the scene of the murder to which I have referred in a note explaining the picture of the Cunningham family, a young lad, who was the only support of his aged parents, was beaten to death within a few months by the “Alley Gang,” for the same offence that drew down the displeasure of its neighbors upon the pedlar: that of being at work trying to earn an honest living. I found a part of the gang asleep the next morning, before young Healey’s death was known, in a heap of straw on the floor of an unoccupied room in the same row of rear tenements in which the murdered boy’s home was. One of the tenants, who secretly directed me to their lair, assuring me that no worse scoundrels went unhung, ten minutes later gave the gang, to its face, an official character for sobriety and inoffensiveness that very nearly startled me into an unguarded rebuke of his duplicity. I caught his eye in time and held my peace. The man was simply trying to protect his own home, while giving such aid as he safely could toward bringing the murderous ruffians to justice. The incident shows to what extent a neighborhood may be terrorized by a determined gang of these reckless toughs.

===Chinese===
他们的这些小小消遣，并非回回都收场得像这一回这样无害——即便站在那个犹太人的立场上看，他也给伤得不轻。在离「蒙哥马利卫队」地盘不远的「贫困谷」（Poverty Gap），就在我为一幅坎宁安一家（Cunningham family）的照片作注时提到过的那桩凶案现场的正对面，一个少年——年迈父母唯一的依靠——在几个月之内被「巷帮」（Alley Gang）活活打死，罪名同招来邻里对那货郎不满的罪名如出一辙：正正经经地干活，想挣一口清白的饭吃。第二天早晨，小希利（Healey）的死讯还没有传开，我在与被害少年家同一排后院廉租公寓的一间空屋地板上，发现这帮派的一部分人正睡在一堆稻草里。一个房客悄悄把我引到他们的巢穴，向我担保天底下再没有比这伙更该绞死的恶棍；十分钟之后，他却当着帮派的面，给他们出了一张清醒本分、于人无害的官样证明，那副腔调差一点惊得我脱口斥责他的两面派。我及时接住了他的目光，忍住没作声。这人不过是想保全自己的家，同时又在不惹火烧身的限度之内，尽一份力把杀人凶徒送上法庭。这件事可以看出，一伙横下心来的亡命恶棍，能把一个街坊恐吓到什么地步。

===Original===
In Poverty Gap there were still a few decent people left. When it comes to Hell’s Kitchen, or to its compeers at the other end of Thirty-ninth Street over by the East River, and further down First Avenue in “the Village,” the Rag Gang and its allies have no need of fearing treachery in their periodical battles with the police. The entire neighborhood takes a hand on these occasions, the women in the front rank, partly from sheer love of the “fun,” but chiefly because husbands, brothers, and sweethearts are in the fight to a man and need their help. Chimney-tops form the staple of ammunition then, and stacks of loose brick and paving-stones, carefully hoarded in upper rooms as a prudent provision against emergencies. Regular patrol posts are established by the police on the housetops in times of trouble in these localities, but even then they do not escape whole-skinned, if, indeed, with their lives; neither does the gang. The policeman knows of but one cure for the tough, the club, and he lays it on without stint whenever and wherever he has the chance, knowing right well that, if caught at a disadvantage, he will get his outlay back with interest. Words are worse than wasted in the gang-districts. It is a blow at sight, and the tough thus accosted never stops to ask questions. Unless he is “wanted” for some signal outrage, the policeman rarely bothers with arresting him. He can point out half a dozen at sight against whom indictments are pending by the basketful, but whom no jail ever held many hours. They only serve to make him more reckless, for he knows that the political backing that has saved him in the past can do it again. It is a commodity that is only exchangeable “for value received,” and it is not hard to imagine what sort of value is in demand. The saloon, in ninety-nine cases out of a hundred, stands behind the bargain.

===Chinese===
「贫困谷」里还剩下几个正派人家。至于地狱厨房（Hell's Kitchen），或是第三十九街另一头、东河沿岸与它旗鼓相当的那几处，再往下的第一大道边的「村子」（the Village），「破布帮」（Rag Gang）及其盟友在同警察周期性开战时，就用不着担心有人出卖了。每逢这种场合，整个街坊都要插上一手，女人们打头阵——一半是纯粹贪这场「热闹」，主要还是因为丈夫、兄弟和情郎无一例外都在战团里，需要她们助阵。烟囱顶的砖头便是那时候的主要弹药，还有一垛垛散砖和铺路石，作为未雨绸缪的应急储备，被仔细地囤在顶层的房间里。这些地段出乱子的时节，警察会在屋顶上设起固定的巡逻哨，可即便如此，他们也未必能全身而退——能不能保住性命都难说；帮派一方同样讨不了好。警察只认得一样东西治得了恶棍：警棍；一有机会，不论何时何地，他都毫不吝惜地抡下去，并且深知一旦落到下风，他这笔支出会连本带利地收回来。在帮派地界上，讲字比不讲还要糟。见面就是一棍，挨了这一记招呼的恶棍从来不会停下来问个缘由。除非他因某桩大案正被「通缉」，警察难得费事去逮捕他。他随手就能指出六个人来，控告他们的公诉书成筐成筐地压着未决，可从没有一座监狱把他们关住过几个钟头。关押只会叫他更加无所忌惮，因为他知道，过去保过他的政治靠山，下次照样保得住。这种商品只肯「收了对价」才肯出手，不难想象市面上要的是什么样的对价。一百回里有九十九回，酒馆就站在交易的后头。

===Original===
For these reasons, as well as because he knows from frequent experience his own way to be the best, the policeman lets the gangs alone except when they come within reach of his long nightstick. They have their “club-rooms” where they meet, generally in a tenement, sometimes under a pier or a dump, to carouse, play cards, and plan their raids; their “fences,” who dispose of the stolen property. When the necessity presents itself for a descent upon the gang after some particularly flagrant outrage, the police have a task on hand that is not of the easiest. The gangs, like foxes, have more than one hole to their dens. In some localities, where the interior of a block is filled with rear tenements, often set at all sorts of odd angles, surprise alone is practicable. Pursuit through the winding ways and passages is impossible. The young thieves know them all by heart. They have their runways over roofs and fences which no one else could find. Their lair is generally selected with special reference to its possibilities of escape. Once pitched upon, its occupation by the gang, with its earmark of nightly symposiums, “can-rackets” in the slang of the street, is the signal for a rapid deterioration of the tenement, if that is possible. Relief is only to be had by ousting the intruders. An instance came under my notice in which valuable property had been well-nigh ruined by being made the thoroughfare of thieves by night and by day. They had chosen it because of a passage that led through the block by way of several connecting halls and yards. The place came soon to be known as “Murderers Alley.” Complaint was made to the Board of Health, as a last resort, of the condition of the property. The practical inspector who was sent to report upon it suggested to the owner that he build a brick-wall in a place where it would shut off communication between the streets, and he took the advice. Within the brief space of a few months the house changed character entirely, and became as decent as it had been before the convenient runway was discovered.

===Chinese===
出于这些缘故，也因为屡屡的经验告诉他，他自己那一套最灵，警察对帮派听之任之，除非他们撞进他那根长夜用警棍够得着的范围。帮派有自己的「会所」，聚会一般在一座廉租公寓里，有时在码头的桩子底下或垃圾堆下，酗酒、打牌、谋划突袭；还有自己的「赃贩」（fence），替他们销赃。在某一桩格外无法无天的暴行之后，一旦确有必要对帮派下手查抄，警察手头的差事可不算轻松。帮派和狐狸一样，一窝不止一个洞。在一些地段，街区的内部塞满了后院廉租公寓，而且往往东倒西歪地摆成各种古怪的角度，唯有出其不意一途可行。想在那九曲十八弯的过道里追赶是不可能的。年轻的窃贼对每一条都烂熟于心。他们有自己翻屋顶、越栅栏的「跑道」，旁人休想找到。他们的巢穴，一般都专门着眼于逃跑的种种可能来选定。巢穴一经选定，帮派住了进去，带着它的那枚标记——夜夜的聚饮，街面黑话叫「酒罐混战」（can-racket）——那便是这座廉租公寓急速败坏的信号，若它还有败坏的余地的话。唯一的解救办法，就是把这伙闯入者轰出去。我经手过一个实例：一处值钱的房产，因为被窃贼们昼夜当作穿堂过道，几乎给毁了。他们选中它，是因为有一条通道穿过整个街区，途经好几处相连的门厅和院子。这地方不久便得名「凶手巷」（“Murderers Alley”）。万般无奈之下，有人就这处房产的状况向卫生委员会提了申诉。奉派前来查看汇报的那位讲求实效的检查员向业主建议，在一处能截断两条街道间交通的地方砌一道砖墙，业主采纳了。短短几个月之内，这座房子彻底改换了面目，变得同那条便利的跑道被发现之前一样体面。

===Original===
This was in the Sixth Ward, where the infamous Whyo Gang until a few years ago absorbed the worst depravity of the Bend and what is left of the Five Points. The gang was finally broken up when its leader was hanged for murder after a life of uninterrupted and unavenged crimes, the recital of which made his father confessor turn pale, listening in the shadow of the scaffold, though many years of labor as chaplain of the Tombs had hardened him to such rehearsals. The great Whyo had been a “power in the ward,” handy at carrying elections for the party or faction that happened to stand in need of his services and was willing to pay for them in money or in kind. Other gangs have sprung up since with as high ambition and a fair prospect of outdoing their predecessor. The conditions that bred it still exist, practically unchanged. Inspector Byrnes is authority for the statement that throughout the city the young tough has more “ability” and “nerve” than the thief whose example he successfully emulates. He begins earlier, too. Speaking of the increase of the native element among criminal prisoners exhibited in the census returns of the last thirty years,21 the Rev. Fred. H. Wines says, “their youth is a very striking fact.” Had he confined his observations to the police courts of New York, he might have emphasized that remark and found an explanation of the discovery that “the ratio of prisoners in cities is two and one-quarter times as great as in the country at large,” a computation that takes no account of the reformatories for juvenile delinquents, or the exhibit would have been still more striking. Of the 82,200 persons arrested by the police in 1889, 10,505 were under twenty years old. The last report of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children enumerates, as “a few typical cases,” eighteen “professional cracksmen,” between nine and fifteen years old, who had been caught with burglars’ tools, or in the act of robbery. Four of them, hardly yet in long trousers, had “held up” a wayfarer in the public street and robbed him of $73. One, aged sixteen, “was the leader of a noted gang of young robbers in Forty-ninth Street. He committed murder, for which he is now serving a term of nineteen years in State’s Prison.” Four of the eighteen were girls and quite as bad as the worst. In a few years they would have been living with the toughs of their choice without the ceremony of a marriage, egging them on by their pride in their lawless achievements, and fighting side by side with them in their encounters with the “cops.”

===Chinese===
这还是在第六选区：直到几年以前，声名狼藉的怀奥帮（Whyo Gang）还把弯道区和五点区残存地带最深重的堕落一概吸纳。这个帮派最终散伙，是在它的头目因谋杀罪被处绞刑之后——那头目作恶一生，从未间断也从未受到惩罚，其罪状的宣读听得他那听忏悔的神父在绞架的阴影下面色发白，尽管在「坟墓」监狱（the Tombs）当过多年牧师的经历，早使他对这类彩排见惯不惊。这位怀奥大帮主曾是「选区里的一股势力」，惯于替恰好用得着他的效劳、又肯用现钱或实物支付的党派或派系操办选举。此后又有别的帮派拔地而起，野心同样高，而且大有胜过前辈的希望。孕育它的那些条件依然存在，几乎原封未动。伯恩斯探长是下面这句话的权威出处：全城上下，年轻的恶棍比起被他成功效仿的那个窃贼来，有更多的「本事」和「胆气」。他下手也更早。谈到过去三十年人口普查报告（21）所显示的刑事囚犯中本土成分的增长，弗雷德·H·怀恩斯牧师（Rev. Fred. H. Wines）说：「他们的年少是一个非常惊人的事实。」倘若他把观察限定在纽约的治安法庭之内，他大可以加重这句断语，并为那个发现——「城市里囚犯的比例，是广大的乡村地区的两又四分之一倍」——找到一层解释；何况这一计算还没有把少年轻犯的教养院算进去，否则这份展览还要惊人得多。1889 年被警察逮捕的 82,200 人中，有 10,505 人不满二十岁。防止虐待儿童协会的最近一份报告列举了「几宗典型案例」：十八名九岁到十五岁的「职业撬贼」，他们或是人赃并获地带着夜盗工具，或是正在行窃时被当场拿住。其中有四个刚够上穿长裤的年纪，在大街上「拦住」了一名过路人，抢走了他的 $73。还有一个十六岁的，「是第四十九街一个著名的少年劫匪帮的头目。他杀了人，现正在州监狱里服十九年徒刑」。十八人里有四个是女孩，坏起来丝毫不让最坏的男孩。再过几年，她们就会不经任何婚礼仪式，同自己选中的恶棍同居，以他们无法无天的业绩为荣，怂恿他们变本加厉，并在他们与「条子」交手时同他们并肩作战。

===Original===
The exploits of the Paradise Park Gang in the way of highway robbery showed last summer that the embers of the scattered Whyo Gang, upon the wreck of which it grew, were smouldering still. The hanging of Driscoll broke up the Whyos because they were a comparatively small band, and, with the incomparable master-spirit gone, were unable to resist the angry rush of public indignation that followed the crowning outrage. This is the history of the passing away of famous gangs from time to time. The passing is more apparent than real, however. Some other daring leader gathers the scattered elements about him soon, and the war on society is resumed. A bare enumeration of the names of the best-known gangs would occupy pages of this book. The Rock Gang, the Rag Gang, the Stable Gang, and the Short Tail Gang down about the “Hook” have all achieved bad eminence, along with scores of others that have not paraded so frequently in the newspapers. By day they loaf in the corner-groggeries on their beat, at night they plunder the stores along the avenues, or lie in wait at the river for unsteady feet straying their way. The man who is sober and minds his own business they seldom molest, unless he be a stranger inquiring his way, or a policeman and the gang twenty against the one. The tipsy wayfarer is their chosen victim, and they seldom have to look for him long. One has not far to go to the river from any point in New York. The man who does not know where he is going is sure to reach it sooner or later. Should he foolishly resist or make an outcry﻿—dead men tell no tales. “Floaters” come ashore every now and then with pockets turned inside out, not always evidence of a postmortem inspection by dock-rats. Police patrol the rivers as well as the shore on constant lookout for these, but seldom catch up with them. If overtaken after a race during which shots are often exchanged from the boats, the thieves have an easy way of escaping and at the same time destroying the evidence against them; they simply upset the boat. They swim, one and all, like real rats; the lost plunder can be recovered at leisure the next day by diving or grappling. The loss of the boat counts for little. Another is stolen, and the gang is ready for business again.

===Chinese===
乐园公园帮（Paradise Park Gang）去年夏天在拦路抢劫方面的种种业绩表明：四散的怀奥帮——乐园公园帮正是在它的残骸上坐大起来的——余烬犹自未熄。德里斯科尔（Driscoll）之被绞决打散了怀奥帮，因为它本是一伙人数较少的匪帮，那位无可匹敌的主心骨一去，便顶不住那桩登峰造极的暴行之后公愤的怒潮。名帮派不时消亡，历史大抵如此。然而这种消亡，名多于实。不多时，又有某个胆大包天的头目把四散的零星余部聚拢到自己身边，对社会的战争重新开张。单是把最出名的帮派名单开列一遍，就要占去本书好几页的篇幅。「石头帮」、「破布帮」、「马厩帮」，还有「钩子」（the Hook）一带的「短尾帮」，连同几十个不常在报纸上抛头露面的帮派，都挣下了臭名远扬。白天，他们在自己巡区内的街角小酒馆里闲混；夜里，便劫掠大道两旁的店铺，或守在河边，专等那些脚下不稳的人自己撞上来。清醒而安分守己的人，他们很少去惹，除非是个问路的外乡人，或者是警察——而帮派人多，二十个对他们一个。脚步踉跄的行人才是他们选中的猎物，而且他们很少要等太久。在纽约，从任何一点去河边都不远。不知道自己要去哪里的人，迟早准会走到那儿。他若不识好歹地反抗或喊叫——死人不会开口。「浮尸」（“Floaters”）隔三差五地漂上岸来，口袋翻在外面，那未必都是码头鼠事后搜检的证迹。警察的水上巡逻艇同岸上巡逻一样时刻提防着这些人，却很难追得上他们。即便在一场小艇之间互放排枪的追逐之后被追上，窃贼们也有的是脱身之计，还能顺手毁灭对自己不利的罪证；他们只消把船弄翻。他们个个游得像真耗子；落水的赃物，第二天可以从容地潜水或用挠钩捞回。损失一条船算不得什么。再偷一条，帮派便又开张了。

===Original===
The fiction of a social “club,” which most of the gangs keep up, helps them to a pretext for blackmailing the politicians and the storekeepers in their bailiwick at the annual seasons of their picnic, or ball. The “thieves’ ball” is as well known and recognized an institution on the East Side as the Charity Ball in a different social stratum, although it does not go by that name, in print at least. Indeed, the last thing a New York tough will admit is that he is a thief. He dignifies his calling with the pretence of gambling. He does not steal: he “wins” your money or your watch, and on the police returns he is a “speculator.” If, when he passes around the hat for “voluntary” contributions, any storekeeper should have the temerity to refuse to chip in, he may look for a visit from the gang on the first dark night, and account himself lucky if his place escapes being altogether wrecked. The Hell’s Kitchen Gang and the Rag Gang have both distinguished themselves within recent times by blowing up objectionable stores with stolen gunpowder. But if no such episode mar the celebration, the excursion comes off and is the occasion for a series of drunken fights that as likely as not end in murder. No season has passed within my memory that has not seen the police reserves called out to receive some howling pandemonium returning from a picnic grove on the Hudson or on the Sound. At least one peaceful community up the river, that had borne with this nuisance until patience had ceased to be a virtue, received a boatload of such picnickers in a style befitting the occasion and the cargo. The outraged citizens planted a howitzer on the dock, and bade the party land at their peril. With the loaded gun pointed dead at them, the furious toughs gave up and the peace was not broken on the Hudson that day, at least not ashore. It is good cause for congratulation that the worst of all forms of recreation popular among the city’s toughs, the moonlight picnic, has been effectually discouraged. Its opportunities for disgraceful revelry and immorality were unrivalled anywhere.

===Chinese===
多数帮派都维持着一个社交「俱乐部」的门面，这层虚构帮他们弄到一个由头，好在每年办野餐会或舞会的时节，敲诈自己地盘上的政客和店主。「窃贼舞会」在下东区是同慈善舞会在另一个社会阶层中一样出名、一样得到公认的一项建制，尽管至少在报面上它不用这个名字。的确，纽约的恶棍最死也不肯承认的一件事，就是他自己是个贼。他用赌博的门面给自己的行当装点体面。他不偷：他是「赢」了你的钱或你的表，而在警务统计表上，他是一名「投机商」。倘若在他传帽子收「自愿」捐款的时候，哪个店主斗胆不肯出份子，那他就等着帮派挑头一个黑夜来光顾；他的铺面若能免于被砸个稀烂，就该谢天谢地了。地狱厨房帮和破布帮近来都大出过风头：用偷来的火药炸掉碍事的铺面。但纵然没有这类插曲搅了庆典，游园会照办不误，而它正是接连不断的一场场醉战的由头，这些醉战十有八九以杀人收场。在我的记忆里，没有一个季节不叫警察预备队出动，去迎接某一批从哈德逊河畔或海峡（the Sound）畔野餐林里归来的、鬼哭狼嚎的群魔。河的上游至少有一个安分的村镇，忍这桩祸害忍到忍无可忍，结果以一种同场合与这船货色相称的排场，接待了一船那样的野餐客。被触怒的市民在码头上架起一门榴弹炮，警告来客胆敢登岸、后果自负。乌黑的炮口直指着他们，怒气冲冲的恶棍们让了步，那一天哈德逊河上总算没有开衅——至少岸上没有。值得庆幸的是，城市恶棍们最钟爱的那一项最要不得的消遣——月光野餐会——如今已被有效地劝止了。它那放浪形骸、伤风败俗的机会，天下无处能及。

===Original===
In spite of influence and protection, the tough reaches eventually the end of his rope. Occasionally﻿—not too often﻿—there is a noose on it. If not, the world that owes him a living, according to his creed, will insist on his earning it on the safe side of a prison wall. A few, a very few, have been clubbed into an approach to righteousness from the police standpoint. The condemned tough goes up to serve his “bit” or couple of “stretches,” followed by the applause of his gang. In the prison he meets older thieves than himself, and sits at their feet listening with respectful admiration to their accounts of the great doings that sent them before. He returns with the brand of the jail upon him, to encounter the hero-worship of his old associates as an offset to the cold shoulder given him by all the rest of the world. Even if he is willing to work, disgusted with the restraint and hard labor of prison life, and in a majority of cases that thought is probably uppermost in his mind, no one will have him around. If, with the assistance of Inspector Byrnes, who is a philanthropist in his own practical way, he secures a job, he is discharged on the slightest provocation, and for the most trifling fault. Very soon he sinks back into his old surroundings, to rise no more until he is lost to view in the queer, mysterious way in which thieves and fallen women disappear. No one can tell how. In the ranks of criminals he never rises above that of the “laborer,” the small thief or burglar, or general crook, who blindly does the work planned for him by others, and runs the biggest risk for the poorest pay. It cannot be said that the “growler” brought him luck, or its friendship fortune. And yet, if his misdeeds have helped to make manifest that all effort to reclaim his kind must begin with the conditions of life against which his very existence is a protest, even the tough has not lived in vain. This measure of credit at least should be accorded him, that, with or without his goodwill, he has been a factor in urging on the battle against the slums that bred him. It is a fight in which eternal vigilance is truly the price of liberty and the preservation of society.

===Chinese===
尽管有门路、有庇护，恶棍终究会走到他那根绳子的尽头。绳头上偶尔——并不常有——吊着一个绳圈。若没有，那么照他的信条欠着他一口饭吃的那个世界，就会坚持要他在监狱高墙的安全一侧挣出这口饭来。有少数几个，极少数，被警棍打到了近乎正派的地步——以警察的标准衡量。定了罪的恶棍上路去服他的「一期」刑，或两期「徒刑」，身后跟着帮派的喝彩。在监狱里，他遇见比自己更老的窃贼，坐在他们脚边，满怀敬慕地听他们讲述把他们送到这里来的那些大买卖。他回来了，身上打着监狱的烙印，迎面撞上旧日同伙对他英雄崇拜，算是对世上其余一切人给他冷眼的一点抵偿。即便他愿意做工——厌倦透了监狱生活的管束与苦役，而且在多数情形下，这个念头多半还盘踞在他心头——也没有人肯把他留在身边。倘若借着伯恩斯探长的助力——探长以他自己的务实方式行着慈善——他寻到一份差事，稍有由头就被辞退，为一点最鸡毛蒜皮的过错。不多时，他便沉回旧日的环境里，再也没能浮起来，直到他以窃贼和堕落女子失踪的那种古怪而神秘的方式，从人们的眼前失去踪影。谁也说不清那是怎么一回事。在罪犯的行当里，他永远升不到「苦力」之上——无非小偷、夜盗或打杂的骗贼，盲目地干着别人替他计划好的活计，冒最大的险，拿最少的钱。不能说「打酒桶」给他带来了好运，也不能说这场交情带来了财运。然而，倘若他的种种罪行有助于昭示这一点——一切拯救他这一类人的努力，都必须从那种生存境况入手，而他的存在本身便是对那种境况的一种抗议——那么，连恶棍也不算白活了一场。至少这一分功劳应该记在他的账上：不管他情愿与否，他终究是推动了讨伐生养他的贫民窟这一战的一个因素。在这场战斗里，永恒的警惕确确实实既是自由的代价，也是社会得以保全的代价。

## XX The Working Girls of New York / XX 纽约的打工女孩

===Original===
Of the harvest of tares, sown in iniquity and reaped in wrath, the police returns tell the story. The pen that wrote the “Song of the Shirt” is needed to tell of the sad and toil-worn lives of New York’s working-women. The cry echoes by night and by day through its tenements:

===Chinese===
这在罪孽中播下、又在忿怒中收割的稗子，其收成如何，警方的呈报自有记述。而要讲述纽约劳动妇女愁惨劳瘁的一生，还须请写出《衬衫之歌》（“Song of the Shirt”）的那支笔来。那哭声昼夜不息，回荡在这座城市的一座座廉租公寓之间：

===Original===
> Oh, God! that bread should be so dear,
 

 And flesh and blood so cheap!

===Chinese===
> 「啊，上帝！面包竟这般昂贵，
 

 而血肉之躯竟这般低贱！」

===Original===
Six months have not passed since at a great public meeting in this city, the Working Women’s Society reported: “It is a known fact that men’s wages cannot fall below a limit upon which they can exist, but woman’s wages have no limit, since the paths of shame are always open to her. It is simply impossible for any woman to live without assistance on the low salary a saleswoman earns, without depriving herself of real necessities.﻿ ﻿… It is inevitable that they must in many instances resort to evil.” It was only a few brief weeks before that verdict was uttered, that the community was shocked by the story of a gentle and refined woman who, left in direst poverty to earn her own living alone among strangers, threw herself from her attic window, preferring death to dishonor. “I would have done any honest work, even to scrubbing,” she wrote, drenched and starving, after a vain search for work in a driving storm. She had tramped the streets for weeks on her weary errand, and the only living wages that were offered her were the wages of sin. The ink was not dry upon her letter before a woman in an East Side tenement wrote down her reason for self-murder: “Weakness, sleeplessness, and yet obliged to work. My strength fails me. Sing at my coffin: ‘Where does the soul find a home and rest?’ ” Her story may be found as one of two typical “cases of despair” in one little church community, in the *City Mission Society’s Monthly* for last February. It is a story that has many parallels in the experience of every missionary, every police reporter and every family doctor whose practice is among the poor.

===Chinese===
还不出六个月，在本市一次盛大的公众集会上，劳动妇女协会（Working Women’s Society）报告说：「一个众所周知的事实是：男子的工钱不可能跌破他赖以活命的限度，女子的工钱却没有什么限度，因为羞耻之路对她永远敞开。任何女子若无人相助，单靠一个女售货员挣的那点低薪，不剥夺自己真正必需的东西，便根本活不下去。……许多情形下她们势必堕入邪路，这是不可避免的。」这一判词说出之前不过短短几个星期，全社会才刚被一桩事情震动：一位温文娴雅的女子，沦于极度的贫困，只身在陌生人中间自谋生计，从阁楼窗口纵身跃下，宁死不肯受辱。「什么正经活儿我都愿意做，哪怕是擦洗，」她写道——那时她刚在驱人的风雨里求职无果，浑身湿透，饥肠辘辘。为这桩疲惫的差事，她已一连数星期奔波街头，而人家肯付给她的唯一「活命工钱」，只有罪的工钱。她那封信墨迹未干，下东区一座廉租公寓里的另一名女子，也写下了自己寻死的缘由：「虚弱，失眠，却还不得不做工。我的气力撑不住了。在我的棺材旁唱吧：『灵魂到哪里才能找到家园和安息？』」她的故事，作为一个小教会团体里两起典型「绝望案例」之一，登在去年二月的《城市布道会月刊》（*City Mission Society’s Monthly*）上。这段故事，在每一位传教士、每一位跑警口的记者、每一位在穷人中间行医的开业医生的经历里，都有无数雷同的篇章。

===Original===
It is estimated that at least one hundred and fifty thousand women and girls earn their own living in New York; but there is reason to believe that this estimate falls far short of the truth when sufficient account is taken of the large number who are not wholly dependent upon their own labor, while contributing by it to the family’s earnings. These alone constitute a large class of the women wage-earners, and it is characteristic of the situation that the very fact that some need not starve on their wages condemns the rest to that fate. The pay they are willing to accept all have to take. What the “everlasting law of supply and demand,” that serves as such a convenient gag for public indignation, has to do with it, one learns from observation all along the road of inquiry into these real woman’s wrongs. To take the case of the saleswomen for illustration: The investigation of the Working Women’s Society disclosed the fact that wages averaging from $2 to $4.50 a week were reduced by excessive fines, the employers placing a value upon time lost that is not given to services rendered. A little girl, who received two dollars a week, made cash-sales amounting to $167 in a single day, while the receipts of a fifteen-dollar male clerk in the same department footed up only $125; yet for some trivial mistake the girl was fined sixty cents out of her two dollars. The practice prevailed in some stores of dividing the fines between the superintendent and the timekeeper at the end of the year. In one instance they amounted to $3,000, and “the superintendent was heard to charge the timekeeper with not being strict enough in his duties.” One of the causes for fine in a certain large store was sitting down. The law requiring seats for saleswomen, generally ignored, was obeyed faithfully in this establishment. The seats were there, but the girls were fined when found using them.

===Chinese===
据估计，纽约至少有十五万名妇女和女孩自食其力；然而，若把那一大批并非全然仰赖自身劳动、却以劳动贴补家庭进项的人充分计入，便有理由相信，这一估计远远不及实情。单是这些人，便构成挣工钱妇女中的一大阶层；而眼下局势的一个特征恰在于：有些人本可不因工钱而挨饿这一事实本身，便判定了其余的人去挨那饿。她们当中有人愿意领受的工钱，全体便都得领受。那条给公众的义愤充当着何等便当封口布的「永恒供求法则」，在这件事里究竟干了什么，只要沿着追查这些妇女真正冤屈的路径一路观察下来，便可学到。拿女售货员作例：劳动妇女协会的调查披露，每星期平均从 $2 到 $4.50 的工钱，还要被苛重的罚金克减——雇主给耽搁的工时定的价码，比付给实际劳务的还要高。一个每星期拿两块钱的小姑娘，单是现款销售一天便做到 $167，而同一部门里一位十五块身价的男店员，进账拢共只有 $125；可为着一点鸡毛蒜皮的差错，小姑娘两块钱里被罚掉六毛。有些商店流行一种做法：年终把罚金在管事与计时员之间均分。有一例罚金总数高达 $3,000，「还听得见那位管事数落计时员，说他职守上不够严厉」。某家大商店里，罚钱的由头之一是坐下。那条要求给女售货员备座的法律，处处遭人无视，在这家商号倒奉行得一丝不苟。座位确是摆在那儿的，只是女孩子们一被发现坐上去，就得挨罚。

===Original===
Cash-girls receiving $1.75 a week for work that at certain seasons lengthened their day to sixteen hours were sometimes required to pay for their aprons. A common cause for discharge from stores in which, on account of the oppressive heat and lack of ventilation, “girls fainted day after day and came out looking like corpses,” was too long service. No other fault was found with the discharged saleswomen than that they had been long enough in the employ of the firm to justly expect an increase of salary. The reason was even given with brutal frankness, in some instances.

===Chinese===
收银女孩们每星期拿 $1.75，干的是一到某些时节便把一天拖长到十六个钟头的活计，有时还须自己掏腰包付围裙钱。有些铺子闷热难当、通风全无，「姑娘们一天接一天地晕倒，走出来时一张脸白得像死尸」；从这类铺子里被辞退的一个寻常由头，竟是在店里年头干得太久。对被辞退的女售货员，挑不出别的错处，只怪她们在这家字号里做得够久了，久到有正当的理由指望加薪。有些情形下，这个理由甚至是以残忍的坦率当面说破的。

===Original===
These facts give a slight idea of the hardships and the poor pay of a business that notoriously absorbs child-labor. The girls are sent to the store before they have fairly entered their teens, because the money they can earn there is needed for the support of the family. If the boys will not work, if the street tempts them from home, among the girls at least there must be no drones. To keep their places they are told to lie about their age and to say that they are over fourteen. The precaution is usually superfluous. The Women’s Investigating Committee found the majority of the children employed in the stores to be under age, but heard only in a single instance of the truant officers calling. In that case they came once a year and sent the youngest children home; but in a month’s time they were all back in their places, and were not again disturbed. When it comes to the factories, where hard bodily labor is added to long hours, stifling rooms, and starvation wages, matters are even worse. The Legislature has passed laws to prevent the employment of children, as it has forbidden saloonkeepers to sell them beer, and it has provided means of enforcing its mandate, so efficient, that the very number of factories in New York is *guessed* at as in the neighborhood of twelve thousand. Up till this summer, a single inspector was charged with the duty of keeping the run of them all, and of seeing to it that the law was respected by the owners.

===Chinese===
这些事实，只能约略勾勒出那一行当的艰辛与薄酬——它鲸吞童工，早已是尽人皆知的名声。女孩子还没怎么跨进十几岁的门槛，就被送进铺子，因为她们能在那儿挣的钱，是养家糊口所必需的。男孩子若不肯做工，若被街头从家里诱了去，女孩子里头至少不许有吃闲饭的懒蜂。为保住位子，她们受教在年纪上撒谎，说自己满过十四岁了。这道防备通常纯属多余。妇女调查委员会（Women’s Investigating Committee）发现，各铺子雇用的孩子大多未到年龄，却只在单独一桩事例里听说过逃学稽查员登门。那一回，他们一年来一趟，把年纪最小的孩子打发回家；可不出一个月，孩子们全都坐回了原位，从此再没有人去搅扰。至于工厂——繁重的体力劳作又添在漫长工时、窒闷车间和饥饿工资之上——情形就更糟了。州议会通过了禁止雇用童工的法律，正如它禁止酒馆老板卖啤酒给儿童一样；它还配备了执行这道训令的手段，其效力之高，高到纽约工厂的确切数目只能*猜*个大概——约在一万二千家上下。直到今年夏天，把所有这些工厂的行踪尽数掌握、督促业主尊奉法律的差事，还全压在一名检查员身上。

===Original===
Sixty cents is put as the average day’s earnings of the 150,000, but into this computation enters the stylish “cashier’s” two dollars a day, as well as the thirty cents of the poor little girl who pulls threads in an East Side factory, and, if anything, the average is probably too high. Such as it is, however, it represents board, rent, clothing, and “pleasure” to this army of workers. Here is the case of a woman employed in the manufacturing department of a Broadway house. It stands for a hundred like her own. She averages three dollars a week. Pays $1.50 for her room; for breakfast she has a cup of coffee; lunch she cannot afford. One meal a day is her allowance. This woman is young, she is pretty. She has “the world before her.” Is it anything less than a miracle if she is guilty of nothing worse than the “early and improvident marriage,” against which moralists exclaim as one of the prolific causes of the distress of the poor? Almost any door might seem to offer welcome escape from such slavery as this. “I feel so much healthier since I got three square meals a day,” said a lodger in one of the Girls’ Homes. Two young sewing-girls came in seeking domestic service, so that they might get enough to eat. They had been only half-fed for some time, and starvation had driven them to the one door at which the pride of the American-born girl will not permit her to knock, though poverty be the price of her independence.

===Chinese===
六毛钱被算作这 150,000 人平均一天的所得；可这笔账里，既有神气的「收银员」每天两块的进项，也有东区工厂里抽线头的可怜小姑娘一天三毛的辛苦，所以只能说，这个平均数多半还偏高了。然而就是这样一笔钱，对这支劳动大军而言，便代表了饭食、房租、衣着和「消遣」。这里有一个受雇于百老汇一家商号制造部的女子的实例，它可以代表上百个同她一样的人。她平均每星期挣三块。房钱付 $1.50；早饭是一杯咖啡；午饭她吃不起。一天一顿，就是她的份例。这女子还年轻，人也生得标致。她「前程万里」。倘若她所犯下的，不过是那桩被道德家们惊呼为穷人苦难一大祸根的「过早而失于轻率的婚姻」，此外再无更坏的过失，这岂非近乎奇迹？处在这种奴役之下，几乎随便哪一扇门，看上去都像是受欢迎的逃生之路。「自从一天吃上三顿正经饭，我觉得身子好多了，」一所女子之家（Girls’ Home）的寄宿者说。两个年轻的缝纫女工进门来找帮佣的活计，为的是能吃上够数的饭。她们已有一阵子只能吃个半饱，饥饿把她们驱到了那一扇门前——土生土长的美国姑娘的自尊，原是不许她敲这扇门的，哪怕贫穷就是她为独立付出的代价。

===Original===
The tenement and the competition of public institutions and farmers’ wives and daughters, have done the tyrant shirt to death, but they have not bettered the lot of the needlewomen. The sweater of the East Side has appropriated the flannel shirt. He turns them out today at forty-five cents a dozen, paying his Jewish workers from twenty to thirty-five cents. One of these testified before the State Board of Arbitration, during the shirtmakers’ strike, that she worked eleven hours in the shop and four at home, and had never in the best of times made over six dollars a week. Another stated that she worked from 4 o’clock in the morning to 11 at night. These girls had to find their own thread and pay for their own machines out of their wages. The white shirt has gone to the public and private institutions that shelter large numbers of young girls, and to the country. There are not half as many shirtmakers in New York today as only a few years ago, and some of the largest firms have closed their city shops. The same is true of the manufacturers of underwear. One large Broadway firm has nearly all its work done by farmers’ girls in Maine, who think themselves well off if they can earn two or three dollars a week to pay for a Sunday silk, or the wedding outfit, little dreaming of the part they are playing in starving their city sisters. Literally, they sew “with double thread, a shroud as well as a shirt.” Their pin-money sets the rate of wages for thousands of poor sewing-girls in New York. The average earnings of the worker on underwear today do not exceed the three dollars which her competitor among the Eastern hills is willing to accept as the price of her play. The shirtmaker’s pay is better only because the very finest custom work is all there is left for her to do.

===Chinese===
廉租公寓，加上公立机构和农家妻女的竞争，已经结果了暴君衬衫的性命，却没有改善缝衣女们的命运。下东区的包工头把法兰绒衬衫独占了去。如今他以一打 4 毛 5 的价钱出货，付给他那些犹太工人每打 2 毛到 3 毛 5。衬衫工人罢工期间，其中一个女工在州仲裁委员会（State Board of Arbitration）作证说，她在工场里做十一个钟头，回家再做四个钟头，年景顶好的时候也从没挣到过一星期六块。另一个则说，她从清晨 4 点一直做到夜里 11 点。这些姑娘还得自备缝线，从工钱里掏钱供自己的缝纫机。白衬衫流进了收容大批年轻姑娘的公立与私立机构，也流进了乡间。今天纽约衬衫女工的数目，只剩几年前的一半不到，几家最大的字号已经关掉了城里的工场。内衣制造商的情形也是如此。百老汇一家大字号的手工活，几乎全由缅因州的农家姑娘包办——她们若能一星期挣到两三块钱，付得起一件礼拜天穿的绸衣或一套新娘行头，便自以为境况不坏了，做梦也想不到自己在饿死城里姐妹这件事上扮演的角色。千真万确，她们当真是在「一针双线，缝的既是衬衫，又是裹尸布」。她们的零花钱（pin-money），定下了纽约成千上万穷缝纫女的工钱标准。今天做内衣的女工，平均所得超不过三块钱——那正是东部山里的竞争者肯当作游戏代价而照单收下的数目。衬衫女工的工钱略好一些，只因顶精细的定制活计全留给她一个人做。

===Original===
Calico wrappers at a dollar and a half a dozen﻿—the very expert sewers able to make from eight to ten, the common run five or six﻿—neckties at from 25 to 75 cents a dozen, with a dozen as a good day’s work, are specimens of women’s wages. And yet people persist in wondering at the poor quality of work done in the tenements! Italian cheap labor has come of late also to possess this poor field, with the sweater in its train. There is scarce a branch of woman’s work outside of the home in which wages, long since at low-water mark, have not fallen to the point of actual starvation. A case was brought to my notice recently by a woman doctor, whose heart as well as her lifework is with the poor, of a widow with two little children she found at work in an East Side attic, making paper-bags.
